
# TOPIC 1.1: Development

## UNIT CU-6136-1.1.01: What development means

### BLK-6136-1.1.01-01: What development means
Development is improvement in people's quality of life across economic, social, political and environmental dimensions. Economic development concerns productive opportunities and material resources. Social development concerns conditions such as health, education, shelter and adequate food. Political development concerns people's ability to participate, exercise rights and hold decision makers accountable. Environmental development includes improving living conditions while protecting the resources on which people depend.

### BLK-6136-1.1.01-02: A project can have several effects
Consider an invented settlement where a new workshop offers paid jobs but releases dirty water into the stream used by households. More production and earnings are possible economic gains. Unsafe water is an environmental and social cost. Calling the project development merely because output rises overlooks those costs. A useful assessment asks who gains, who loses and whether the gains can last. If a treatment system removes the waste and can be maintained, jobs and cleaner water may be achieved together. If the workshop cannot afford to maintain that system, its installation alone does not settle the issue.

### BLK-6136-1.1.01-03: Link the dimensions
This distinction underlies the rest of the subject. Production in Topic 3.1 explains how goods and services are created. Health in Topic 7.2 and education in Topic 7.3 show why services matter to people's lives. When considering any new activity, connect its economic effect to at least one effect on people or their environment. A higher money income is evidence about one dimension; it is not a complete description of development.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.1.02: World patterns and differences within countries

### BLK-6136-1.1.02-01: World patterns and differences within countries
Development varies across the world and within individual countries. Broad historical patterns place many higher-income industrial economies in Europe, North America and parts of East Asia, while many lower-income economies are found in sub-Saharan Africa and parts of Asia. These are broad geographical patterns, not a current classification list or a claim about every country in a continent. High-income economies also occur outside those regions. Differences within a country can be large: an urban area may have a hospital and reliable water while a remote community has neither. A national average cannot locate those differences.

### BLK-6136-1.1.02-02: Developed, developing, LEDC and MEDC
Developed and developing describe relative levels of development in a simplified way. More developed economies commonly have higher average incomes and wider access to services, but deprivation can persist in them. Developing covers countries with very different resources and experiences; it does not mean that nothing has developed. LEDC means less economically developed country and MEDC means more economically developed country. The economic emphasis matters. A higher income grouping does not show whether rights are protected, water is safe or income is shared equally. Use a label to introduce a comparison, then support that comparison with the indicator that actually answers the question.

### BLK-6136-1.1.02-03: Industrialised and less industrialised economies
Industrialisation is growth in the importance and capacity of industrial production within an economy. Industrialised describes an economy with substantial industrial capacity; less industrialised describes relatively less. The existence of one factory does not settle the classification. Industrialisation can expand production, paid work and opportunities to learn technical skills. Its effect on living standards also depends on wages, safety, access to those jobs and environmental damage. An economy with a large service sector may still have substantial industrial capacity. Economic structure and quality of life are related questions, but neither is a complete substitute for the other.

### BLK-6136-1.1.02-04: Capitalism and communism
Capitalism emphasises private ownership of productive resources and market decisions about production and exchange. Communism as an economic ideal involves common ownership of productive resources; economies described as communist have generally relied heavily on state ownership and central planning. These descriptions concern ownership and decision making. They are not names for rich and poor. In practice, an economy may combine private shops, state services and public regulation. Establish the ownership arrangement from the evidence rather than assigning a development level from its label. Political institutions and economic arrangements also need to be examined separately.

### BLK-6136-1.1.02-05: Newly industrialised countries
A newly industrialised country, or NIC, has undergone substantial, relatively recent industrial expansion and structural change. Manufacturing and industrial employment become more important. The term is used in historical accounts of development and has no single permanent membership list. For this topic, understand the change the term describes rather than memorising a supposed up-to-date list. Expanding industry may create employment and exports while some households remain poor. To describe development, ask about the effects on income and living conditions as well as the change in production.

### BLK-6136-1.1.02-06: Reading an original comparison
In a fictional same-year comparison, Region A has income per person of N$90,000 and 95% safe-water access; Region B has N$25,000 and 60% access. Region A combines higher average income with wider water access: income is N$65,000 higher and access is 35 percentage points higher. Those two observations support a difference in both economic resources and a living-condition indicator. They do not establish the political system or prove that income caused water access. A figure describes what was measured. A relationship links relevant figures without claiming more than they establish.

### BLK-6136-1.1.02-07: Keep classifications separate
Topic 4.1 develops industrialisation and its consequences. The indicators in the next lesson give evidence for comparing development. When reading a source, first identify whether its labels concern income, industry or ownership. Then choose evidence relevant to the comparison. This avoids treating all three classifications as interchangeable.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.1.03: Indicators are evidence

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-01: Indicators are evidence
An indicator is a measurable feature used as evidence about a wider condition; one indicator seldom gives a complete account of development. An environmental measure describes environmental conditions; a social measure describes people's living conditions; a political measure concerns participation or rights; an economic measure concerns productive resources, output or income. Check the definition, date, unit and population before comparing values. A percentage and a total answer different questions. Two measures can move together without one being proven to cause the other.

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-02: Environmental evidence
Environmental indicators include the share of households using safe water, a measure of air pollution or an area of forest lost. Interpret the direction: more safe-water access is generally an improvement, while more harmful pollution is deterioration. In a fictional settlement, comparable annual records show safe-water access rising from 55% to 75% and unsafe water samples falling from 18 to 6 out of 30 tested. Access improves by 20 percentage points, and unsafe samples fall by 12. Together the observations support an improvement in water-related environmental conditions. They do not describe the settlement's air quality or identify the cause of the changes.

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-03: Social evidence
Social indicators include education, health, housing and service access. Literacy concerns the ability to read and write according to the measure used. Life expectancy is the average number of years a person at a stated age is expected to live under the mortality conditions used in the estimate; it is not a promise for an individual. Infant mortality rate records deaths before the first birthday per thousand live births for a stated period. In fictional District C, school participation rises from 70% to 85% and infant mortality falls from 40 to 25 per thousand live births. The combination supports improvement in education access and infant survival. Keep the units distinct: 15 percentage points and 15 deaths per thousand live births are not the same measure.

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-04: Political evidence
Political development concerns opportunities to participate and influence decisions, rights and accountability. Indicators may include participation in a decision process or evidence of access to it. In an invented community election with 1,000 eligible adults in each period, participation rises from 400 to 700 adults, while the number of neighbourhoods able to use an accessible meeting venue rises from two of five to five of five. Wider access accompanies higher participation. Counts from a meeting alone would not establish that rights are protected or that everyone had a free choice. Identify what the source actually measures and combine complementary observations rather than treating one statistic as a complete political judgement.

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-05: Economic evidence
Economic indicators can include income per person and employment. In fictional areas D and E, annual income per person is N$30,000 and N$45,000 respectively; employment among a consistently defined working-age population is 50% and 65%. E combines N$15,000 more average income with employment 15 percentage points higher. This supports a stronger position on those two measures. It does not show how much the lowest-income households receive or the quality of available jobs. Comparable definitions matter: changing the age range or what counts as employment can change the apparent pattern.

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-06: National totals and per-person figures
A national total is the amount for the whole country. A per-person figure divides a total by population. GNP of N$120 million shared across 6,000 people gives N$20,000 per person for that period. A learner who calls the N$20,000 figure the national total has confused the two measures. GNI per person is also an average, rather than the income received by the whole nation. The separating test is whether the national measure has been divided by population. Neither average means that each person actually receives that amount. These examples explain the total-versus-average distinction, without treating different national accounting labels as interchangeable in every dataset.

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-07: Combine dimensions without hiding a conflict
Two fictional areas each have annual income per person of N$40,000. Area F has 90% safe-water access and a 20-minute average journey to a clinic; Area G has 50% access and an 80-minute journey. The equal income averages conceal differences in environmental and social access. Area F has water access 40 percentage points higher and a clinic journey 60 minutes shorter. Those differences justify examining service access separately from income. They do not prove that Area F is better on every aspect of development, since the data says nothing about participation or income distribution.

### BLK-6136-1.1.03-08: Choose the measure for the question
Health in Topic 7.2 and education in Topic 7.3 explain mechanisms behind social indicators. Research in Topic 10.1 explains how data is collected and assessed. To interpret a source, name the measured dimension, compare relevant observations with their units, and state the relationship they support. Add a limitation when the question needs a wider judgement, rather than allowing a caution to replace the required description.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.1.04: GNP and the per-person measure

### BLK-6136-1.1.04-01: GNP and the per-person measure
GNP is the value of final output attributable to a country's residents over a period, including net income from abroad. Distinguish the national total from a per-person figure. GNP per person is GNP divided by the population; an average that does not show how income is distributed. Use the same period and compatible currency units in a comparison. A larger population can produce a larger total without having more resources per person.

### BLK-6136-1.1.04-02: Show the division and the unit
For a fictional country, annual GNP is N$240 million and population is 12,000. GNP per person = N$240,000,000 ÷ 12,000 = N$20,000 per person per year. Write out the millions before dividing, or keep a consistent power-of-ten method. This answer is an average, not the wage of a typical worker. If another fictional country has GNP of N$300 million and 30,000 residents, its average is N$10,000 per person per year despite its larger total.

### BLK-6136-1.1.04-03: Use it for an appropriate judgement
For fictional Area H, average annual income per person is N$50,000 and safe-water access 40%; J has N$30,000 and 85%. An income-only rule prioritises J. For improving water access, H’s larger access gap provides stronger evidence of need. Income and access answer different questions: compare the measure directly relevant to the decision.

### BLK-6136-1.1.04-R01: Why an income average is incomplete
GNP per person divides a national aggregate by population, so unequal distribution can leave many households with low incomes despite a high average. Unrecorded household farming and informal sales can make measured output incomplete. Money income does not show whether people can obtain safe water, education or health care. It also leaves out losses such as damaged habitats and the value of unpaid care. Price differences change what a given income buys, so international comparisons need a comparable basis. An average may help compare economic resources, but combine it with evidence on distribution, services and environmental conditions before judging living standards.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.1.05: A pattern across living conditions

### BLK-6136-1.1.05-01: A pattern across living conditions
Higher development is commonly associated with higher income per person, longer life expectancy and wider education and health access. Adequate housing includes shelter and access to essential services; diet concerns sufficient and varied food as well as the ability to buy it. Job opportunities matter through earnings, stability, safety and skills. A change from agriculture towards industrial and service employment can accompany development, but the sector name alone says little about wages or working conditions. Population growth also varies. It should be described from the source, rather than assumed from a country label.

### BLK-6136-1.1.05-02: A fictional comparison
In a same-year synthetic table, Country K has annual income per person of N$22,000, life expectancy of 61 years and 58% of households with a basic sanitation service. Country L has N$68,000, 76 years and 91%. L combines higher income with longer life expectancy and wider sanitation access. The income difference is N$46,000, life expectancy is 15 years longer and sanitation access is 33 percentage points higher. The pattern spans economic and social conditions. It does not describe income inequality within either country. Nor does it justify a stereotype about an individual resident's diet, occupation or health.

### BLK-6136-1.1.05-03: Describe people as well as production
Population in Topic 7.1, health in Topic 7.2 and education in Topic 7.3 provide explanations for these patterns. Start with what a table shows, select the relevant features, and distinguish a general association from a claim about every person. A useful comparison links the same characteristic across the two places.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.1.06: Human Development Index

### BLK-6136-1.1.06-01: Human Development Index
HDI is a composite measure combining health, education and income dimensions of human development; it does not capture every aspect of development. Composite means that several components are brought together. This helps a comparison move beyond national income alone. A higher comparable HDI indicates a higher combined level of achievement in its included dimensions. It does not mean that each household has the same standard of living or that environmental and political conditions have been fully measured.

### BLK-6136-1.1.06-02: Multidimensional Poverty Index
MPI is a measure of overlapping deprivations in health, education and living standards; it differs from an income-only poverty measure. Deprivation means a lack in a relevant condition, such as an essential service. The overlap matters: disadvantages can occur together in the same households. A lower comparable MPI indicates less multidimensional poverty. This is a different direction from HDI, where a higher value indicates higher achievement. Do not interpret a higher MPI as higher development simply because the number is larger.

### BLK-6136-1.1.06-03: Read the two indices together
In an original fictional comparison made using consistent methods, Country M has HDI 0.72 and MPI 0.08; Country N has HDI 0.55 and MPI 0.24. M combines a higher HDI with a lower MPI. The HDI difference is 0.17 and MPI difference is 0.16. Together these support higher combined human development and less multidimensional poverty in M. Do not call either difference a percentage change or assume it tells how many people lack a service. The indices summarise different information and are not subtracted from each other. They help reveal why income alone is incomplete, but a national summary still needs more detailed evidence about particular groups.

### BLK-6136-1.1.06-04: What the measures can guide
If two places have similar average incomes but different school participation and health conditions, the extra dimensions matter to a comparison of human development. If the question concerns households experiencing several disadvantages together, MPI addresses that issue more directly than an income average. Select a measure by the question it can answer. A local decision about a particular water point still needs local access and cost evidence; an index alone cannot locate the repair or establish its feasibility.

### BLK-6136-1.1.06-05: Use dimensions, not a complicated formula
Health and education are developed in Topics 7.2 and 7.3. Here, explain what the indices represent, their directions and their limits. Calculating a full HDI or MPI from component data is outside this lesson's teaching depth.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.1.07: Factors interact

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-01: Factors interact
Development reflects interacting historical, political, social, resource and economic factors. A mineral deposit may provide an opportunity, but machinery, transport, skills and public decisions influence whether it benefits people. A barrier can also persist after the event that created it. Explain a factor by tracing a mechanism: identify the condition, show how it changes an opportunity or constraint, then connect that change to people's lives. Avoid treating a continent, culture or resource as a fixed destiny.

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-02: Historical influences
Past arrangements can shape present access to land, schooling and transport. In an invented region, a railway was built to carry ore directly to a port, while nearby farming settlements were left without feeder roads. Farmers now have produce to sell but cannot afford the slow journey to the railway. The inherited transport layout limits market access, so produce may remain unsold and farm income constrained. The explanation connects a historical pattern to a present mechanism. A road investment could reduce that constraint, but only if it can be maintained and farmers have buyers. History influences opportunities; it does not prevent choices that alter them.

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-03: Political decisions and implementation
Public decisions influence where resources go and whether services function. In a fictional district, a clinic budget is approved, but money intended for repairs is diverted and the roof remains damaged. Rain then closes the treatment room. The development problem is not merely a low budget: failed use of allocated funds prevents the facility operating. Accountability and repair oversight may help convert available resources into service access. A general claim that any one political label guarantees development misses the role of specific decisions, implementation and scrutiny.

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-04: Social opportunities
Education can develop skills, and health can affect people's ability to learn and work. Access is shaped by barriers such as distance, cost or exclusion. In an invented settlement, skilled training places are available and a workshop is seeking trained workers, but afternoon sessions clash with learners' caring duties. Changing session times could allow more of those learners to attend, gain the relevant skill and apply for the available work. The job opportunities and available training are both material facts: changing the timetable would not by itself create jobs where none exist. Avoid blaming a group for barriers that the evidence identifies in its circumstances.

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-05: Resources and their use
Natural resources are opportunities with conditions. In a fictional farming area, productive soil is available but a damaged water pump prevents irrigation during a dry period. Repairing the pump can help the soil support crops if the water supply is sufficient and withdrawals remain sustainable. In a different case, mineral earnings might finance school buildings, yet benefits may be limited if earnings leave the region or pollution damages water. State how resources are used and who benefits; naming a mineral is not an explanation of development.

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-06: Economic bottlenecks
Finance, equipment, infrastructure and markets affect production and earnings. An invented sewing workshop has working machines, trained workers and confirmed orders, but lacks N$2,000 for cloth. Finance for cloth would remove the stated input shortage, allowing existing resources to meet the orders and earn revenue. Buying another machine would leave the cloth shortage unresolved. The consequence is conditional on the facts: without demand, more cloth would not establish that output could be sold. This is why a developed explanation uses the actual constraint rather than a memorised claim that any investment increases development.

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-07: Combine factors in a new case
Suppose a fictional rural processing group has a reliable water supply and trained workers, but an impassable road prevents delivery of its products. The resource and social opportunities exist, yet the economic transport barrier prevents sales. Repairing that connection could allow income to be earned if buyers remain available. This case shows interaction: strengths in one dimension do not cancel a binding constraint elsewhere. To test an explanation, ask whether removing the proposed cause would change the outcome under the supplied conditions.

### BLK-6136-1.1.07-08: Carry mechanisms into the subject
Production in Topic 3.1, industrialisation in Topic 4.1 and rural development in Topic 5.1 revisit these connections. Public choices, social access and resources should be linked to a concrete mechanism and a development consequence. A well-supported explanation is more useful than a long list of factors with no connection between them.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-014-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.03-03"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}

ANSWER: Participation rises from 40% to 70% while venue access expands to all five neighbourhoods. Wider access accompanies higher participation in this case.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: use both voter participation and venue access.", "Relationship: link wider access with higher participation without treating the source as proof of all political rights.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-015-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.03-04"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe the economic relationship in this original fictional comparison: D has annual income per person N$30,000 and employment 50%; E has N$45,000 and 65%, measured using the same working-age definition.
ANSWER: Higher employment accompanies higher income per person in E. E is 15 percentage points higher on employment and N$15,000 higher on average annual income.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: accurate paired comparisons with units.", "Relationship: associate higher employment with higher average income in these data.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-016-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.04-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why GNP per person is more useful than total GNP for comparing average economic resources in countries with different populations.
ANSWER: Dividing GNP by population accounts for how many people share the national total. A larger total may reflect more people rather than more resources per person.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: connect division by population to a meaningful average comparison.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-017-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.04-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional area should be investigated first for water support if the aim is to improve access: H has annual average income per person N$50,000 and safe-water access 40%; J has N$30,000 and 85%. Weigh an income-only rule against the supplied access evidence.
ANSWER: Investigate H first for the stated aim: only 40% have safe-water access compared with 85% in J. An income-only rule would prioritise J because its average is lower, but that would overlook H's much larger access gap. Average income per person remains useful for economic resources, yet access evidence is more directly relevant to this decision.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: a supported priority tied to improving water access.", "Reason: weigh the conflicting income and access signals, explaining why the selected evidence fits the aim. Accept a conditional alternative if it genuinely addresses the criterion and both sources.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-018-CALC

PROMPT: Calculate annual GNP per person for a fictional country with annual GNP N$240 million and population 12,000. Show your working.
ANSWER: N$240,000,000 ÷ 12,000 = N$20,000 per person per year.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-019-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.05-00"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 2}

ANSWER: Longer life expectancy and wider access to education or health services.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-020-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.05-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe the development pattern in this original fictional same-year table: K has annual income per person N$22,000, life expectancy 61 years and sanitation access 58%; L has N$68,000, 76 years and 91%.
ANSWER: L combines higher average income with longer life expectancy and wider sanitation access. For example, income is N$46,000 higher and life expectancy 15 years longer; sanitation access is also 33 percentage points higher.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-021-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.06-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why HDI and MPI add useful information to an income-only comparison of development.
ANSWER: They include health and education as well as other dimensions. HDI summarises achievement, while MPI captures overlapping deprivations, so differences hidden by similar income averages can be considered.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: explain the additional dimensions or overlapping disadvantages rather than merely expanding the abbreviations.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-022-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.06-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe the human-development pattern in this original fictional comparison using consistent methods: M has HDI 0.72 and MPI 0.08; N has HDI 0.55 and MPI 0.24.
ANSWER: M has both higher combined human development and less multidimensional poverty: its HDI is 0.17 higher and MPI 0.16 lower. The two indicators support the same broad direction despite their opposite scales of improvement.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: correctly compare both indices.", "Relationship: higher HDI and lower MPI support a consistent development pattern.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-023-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.06-01"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Define Human Development Index (HDI).
ANSWER: A composite measure combining health, education and income dimensions of human development; it does not capture every aspect of development.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: a composite measure combining health, education and income dimensions of human development.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-024-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.06-02"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}

ANSWER: A measure of overlapping deprivations in health, education and living standards; it differs from an income-only poverty measure.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: a measure of overlapping deprivations in health, education and living standards.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-025-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.07-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why having natural resources does not by itself guarantee development.
ANSWER: People also need capabilities, infrastructure and decisions that enable productive use and share the benefits. Resources may remain unused or their gains may be offset by depletion, pollution or exclusion.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: develop one link between a condition on resource use and a development outcome.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-026-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.07-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the constraint on development in this invented processing group: it has reliable water and trained workers, but an impassable road prevents delivery to confirmed buyers.
ANSWER: The transport barrier prevents the group turning its water supply and skills into sales. Restoring a usable route could allow deliveries and earnings while buyers remain available, so the economic constraint limits the gains from existing resources and capabilities.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: use the contrast between available inputs and blocked delivery.", "Consequence: connect access to sales or earnings, conditional on usable transport and demand.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-027-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.07-01"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain how history influences present farm incomes in an invented region where an old ore-to-port railway bypasses farming settlements and farmers with produce cannot afford the slow journey to it.
ANSWER: The inherited railway layout leaves farmers without an affordable market connection. Produce may remain unsold or transport costs absorb earnings, restricting farm income despite available output.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: connect the inherited layout to present transport access.", "Consequence: explain the effect on sales or income using the supplied produce and cost facts.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-028-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.07-02"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the development effect in an invented district where allocated clinic-repair money is diverted and rain closes the treatment room under its unrepaired roof.
ANSWER: Diversion prevents the funded repair being carried out. The damaged room then closes during rain, reducing treatment access even though resources had been allocated.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: diverted resources prevent implementation.", "Consequence: connect the unrepaired roof and rain to reduced health-service access.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-029-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.07-03"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain how changing training times could support development in an invented settlement where afternoon sessions clash with caring duties and a workshop has vacancies for people with the skills taught.
ANSWER: Changing the times could let affected learners attend and gain the required skill. Because relevant vacancies exist, the new access could improve their employment opportunities and potential income.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: connect the timetable barrier to access to training.", "Consequence: link skills to the stated vacancies; attendance does not guarantee a job.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-030-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.07-04"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain how repairing a pump could affect development in an invented area with productive soil, sufficient available water and crops failing because irrigation has stopped during a dry period.
ANSWER: Repair restores access to the available water, allowing the productive soil to support irrigated crops. Reduced crop losses could improve food supply or farm earnings if the pump and withdrawals can be maintained sustainably.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: connect pump repair to the supplied water and soil resources.", "Consequence: food or income gains follow reduced crop failure under the stated conditions.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-031-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.07-05"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain which economic constraint should be removed first in an invented workshop with working machines, trained workers and confirmed orders but a shortage of N$2,000 for cloth.
ANSWER: Finance for cloth removes the missing-input constraint. The workshop can then use its existing workers and machines to fulfil the orders and earn revenue; another machine would leave the cloth shortage unresolved.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: identify the binding finance/input shortage using the other available resources.", "Consequence: connect cloth to fulfilling existing orders and earning revenue.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.1.03-00", "OBJ-6136-1.1.04-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Calculate (a) annual GNP per person for fictional Country R, showing working [2]. Explain (b) whether R's larger total GNP is a sound reason to prioritise S for a project whose stated aim is to reduce the share lacking safe water, using the averages and access figures [4]. Original same-year data: R, GNP N$360 million, population 18,000, safe-water access 45%; S, GNP N$240 million, population 8,000, safe-water access 80%. The definitions and currency basis are comparable.
ANSWER: (a) N$360,000,000 ÷ 18,000 = N$20,000 per person per year. (b) No. S has N$30,000 per person per year, so R's larger total actually accompanies a lower average. Moreover, 55% in R lack safe-water access compared with 20% in S. R therefore warrants priority investigation for the stated water aim. The figures establish average resources and access gaps, not final project costs or which particular households need support.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence (a), 2 marks: accurate division and N$20,000 per person per year.", "Meaning (b), up to 4 marks: compare the two averages, use the water-access evidence and give a connected reason for the priority under the stated aim. Detailed project feasibility is not supplied; accept a conditional priority consistent with that limit.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 1.2: Sustainable Development

## UNIT CU-6136-1.2.01: Present needs and future opportunities

### BLK-6136-1.2.01-01: Present needs and future opportunities
Sustainable development is development that meets present needs without undermining future generations' ability to meet their own needs. Present needs include adequate food, water, shelter and opportunities to earn a living. Future generations also need a functioning environment and usable resources. The idea connects these needs: keeping people poor is not a satisfactory way to protect a resource, and meeting needs today by destroying tomorrow's productive base is not a lasting solution.

### BLK-6136-1.2.01-02: A water-use decision
Consider an invented settlement whose shared water source supports households and a small irrigated garden. During a dry period, unrestricted garden pumping leaves too little water for households and risks exhausting the available supply. A locally agreed allocation could reserve essential household water, limit pumping to a monitored amount and reduce avoidable losses from leaking pipes. The proposal addresses a present need without assuming that unlimited extraction can continue. Its success depends on a realistic water assessment, workable rules and maintenance. Installing a pump alone does not show that the water use is sustainable.

### BLK-6136-1.2.01-03: Ask what lasts and who benefits
A proposal should identify the need it meets, the resource or condition it depends on and how that resource will remain usable. Benefits also need to reach the people whose needs are being discussed. A project that protects water while excluding households from essential access leaves an important development problem unresolved. Explain the trade-off with a concrete mechanism rather than labelling an activity sustainable because it sounds environmentally friendly.

### BLK-6136-1.2.01-04: Connect to development and production
Topic 1.1 explains the economic, social, political and environmental dimensions. Topic 3.1 explains production and resource use. Sustainability asks whether improvements in those dimensions can continue while resources and living conditions are protected.

### BLK-6136-1.2.01-R01: Sustainable Development Goals
The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) combine economic, social and environmental pillars—livelihoods, well-being and natural systems—with ambitions for 2030. Examples are SDG 1, No Poverty; 2, Zero Hunger; 3, Good Health and Well-being; 4, Quality Education; 5, Gender Equality; 6, Clean Water and Sanitation; 8, Decent Work and Economic Growth; 12, Responsible Consumption and Production; and 13, Climate Action. Climate-related drought and flooding threaten crops, water and infrastructure, undermining livelihoods and development gains. Suitable adaptation reduces vulnerability, while reducing emissions limits further warming and protects future needs. A particular local event cannot be attributed to climate change merely because it occurred.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.2.02: Regeneration and finite stocks

### BLK-6136-1.2.02-01: Regeneration and finite stocks
A renewable resource can replenish on a human timescale under suitable conditions. A woodland can regrow, but cutting trees faster than they regenerate can still deplete it. A non-renewable resource, such as a mineral deposit, has a finite stock that is not replaced on that timescale. Reducing waste, recovering usable materials and avoiding unnecessary extraction conserve that stock. The separating test is whether the resource itself can regenerate within the relevant human timescale, not whether people can use it repeatedly.

### BLK-6136-1.2.02-02: An original woodland example
In a fictional community woodland, an estimate suggests 100 trees replace losses each year under existing conditions, but 160 are cut annually. That is a net loss of 60 trees a year if those rates persist. Because wood is renewable, controlling cutting, protecting regrowth and reducing fuel waste can help maintain it. Calling it renewable does not make the excessive extraction harmless. Regeneration depends on conditions such as surviving young trees and adequate growing conditions; a single estimate should be monitored rather than treated as a permanent guarantee.

### BLK-6136-1.2.02-03: Farming is one application
Sustainable agriculture concerns maintaining farming and its resource base, for example by protecting soil from erosion and using water carefully. Sustainable development is broader: it also concerns services, employment and other activities. To separate the concepts, ask whether the statement specifically concerns farming practices or a wider development principle. A soil-protection example illustrates sustainability; it does not make the entire concept a farming definition.

### BLK-6136-1.2.02-04: Follow the resource through use
Rural development in Topic 5.1 examines farming in more detail. Here, describe the resource, the way it is used and the effect on future availability. Conservation measures should fit whether the problem is loss of regeneration, waste of a finite stock or damage to the wider environment.

## UNIT CU-6136-1.2.03: Responsible consumption and production

### BLK-6136-1.2.03-01: Responsible consumption and production
Responsible consumption and production concern how resources are obtained, used and discarded. The development goal is to meet needs while reducing avoidable waste and damage. Producers can prevent material losses, design repairable products and manage waste. Consumers can avoid unnecessary purchases, maintain goods and choose suitable reusable alternatives. Recycling recovers materials after use; preventing waste avoids the loss in the first place. None of these labels proves that a particular option is best without examining how it works.

### BLK-6136-1.2.03-02: Compare a workable alternative
An invented school serves drinking water at events. One option uses 200 single-use cups each week. A second uses durable cups, available storage and an existing safe washing arrangement. If the durable cups are repeatedly used and maintained, they can reduce the flow of discarded cups. They also require an initial purchase, washing and replacement when damaged. The stated resources make reuse feasible in this case; where safe washing is unavailable, that constraint must be solved before recommending the same arrangement. A useful judgement weighs the relevant benefits and costs against the aim rather than listing unrelated advantages.

### BLK-6136-1.2.03-03: Prevent loss in production
A fictional tailoring group discards large usable fabric offcuts while buying new fabric for small bags. Sorting and using suitable offcuts for those bags could reduce new-material demand and waste without reducing the number of bags made. It requires suitable sizes and a workable sorting routine. The responsible choice follows the actual loss identified; a general instruction to recycle would be less precise.

### BLK-6136-1.2.03-04: Judge by evidence
Production in Topic 3.1 connects inputs to outputs. Compare resource use for the same need or output, include the practical conditions, and make a supported judgement. A reduction in waste is useful evidence, not proof that every social or environmental effect has been resolved.

### BLK-6136-1.2.03-R01: SDG 12 in everyday production
SDG 12 concerns responsible consumption and production. Reusing suitable fabric offcuts can reduce the need for new material and reduce waste. SDG 8 concerns decent work and economic growth; a viable product can connect to this goal when it supports decent paid work. Goals can interact, but a local action does not by itself establish progress against a global goal.

ANSWER: Development that meets present needs without undermining future generations' ability to meet their own needs.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-002-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.01-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a sustainable water-use response for an invented settlement where garden pumping during a dry period leaves insufficient water for essential household needs. Explain why it fits this problem.
ANSWER: Agree a monitored allocation that reserves essential household water and limits garden pumping, while repairing avoidable leaks. This addresses current needs and reduces the risk of exhausting the available supply.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: respond to competition between the garden and essential household access.", "Reason: explain how the proposed allocation or reduced waste protects both present needs and future supply; accept another feasible matched response.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-003-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.02-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a conservation measure for an invented woodland where trees are cut faster than they regrow. Explain why it fits a renewable resource.
ANSWER: Limit cutting to a monitored level compatible with regeneration and protect young trees. This gives regrowth an opportunity to replace losses rather than assuming renewability prevents depletion.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: address excessive cutting relative to regrowth.", "Reason: connect the measure to regeneration; planting without changing excessive extraction is not sufficient by itself.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-004-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.02-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the likely change in an invented woodland if 160 trees are cut but 100 replace losses annually, and those rates persist.
ANSWER: The woodland loses a net 60 trees per year. Extraction exceeds replacement, so the stock declines despite the resource being renewable and may become less able to support future use.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: link extraction above replacement to a falling stock, using both supplied rates.", "Consequence: connect net loss of 60 trees to reduced future availability.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-007-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.02-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain the error in saying: “Wood is renewable, so limiting tree cutting is unnecessary.”
ANSWER: Renewable means it can regenerate on a human timescale under suitable conditions, not that its stock cannot fall. Test whether cutting exceeds regrowth: if it does, conservation is needed to maintain the stock.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: treating renewability as immunity to depletion.", "Distinction: regeneration versus a finite non-renewable stock; use the cutting-versus-regrowth test.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-008-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.02-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain the error in defining sustainable development solely as farming that protects soil.
ANSWER: Soil-protecting farming is an example of sustainable agriculture. Sustainable development is broader and includes other activities that meet present needs while protecting future needs; test whether the statement concerns farming specifically or the wider principle.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: a farming-specific application is substituted for the broader concept.", "Distinction: sustainable agriculture versus sustainable development, with a farming-specific versus wider-principle test.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-005-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.03-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Discuss", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Discuss whether an invented school should replace 200 single-use cups a week with durable cups to reduce discarded material. It has storage and a safe washing arrangement, but must pay an initial purchase cost.
ANSWER: Durable cups are a reasonable choice for the stated waste aim because repeated use can replace a continuing flow of discarded cups, and storage and safe washing are available. The initial cost and maintenance remain drawbacks. Adoption is justified if the purchase is affordable and cups are repeatedly used rather than quickly lost or discarded.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: support a choice against the waste-reduction aim.", "Reason: weigh repeated-use benefits against the supplied initial cost and practical washing/storage conditions. A supported conditional decision is valid.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-006-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.03-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest one responsible-production response for a fictional tailoring group that discards usable fabric offcuts while buying new fabric for small bags. Explain one resource-saving benefit and name one Sustainable Development Goal directly supported by the action.
ANSWER: Sort suitable offcuts and use them for the small bags. This reduces the new fabric required and the usable fabric discarded, provided the pieces fit the required bag sizes. It directly supports SDG 12, Responsible Consumption and Production.
GUIDANCE: ["Context, 1 mark: a feasible reuse response addressing the offcuts and small bags.", "Reason, 1 mark: connect that response to reduced new-material use or avoidable waste; do not count the same benefit twice.", "Goal, 1 mark: identify SDG 12 or its title, Responsible Consumption and Production. No paid-work assumption or second goal is required.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.01-00", "OBJ-6136-1.2.02-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) two pillars of sustainable development [2]. Explain (b) why an invented woodland’s renewability does not establish that its use is sustainable when 90 trees regenerate but 130 are cut annually, with rates otherwise unchanged [4].
ANSWER: (a) Any two of the economic, social and environmental pillars. (b) Cutting 130 trees while only 90 regenerate gives a net loss of 40 trees annually. If the rates persist, the woodland stock declines despite the resource being renewable. That decline can reduce resources available for future users. Limiting extraction and protecting regeneration addresses this mechanism; the renewable label alone does not establish sustainable use.
GUIDANCE: ["Point (a), 2 marks: name any two distinct pillars from economic, social and environmental; one mark for each.", "Development (b), up to 4 marks: compare extraction with regeneration, establish the declining stock, connect it to future needs and explain a matched response or condition. A definition without application is insufficient.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-1.2-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-1.2.02-00", "OBJ-6136-1.2.03-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Describe (a) the resource-use relationship in this original fictional table for the same output of 100 bags: Method A uses 25 metres of new fabric and discards 5 metres; Method B uses 20 metres and discards 1 metre [3]. Suggest (b) which method a group should adopt if its aim is to reduce new-fabric use and waste; both methods meet the same quality standard and require the same available skills [3].
ANSWER: (a) B produces the same output using 5 metres less new fabric and discarding 4 metres less. Lower new-input use accompanies less waste. (b) Choose B for the stated aim: it reduces both new-fabric demand and discarded material without changing the required output, quality or skill availability. The conclusion is confined to the stated criteria.

Do not invent a price saving or unprovided environmental effect.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 2.1: Namibia Vision 2030

## UNIT CU-6136-2.1.01: Five connected areas

### BLK-6136-2.1.01-01: Five connected areas
Vision 2030 provides the long-term development context for this lesson. Study the five areas named here without assuming that a stated ambition has already been achieved. Education, science and technology can build knowledge and usable skills. Health and development connect people's well-being with their ability to learn, work and participate. Sustainable agriculture maintains farming while protecting soil, water and future production. Peace and social justice concern cooperation, fair treatment and access to opportunities. Gender equality means equal rights, opportunities and treatment regardless of gender; formal equality does not by itself establish equal lived outcomes.

### BLK-6136-2.1.01-02: Make the connection explicit
In an invented community, a training centre has equipment but some learners cannot attend because sessions clash with caring duties. Offering workable session times and fair access could connect education and gender equality: more people could gain usable skills. This proposal addresses the identified barrier rather than assuming that equal admission rules have removed it. In a separate fictional comparison, Centre A has 40% course completion and 35% participation among eligible women; Centre B has 75% completion and 70% participation. B combines higher completion with wider participation. That relationship helps identify an access issue to investigate; the figures do not prove which policy caused it.

### BLK-6136-2.1.01-03: Use the areas together
These areas reinforce one another when practical conditions allow it. Healthy learners can benefit from accessible education; usable skills and sustainable farming can support livelihoods. Topic 1.2 explains sustainability, Topic 7.2 health, Topic 7.3 education and Topic 6.2 gender. Here, describe each area's contribution and show a connection using the facts of a case. A programme's title is not evidence that its intended outcome occurred; look for participation, service and living-condition changes.

### BLK-6136-2.1.01-R01: Meaning and time horizon of Vision 2030
Namibia Vision 2030 is a national development framework setting a direction from the country’s position in 2004 towards improved living conditions by 2030. It connects long-term social and economic goals with development strategies. It describes an intended future; the title and end date are not evidence that the goals have been achieved.

ANSWER: Vision 2030 is Namibia’s long-term development framework, linking its 2004 starting position to improved living conditions by 2030. Education can develop skills that people use in productive work and public life.

# TOPIC 2.2: National Development Plans (NDPs)

## UNIT CU-6136-2.2.01: From national direction to action

### BLK-6136-2.2.01-01: From national direction to action
National Development Plans provide direction by connecting development priorities to actions and intended results. Planning identifies the problem, the objective and the resources available. Implementation puts decisions into practice through assigned responsibilities and a timetable. Monitoring checks what was delivered and whether it helped produce the intended outcome. Evaluation uses that evidence to judge progress and decide what to change. A plan can coordinate activities, but its existence does not guarantee that resources arrive or actions are carried out.

### BLK-6136-2.2.01-02: A worked planning sequence
In a fictional district, long journeys prevent access to skills training. The objective is to improve usable access. Planners first identify affected communities and the size of the access problem. They then choose a feasible response, allocate funds and assign responsibility for transport or local provision. During implementation they record what is delivered and who can use it. Monitoring compares attendance and completion with the starting position and the aim. If attendance remains low, the district investigates the remaining barrier and changes the action rather than simply declaring the project complete.

### BLK-6136-2.2.01-03: Output and outcome
Vehicles purchased or lessons scheduled are outputs. Improved access and completion are outcomes. A vehicle without a driver or reliable maintenance may not improve access. In a fictional programme, two vehicles are purchased as planned, but use reaches only 30% of intended learners because one route cannot operate. The purchase target is met while the access outcome remains limited. Judge the evidence at the level of the stated objective.

### BLK-6136-2.2.01-04: Keep the development purpose in view
Topic 1.1 explains why income, services and participation are different dimensions. Topic 10.1 explains evidence collection. A useful planning account connects resources, responsible action and measurable outcomes instead of listing ambitions without implementation.

## UNIT CU-6136-2.2.02: Short and long time horizons

### BLK-6136-2.2.02-01: Short and long time horizons
Short-term planning concerns nearer actions and resource decisions. Long-term planning sets a direction and intended outcomes over a longer horizon. Their distinction is relative to the context; there is no universal number of years that fits every organisation or activity. A long-term aim to improve access to skilled work can involve shorter actions such as recruiting tutors, arranging transport or repairing a training room. These actions form a route towards the wider goal. They must be relevant to the barrier identified, not merely convenient to count.

### BLK-6136-2.2.02-02: Stages should connect
Consider an invented district aiming to widen training access over several years. For the next term, it schedules a serviceable vehicle, driver and route to communities with the longest journeys. That near-term action is specific enough to implement. The longer direction is wider access and improved capabilities. Monitoring attendance and completion tests whether the transport action contributes to that direction. If missing childcare rather than distance remains the main barrier, the route may need revision or a complementary response. Changing a practical action in light of evidence can support the long-term aim rather than abandon it.

### BLK-6136-2.2.02-03: A dated national example: NDP5
Namibia's Fifth National Development Plan, NDP5, covered 2017/18–2021/22. Use it here as a historical example. Its stated goals addressed inclusive and equitable economic growth, people's capabilities and health, environmental protection and resilience, and governance through effective institutions. These goals connect economic progress with social conditions, resources and public implementation. They are intended directions; listing them does not establish what was achieved. The National Planning Commission's [NDP5 summary](https://www.npc.gov.na/national-plans/national-plans-ndp-5/) records the period and goal indicators, checked for this lesson on 20 October 2026. Its target columns must be read as targets, not observed results. NDP5 organised its priorities under four named pillars: Economic Progression, Social Transformation, Environmental Sustainability and Good Governance. Pillar labels organise the plan; goals describe the intended changes within it.

### BLK-6136-2.2.02-04: How to evaluate the example
Begin with one stated goal, then identify evidence that would show progress towards it. For a human-capabilities goal, facility construction is relevant implementation evidence, but service access, quality or usable skills are closer to the intended outcome. Compare a baseline and later observation measured consistently. Ask whether progress reaches the relevant groups and whether the supporting resources can be maintained. A positive change may have several causes, so avoid attributing the whole change to a plan without evidence. Equally, an unmet target is not evidence that every action had no benefit. An evaluation should distinguish progress, remaining gaps and the limits of the evidence.

### BLK-6136-2.2.02-05: An original evaluation case
The following is fictional practice and is not a claim about NDP5 performance. A programme aims to increase training access in remote settlements. It meets a target of opening four centres. However, only one has regular tutors and attendance remains unchanged. Judged by buildings, implementation appears successful; judged by usable training access, the result is limited. Assigning tutors and monitoring actual attendance would address the gap more directly than opening a fifth centre. The judgement follows the stated access criterion and uses both construction and service evidence. It is stronger than declaring the whole programme successful because one target was met.

### BLK-6136-2.2.02-06: Keep policy examples dated
Vision 2030 in Topic 2.1 gives the longer context; health and education in Topics 7.2 and 7.3 explain some intended benefits. When evaluating NDP5, name its period and goals, then separate real source evidence from an illustrative scenario. This lesson does not claim a final national verdict or describe NDP5 as the current programme.

### BLK-6136-2.2.02-R01: Harambee Prosperity Plan: a dated example
The first Harambee Prosperity Plan (HPP) was introduced in 2016 under President Hage Geingob. It sought faster progress against poverty and large inequalities in wealth, supporting the longer national development direction. Its five pillars joined effective governance and service delivery, economic advancement, social progression, infrastructure development, and international relations and cooperation. Governance concerns accountable decisions and useful services. Economic advancement concerns productive opportunities and employment. Social progression includes food security, housing, sanitation and skills. Infrastructure supplies networks such as water, electricity and transport. International cooperation links national action with regional and wider partners. These are the plan’s intended contributions, not five proven achievements. Keep this 2016 example distinct from later plans.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-2.2-006-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-2.2.02-02"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
