# TOPIC 4.1: Industrialisation

## UNIT CU-6136-4.1.01: The process of industrialisation

### BLK-6136-4.1.01-01: The process of industrialisation
Industrialisation is growth in the importance and capacity of industrial production within an economy. It can involve investment in equipment, skills, supporting services and organised production; building one factory does not establish an economy-wide structural change. Industrial production transforms materials into goods, such as food processing or textile manufacture. Greater productive capacity can provide jobs, goods and market opportunities. The development result depends on who can participate, conditions of work and environmental effects. Capacity, actual output and the distribution of benefits are related but different measures.

### BLK-6136-4.1.01-02: An expansion with conditions
In an invented district, processing equipment and training allow more local raw material to be made into finished goods. Capacity and industrial activity increase, which illustrates industrialisation. Whether households benefit also depends on wages, access to work and resource effects. If untreated waste damages water, the output gain coexists with a development cost. Topic 3.1 explains the inputs; Topic 1.1 supplies the wider development dimensions. Use the process definition and a mechanism rather than calling any new shop an industrial transformation.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.1.02: Modern production is coordinated

### BLK-6136-4.1.02-01: Modern production is coordinated
Modern industrial production can combine machinery, varied inputs and specialised tasks that need coordination. Supporting infrastructure enables production, but naming land, labour and capital is not the same as describing the networks and services connecting activities. Infrastructure includes transport, electricity, water and communication networks. The factors-of-production classification concerns inputs; infrastructure concerns supporting systems. In that input classification, money capital finances resources and capital goods are produced assets used in production, as Topic 3.1 explains. Categories can overlap: a road is a produced asset, yet is discussed as transport infrastructure when its network role matters. Test what the question asks rather than treating the classifications as mutually exclusive lists.

### BLK-6136-4.1.02-02: Machinery and advanced technology
Complex machinery and advanced technology can provide control, precision or productive capacity beyond simpler methods. Their productive value depends on suitable tasks, skilled operation, reliable inputs and maintenance. A computer-controlled cutter in an invented garment factory can repeat specified shapes accurately. If no technician can repair a fault, that potential capacity may stand idle. Identify the feature from its productive role, not just the fact that a device looks modern.

### BLK-6136-4.1.02-03: Several raw materials
An industrial product can require several raw materials or components whose quantities and quality must fit the process. A shortage of one essential input can interrupt completion even when the other materials are available. A fictional garment producer combines fabric, thread, fasteners and labels. Buying more fabric will not complete an order that lacks suitable fasteners. A wide range of materials means coordination of supply and specifications, not an assertion that every industry requires the same number of inputs.

### BLK-6136-4.1.02-04: Specialised industrial tasks
Complex division of labour assigns different specialised tasks or stages to workers and teams. Specialist skills can improve task performance while creating dependence between stages. Designers, cutters, machine operators, quality checkers and dispatch workers perform different roles in a garment process. Naming the total workforce does not show the division. Explain what each relevant stage contributes and how an error in one stage affects the next.

### BLK-6136-4.1.02-05: Coordinate the stages
Coordination of specialised tasks arranges the timing, flow and information linking production stages. An unbalanced flow can create queues or idle capacity even when each stage has capable workers. If cutting produces 100 sets a day while stitching completes 60, unfinished sets accumulate unless the imbalance is addressed. Changing schedules, addressing the slow stage or matching output between stages is more useful than simply demanding that cutters work faster.

### BLK-6136-4.1.02-06: An original production comparison
In a fictional factory, cutting capacity is 100 sets daily and stitching completes 60. Completed output is 60 and the unfinished queue grows by 40 sets a day. After a suitable stitching improvement, cutting remains 100 and stitching completes 90, so the queue grows by 10. The smaller imbalance accompanies higher finished output. Faster cutting alone would increase the backlog. Topic 3.1 explains specialised tasks; this example links their flow and coordination to useful industrial output.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.1.03: Strategies answer different questions

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-01: Strategies answer different questions
Import substitution develops domestic production to replace imports, while export-orientated production is directed primarily towards foreign markets. Strategy, scale and ownership are different dimensions; a locally owned factory can be export-orientated or replace imports. Exports are sales abroad; export-orientated industrialisation is a strategy for building productive capacity around foreign demand. One export sale does not demonstrate that strategy. Compare alternatives against a stated aim using demand, resources, feasibility and who benefits. An ownership label is not evidence of efficiency.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-02: Import substitution
Import substitution means developing domestic production to replace goods previously imported. It may build local productive capacity and reduce dependence on some imports, but needs viable demand, suitable inputs and the ability to produce acceptable goods. In a fictional case, local producers can meet a steady demand for school bags with available skills and material supply. Replacing imported bags may support work and learning. If the domestic process is very costly or still depends on imported inputs, the benefit is narrower than a claim of complete self-sufficiency. Protecting production without improvement may leave users paying more for poor quality.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-03: Export-orientated industry
Export-orientated production means producing goods or services primarily for sale in foreign markets. Access to a larger market can support expansion, but producers must meet delivery, quality and cost requirements and face changes in overseas demand. Confirmed foreign orders can make a larger output worthwhile when transport and inputs are reliable. A producer without a feasible delivery route may fail those orders. Foreign sales can bring earnings, yet dependence on one buyer or product can make local work vulnerable to external changes. Judge the supply and demand conditions rather than assuming a larger market guarantees success.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-04: Large and small scale
Large-scale production can spread some fixed costs and use specialised equipment, while small-scale production may fit limited markets and flexible local tasks. The suitable scale depends on demand, finance, coordination and local resources; greater size alone does not establish lower average cost or wider benefits. A large standardised order may justify equipment that would be underused by a small repair business. A small workshop may adapt products more easily but lack finance for expensive machinery. Compare the same output need, include spare capacity and ask whether a claimed economy of scale actually lowers cost per useful unit.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-05: Multinational investment
Multinational companies operate across countries and may bring finance, technology or links to wider markets. Local gains depend on jobs, training, purchasing, taxation and environmental practice; profits and important decisions may also leave the host economy. An invented investor offering equipment and training can help remove a capacity constraint. If local suppliers are excluded and no skills are transferred, the local link is weaker. Evaluate enforceable arrangements and actual retained benefits. Do not promise that a company will train, hire or buy locally merely because these outcomes are possible.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-06: Local investors and joint enterprises
Local investors organise finance and decisions from within the economy; a joint enterprise combines participation from partners, which may include local and foreign investors. Partners can combine local knowledge with finance or technology, but need workable agreements about decisions, contributions and benefits. Local ownership can support retained earnings and responsiveness to users, but does not remove finance or skill shortages. A joint venture might bridge those gaps, while exposing disagreement over control. Compare a feasible partnership with what the local producer could achieve alone; nationality is not a substitute for evidence about performance.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-07: State enterprises
State enterprises organise production under state ownership and can pursue strategic services or access goals. They need finance, capable management, maintenance and accountability; public ownership alone does not establish efficient or equitable delivery. A fictional state provider may extend a service to sparsely served communities where commercial returns are limited. It must still sustain the operation and use resources well. Compare reliable access, cost and implementation with a feasible alternative, rather than treating either state or private ownership as an automatic answer.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-08: Public private partnership
A public private partnership is an arrangement in which public and private participants share specified responsibilities for providing infrastructure or a service. It can combine capabilities and resources, but its costs, risks, access requirements and accountability depend on the agreement and its implementation. Public ownership may be retained while a private partner finances or operates a specified facility. That differs from a simple transfer of ownership. A contract that leaves costly failures with the public while excluding low-income users may serve the development aim poorly. Assess responsibilities and realistic enforcement rather than the partnership label.

### BLK-6136-4.1.03-09: Choose a strategy against the same aim
Fictional District A wants reliable local employment. A modest domestic market has steady demand and producers can match imported product quality with available skills. A small import-substitution operation is plausible. A much larger export plant would require finance and a reliable shipping route that the district lacks. Under these facts, the smaller operation is better supported, although its potential market is limited. If confirmed foreign demand and a workable route become available, the comparison changes. Strategies can be combined over time: an enterprise may first learn to serve local users and later supply foreign buyers. That is a possible route, not a promise that each stage will occur. For ownership choices, compare who supplies finance, who decides, who bears risk and who retains benefits. The same questions distinguish private investment, state activity and partnership arrangements. Topic 3.1 supplies enterprise and economies of scale; Topic 9.1 develops trade.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.1.04: NIC examples and the state

### BLK-6136-4.1.04-01: NIC examples and the state
The syllabus names South Africa, India, the Philippines and Indonesia as illustrative newly industrialising countries; these are syllabus-context examples rather than a current official classification list. State provision of infrastructure, skills or enabling arrangements can support industrial capacity, but the effects depend on relevance, implementation and opportunity cost. A state can help address a missing road connection or skill shortage. It can also spend on facilities that remain unused. To evaluate its role, trace the constraint, the action and the evidence of productive or social benefit. The fictional examples here model that reasoning and are not claims that a named country used a particular policy or achieved a stated result.

### BLK-6136-4.1.04-02: Evaluate an illustrative state action
In a fictional industrial area, firms face delivery delays and a shortage of technical skills. A freight upgrade offers earlier gains for existing firms; a training centre develops future capability but takes longer. Where delays threaten current orders, the route may merit priority. Where deliveries remain manageable but skills block expansion, training may be stronger. Compare urgency, reach, cost and longer-term benefits. This is an illustrative decision, not a claimed policy result for a named country.

### BLK-6136-4.1.04-R01: State roles in industrial development
The syllabus’s NIC examples include South Africa and India. South Africa’s public transport and electricity networks illustrate shared infrastructure used in industrial production; India’s public technical education and research illustrate support for productive skills and knowledge. Neither example proves that a single policy caused growth or that services are currently reliable. More generally a state can coordinate investment, improve export links or support access to finance. These measures have opportunity costs and can fail through poor selection or weak accountability. Test actual use, access and productive benefit rather than the spending total.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.1.05: Industries and development in Namibia

### BLK-6136-4.1.05-01: Industries and development in Namibia
Mining, fishing, agriculture, textiles and tourism illustrate different routes through which production can contribute to Namibia’s economic and social development. Employment, local purchases, processing and public revenue are potential benefits; resource depletion, pollution, insecure work or benefits leaving the area can limit gains. Mining can supply raw materials and earnings, fishing can link catches with processing, agriculture can support food and livelihoods, textiles can create manufactured goods, and tourism can create service demand. These are mechanisms, not current contribution statistics. Tourism increases local income when local workers, owners or suppliers participate and retain earnings. A site attracting visitors alone does not show that local households benefit.

### BLK-6136-4.1.05-02: Trace who receives the gain
In a fictional coastal processing activity, a group buys local catches and hires local workers for additional processing, with suitable demand and maintained resource limits. Local wages and supplier earnings can rise because households participate in the added activity. If the same raw fish were sent away with no local processing, those particular processing gains would occur elsewhere. Yet more extraction is not automatically sustainable: catch management and the condition of the stock still matter. This is a conceptual illustration, not a claim about any real Namibian company or present fishery. Topic 1.2 explains resource sustainability; Topic 5.1 explores rural livelihoods. Connect an industry to employment, income, skills or services and also identify the condition that makes the benefit plausible.

### BLK-6136-4.1.05-R01: Namibian industries and links to livelihoods
Namibian examples include mineral extraction and processing, fish processing, meat processing and tourism-related services. A fish-processing activity can add local handling, packaging and transport work to landed fish, provided stocks and quality are protected. Meat processing can connect livestock producers with slaughtering, storage and marketing. Mineral processing can retain a production stage locally but needs power, skills and buyers; mining income alone does not establish benefits for every household. Tourism creates service demand where local guides, accommodation and suppliers participate. These examples identify established activities, not current employment totals or a ranking of their size.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.1.06: Sustainable production improvements

### BLK-6136-4.1.06-01: Sustainable production improvements
Possible production improvements include value-added processes, investment partnerships, suitable modern processes and research into resources or methods. A sustainable improvement addresses a real productive constraint while considering resources, costs, capabilities, access and environmental effects. If suitable raw material is wasted because storage fails, better handling may increase useful output more directly than expanding extraction. If an order requires a new quality standard, a controlled process and skills may be needed. Select the improvement from the evidence instead of prescribing every strategy in every setting.

### BLK-6136-4.1.06-02: Value-added processes
Value-added processes transform or improve an input into a product with additional economic value. Local processing can retain additional activity and skills where demand, viable costs and suitable capacity exist; a higher selling price alone does not establish a higher net gain. An invented group may process suitable fruit into a stable product rather than lose it before sale. Processing needs equipment, quality control, packaging and buyers. Compare the additional value with the additional costs and losses avoided; do not count the whole sale price as the extra value created.

### BLK-6136-4.1.06-03: Foreign and local joint ventures
A joint venture combines partners’ contributions to a specified enterprise or activity, potentially linking foreign finance or technology with local knowledge and participation. Benefits require compatible aims and workable terms for control, skills and retained returns. An invented local processor with buyers but insufficient equipment finance might gain capacity through a partner. Without clear contributions, training and decision rules, the arrangement can create dependence or conflict. A partnership should address the documented gap and protect feasible local participation rather than merely add a foreign name.

### BLK-6136-4.1.06-04: Modern production processes
Modern processes can improve control, efficiency or quality where they suit the task and supporting resources. Reducing defects or avoidable losses can increase useful output without a proportional rise in raw-material extraction. A fictional food processor with temperature-related spoilage might improve monitoring and control. The system needs staff able to use and maintain it. A sophisticated device that is not used correctly may add expense without reducing losses. Improvement is shown by comparable usable output and quality, not simply by equipment purchased.

### BLK-6136-4.1.06-05: Research and exploration
Research gathers and tests information about products, processes or conditions; exploration investigates the location and characteristics of resources. Better evidence can reduce uncertainty and guide feasible decisions, but does not guarantee a commercially or environmentally viable discovery. Testing whether a local material meets a quality requirement can prevent an unsuitable investment. Discovering a deposit does not establish access, recoverability, an affordable productive method or acceptable impacts. Describe what uncertainty the enquiry addresses and how the result would change the decision.

### BLK-6136-4.1.06-06: Increase usable output before extracting more
In an invented processor, 100 units of input yield 70 saleable units and 30 spoiled units. After a suitable handling change, the same input yields 90 saleable and 10 spoiled units. Useful output rises by 20 while spoilage falls by 20; more extraction is not required for this gain. The change needs working equipment, appropriate staff skills and buyers for the usable product. An unexplained before-and-after pattern alone cannot isolate the cause, so a real evaluation would check whether relevant conditions changed. Topic 3.1 explains production and appropriate technology; Topic 1.2 asks whether improvements can last. The evidence here supports investigating avoidable losses rather than immediately seeking a larger raw-material supply.

ANSWER: Industrialisation is growth in the importance and capacity of industrial production within an economy.

{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.01-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-004-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.02-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe the production relationship in this fictional factory: cutting stays at 100 sets a day; stitching and completed output rise from 60 to 90 sets; the daily unfinished queue growth falls from 40 to 10 sets.
ANSWER: Reducing the imbalance between stages accompanies higher completed output. Stitching rises by 30 sets, while queue growth falls by 30, with cutting unchanged.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-029-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.02-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why naming land, labour and capital does not by itself answer a question about industrial infrastructure.
ANSWER: Those are factor-of-production categories, whereas infrastructure refers to supporting networks and services such as roads, electricity and communications. Test whether the question asks about inputs or their supporting connections; a produced road can have both a capital-asset role and an infrastructure role.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: the input classification substitutes for the requested network/service description.", "Distinction: supporting systems versus factor categories, without claiming there can be no overlap.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.02-01"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Identify", "mark_tariff": 1}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-009-DIST
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-00"], "subtype": "DIST", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Differentiate", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Differentiate between import substitution and export-orientated industrialisation.
ANSWER: Import substitution develops domestic production to replace imports; export-orientated industrialisation develops production primarily for foreign markets.
GUIDANCE: ["Comparison: pair the domestic replacement versus foreign-market direction.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-010-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which industrial strategy better fits stable local employment in this fictional district. A small workshop serves steady local orders and can open immediately, but offers 12 jobs and little room to expand. A funded export plant offers 40 jobs and has a confirmed delivery route, but depends on one foreign buyer. Both have trained staff. Weigh the options.
ANSWER: The workshop offers fewer jobs but a steadier local demand base and immediate work. The export plant offers substantially more employment and feasible delivery, but losing its single buyer would put more jobs at risk. For stability, the workshop is safer initially; the plant becomes stronger if it diversifies buyers or secures durable orders. The decision turns on resilience as well as the number of jobs, rather than assuming either local or export sales are sufficient.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-028-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain the error in calling one occasional sale of surplus goods abroad proof of export-orientated industrialisation.
ANSWER: An export is a sale abroad; export-orientated industrialisation is a strategy developing productive capacity primarily for foreign markets. Test whether the evidence shows a planned production direction rather than a single transaction.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: one foreign sale substitutes for an industrial strategy.", "Distinction: transaction versus productive-market strategy, with the direction-of-production test.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-011-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-01"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether import substitution suits this fictional case: a local producer can match imported school-bag quality, has reliable inputs and steady local orders, but its unit cost is slightly higher. The aim is to build local capacity without making bags unaffordable.
ANSWER: It is plausible because inputs, quality and demand support productive learning and local work. The higher cost could undermine affordability, so suitability depends on keeping the price within users’ means or reducing costs as capacity develops. Domestic production alone does not settle the combined aim.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: supported conditional suitability against capacity and affordability.", "Reason: weigh the demand/input strengths with the higher cost; an unconditional claim of self-sufficiency is insufficient.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-012-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-02"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a fictional producer should expand for confirmed foreign orders. It has suitable quality, finance and delivery capacity. Expansion would raise sales but put 70% of output with one buyer and require overtime for six months. A smaller expansion earns less but retains several buyers. The aim is dependable earnings.
ANSWER: Expansion has a credible revenue opportunity because orders, capacity and delivery are available. However, concentration on one buyer exposes earnings to a cancelled contract, and sustained overtime may add costs or strain. A smaller expansion preserves diversification but forgoes some income. For dependable earnings, stage the increase while negotiating durable orders and manageable workloads; full expansion is more defensible if those risks are controlled.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-013-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-03"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which scale suits an invented repair group. A small workshop can adapt to varied orders with low borrowing but has higher unit costs. A funded automated line has reliable operators and can reduce unit costs for confirmed standard orders, but commits most capacity to them. The group values flexibility and dependable income.
ANSWER: The workshop preserves flexibility and limits repayment exposure, useful when orders vary. The line has a genuine cost advantage on its confirmed standard work, but commits capacity and finance that cannot easily move to other tasks. A workshop is preferable if varied orders provide the more stable income; a line becomes attractive when standard demand is durable enough to cover its costs. Compare the actual mix of work before choosing scale.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-014-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-04"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a fictional multinational proposal meets a district’s aim of local skill development: it finances equipment and 80 jobs, but offers no training and imports its specialist team. A smaller alternative funds 40 jobs with documented local training.
ANSWER: The smaller alternative is more directly aligned with the skill aim because it includes a training route. The multinational offers more jobs and finance, but its specialist arrangement supplies no evidence of local skill transfer. It may fit better if credible training commitments are added; job numbers alone do not establish the stated outcome.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: relate the choice to skills rather than jobs alone.", "Reason: weigh the larger employment offer against actual training provision and retained capability.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-015-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-05"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether an invented local processor should use a joint venture. It has buyers and local knowledge but insufficient equipment finance; a partner offers finance and training with clear shared-control terms. The alternative is a smaller operation it can fund alone. The aim is increased output with local participation.
ANSWER: The joint venture is supported if the agreed terms can be implemented: it addresses missing finance while retaining local involvement and adding training. Independent operation preserves sole control but limits feasible capacity. The terms, rather than the partner’s nationality alone, make the proposed gain credible.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-016-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-06"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which option better fits affordable service to sparsely served communities: a fictional state enterprise has funded maintenance and an access obligation; a private alternative promises faster expansion but has no provision for low-income users.
ANSWER: The state option better matches affordable access under the supplied arrangements. The private proposal’s faster expansion is a benefit, but it does not address the specified users unless workable access protection is added. State ownership itself is not the reason: funded maintenance and the access arrangement are decisive.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-017-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-07"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a fictional public private partnership is preferable to direct public provision for a processing site. It provides equipment finance sooner but its contract requires payment to the private partner even if equipment fails; direct provision is slower but has funded maintenance. The aim is dependable useful capacity within a limited public budget.
ANSWER: The partnership’s speed is attractive but its failure-payment term exposes the limited public budget without assuring useful capacity. Compare the actual risk and delivery terms rather than assuming a partnership removes public costs.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-019-EVAL

A funded technical-training centre would address a documented skills shortage for future production but take longer to benefit firms. Funds cover one first.
ANSWER: The freight upgrade can improve current deliveries and earnings across existing firms. Training offers longer-term productive capability and access to skilled jobs, but brings less immediate relief. ### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.02-00", "OBJ-6136-4.1.02-04"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) what is meant by division of labour and coordination of tasks [2]. Explain (b) the productive priority in an invented factory where cutting completes 120 sets daily but stitching completes 70 and the unfinished queue grows daily. Buyers want completed items and extra cutting capacity is proposed [4].
ANSWER: (a) Division of labour allocates specialised tasks to different workers; coordination links the timing and flow between them. (b) Stitching is the constrained stage: 50 more sets are cut than stitched daily. Extra cutting would increase unfinished work rather than completed items. Improving or matching flow to stitching capacity addresses useful output for the buyers more directly.
GUIDANCE: ["Point (a), 2 marks: correct meanings for task allocation and linking task flow.", "Development (b), up to 4 marks: use the stage imbalance and buyer requirement to explain the bottleneck and why extra cutting is weaker.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.1.03-00", "OBJ-6136-4.1.06-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Describe (a) the relationship in this original fictional comparison, with the same raw input and quality standard: Process A uses 100 input units, gives 60 saleable units and 40 spoiled; B uses 100, gives 85 saleable and 15 spoiled [3]. Suggest (b) which process an industrial group should adopt to increase useful output without more extraction; staff and maintenance for either are available [3].
ANSWER: (a) B combines 25 more saleable units with 25 fewer spoiled units from the same input. Lower loss accompanies greater useful output. (b) B fits the aim because it improves conversion of existing material while avoiding additional extraction. The stated skills and maintenance make adoption feasible under these facts; no unprovided price saving is assumed.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence (a), up to 3 marks: pair the output and spoilage differences for unchanged input.", "Meaning (b), up to 3 marks: choose against useful output/resource use and justify with data and support conditions.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 4.2: Urbanisation

## UNIT CU-6136-4.2.01: Urbanisation is a rising share

### BLK-6136-4.2.01-01: Urbanisation is a rising share
Urbanisation is an increase in the proportion of a population living in urban areas; distinguish it from an increase in urban population alone. Urban share is urban population divided by total population, multiplied by 100; a larger urban number does not prove a larger share. Use consistent urban definitions and a stated area and period. In a fictional country, 400,000 of 1,000,000 people are urban: 40%. Later, 600,000 of 2,000,000 are urban: 30%. The urban number rises by 200,000, yet its share falls by 10 percentage points. These figures therefore do not show urbanisation over that interval. A percentage-point change compares the shares directly; it is different from percentage growth in the urban count.

### BLK-6136-4.2.01-02: Calculate the share before naming the trend
For a separate fictional calculation, 360,000 urban residents among 900,000 total gives 360,000 ÷ 900,000 = 0.4. Multiplying by 100 gives 40%. For a trend, compare at least two periods: if the next comparable share is 45%, the increase is five percentage points. Topic 7.1 explains population measures. Urbanisation concerns the changing urban share, urban growth the urban number; check which the prompt asks for.

### BLK-6136-4.2.01-03: Connect causes and responses
Use the denominator and a comparable definition before describing urbanisation. Population in Topic 7.1 supplies related measures.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.2.02: Causes and characteristics

### BLK-6136-4.2.02-01: Causes and characteristics
Rural–urban migration, different rates of natural population change and changes in settlement classification can affect the urban share. People may move in response to rural constraints and perceived urban opportunities, but migration and urbanisation are not interchangeable concepts. A push factor encourages departure, such as loss of a livelihood; a pull factor attracts people, such as access to work or education. A perceived opportunity may not become an actual job. Births and deaths in urban and rural populations also affect their relative sizes. Changing a boundary can alter measured shares without people physically moving, so compare definitions before drawing conclusions.

### BLK-6136-4.2.02-02: Industrialised-country patterns
Many industrialised economies experienced substantial earlier rural–urban movement associated with industrial employment and service concentration. Where a large share is already urban, further growth, suburban movement or movement away from city centres can follow different patterns. These are broad historical tendencies, not current country rankings or rules for every resident. Movement from one urban area to another can change a city’s population without changing the national urban share. A crowded city alone does not show how quickly a country is urbanising. Use the supplied dates and denominators.

### BLK-6136-4.2.02-03: Developing-country patterns
Some developing economies experience rapid urban growth where migration and natural increase combine with a growing urban share. If housing, services and work opportunities expand more slowly than needs, existing access gaps can widen. The rate and form vary between countries and settlements. Urban residents can benefit from concentrated markets, schools and services; rapid growth creates pressure when provision cannot keep pace. Avoid describing urban growth as an automatic cause of poverty or assuming that all residents have the same experience.

### BLK-6136-4.2.02-04: Read a pattern rather than assume one
In an original fictional comparison, Area A’s urban share rises from 30% to 45%, with an annual net rural–urban inflow of 5,000; Area B’s share rises from 70% to 72%, with an inflow of 500. Both urbanise, but A has the larger percentage-point rise and supplied inflow. These observations support a contrast, not proof that migration caused the whole change. The periods and definitions must match. Topic 7.1 develops natural change and migration; do not confuse a higher starting level with faster urbanisation.

### BLK-6136-4.2.02-05: Connect causes and responses
Industrialisation in Topic 4.1 explains some work opportunities. Rural development in Topic 5.1 explains opportunities at origins. Neither turns all migration into one simple cause.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.2.03: When needs outpace provision

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-01: When needs outpace provision
Rapid urban growth can place pressure on housing, water, sanitation, transport and employment where provision does not expand with needs. Problems depend on service capacity, location and access as well as population numbers; residents are not themselves the cause of poor planning or exclusion. In an invented settlement, households increase from 500 to 900 while functioning water points remain at five. Households per point rise from 100 to 180. The additional demand can lengthen queues if supply and use conditions are similar. The ratio identifies pressure, not the measured waiting time or an explanation of why services were not expanded.

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-02: Effects on rural areas
Departure can reduce available rural labour or care support, while remittances and new connections can also support rural households. The net effect depends on who leaves, when work is needed, whether earnings are available and whether money or knowledge returns. In a fictional village, several experienced workers leave before planting and remaining households cannot hire replacements. A labour shortage may delay planting and reduce production. In a different case, earnings sent home might finance tools or hired help. Do not assume departure has only a cost or only a benefit.

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-03: Housing needs and informal settlements
When affordable housing and serviced land are insufficient, some residents build or occupy housing outside formal planning or tenure arrangements. Informal settlements vary; inadequate infrastructure or insecure access can affect living conditions, but a settlement label does not describe every household. The syllabus term shanty towns refers to a form of low-quality informal housing. Use respectful descriptions of the actual shelter, service and access conditions. In an invented town, rents exceed new workers’ earnings and no serviced low-cost plots are available. Self-built housing near work can meet an immediate shelter need while leaving water and sanitation unresolved.

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-04: Sanitation pressure
Sanitation concerns safe management of human waste, and insufficient capacity can leave waste poorly contained or treated. When demand exceeds functioning provision, contamination of the living environment becomes a greater concern. In a fictional settlement, household numbers rise but toilets and waste-removal capacity do not. Overflowing facilities and uncollected waste show a service gap rather than proof of residents’ habits. A response needs usable facilities, maintenance and a workable disposal route, not construction alone.

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-05: Water supply and pollution
Water provision depends on a safe and accessible supply, functioning distribution and management of demand and contamination. A damaged or overloaded system can combine access difficulties with pollution risks where waste reaches the supply. In an invented neighbourhood, households rely on a shared water point beside an overflowing drain, and several supply interruptions force collection from an unprotected alternative. Both reliability and protection matter. More pipe capacity would not by itself address waste reaching the collection area; drainage management alone would not restore missing supply.

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-06: Transport and communication
Transport connects residents with work and services, while communication supports access to information and coordination. Growth at poorly connected edges can increase journey time and costs and limit the opportunities residents can actually reach. In a fictional settlement, homes expand beyond a bus route and workers walk far before boarding an already full bus. Frequent late arrivals can threaten access to work even when vacancies exist. A route extension, frequency improvement or better connection should be judged against distance, capacity and affordability rather than road length alone.

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-07: Develop the mechanism
Take a new fictional case: a town adds 200 households but its water points and sanitation capacity are unchanged. Greater demand can lengthen collection queues and overload waste facilities, creating linked access and environmental problems. To explain the effect, use both the increase in demand and the unchanged capacity. Do not invent measured illness rates or queue times. Rural household effects and housing costs are separate mechanisms that also need their own evidence. Topic 7.2 develops the health context; this lesson identifies infrastructure and access pressures.

### BLK-6136-4.2.03-08: Connect causes and responses
Link each observed service or livelihood constraint to the relevant mechanism and affected people; avoid blaming residents or assuming identical experiences.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.2.04: Responses must fit the barrier

### BLK-6136-4.2.04-01: Responses must fit the barrier
Possible urban-growth responses include self-help housing, site-and-services schemes, rural development and planned new towns. These responses address different mechanisms and require resources, participation and continuing services; judge them by delivered access and functioning services. An urgent water-service failure needs a different first response from a shortage of job opportunities or serviced residential land. Identify affected residents, feasibility and an implementer. Compare improvements to existing areas with relocation: a distant site may solve a land constraint while making jobs less accessible. The stated criterion should guide the choice.

### BLK-6136-4.2.04-02: Self-help schemes
Self-help housing involves residents contributing labour, decisions or resources to improve or build shelter, usually with suitable supporting arrangements. It may use local capability and staged improvements but can exclude people lacking time, physical capacity, finance or secure access. An invented community has willing builders and appropriate materials but needs technical support and a safe service connection. Supported self-help may fit those capabilities. Requiring households already working long hours to build unaided is a different proposition. Participation should be feasible and service responsibilities clear; self-help does not mean abandoning residents to provide everything.

### BLK-6136-4.2.04-03: Site and services schemes
A site-and-services scheme provides planned plots with essential supporting services or access arrangements, with housing often developed in stages. It can address a serviced-land shortage, but affordability, location, secure usable access and continued service maintenance determine who benefits. A plot without a practical work connection can increase travel costs. A plot price above residents’ means leaves the access problem unresolved. Compare real household capabilities and the site’s services rather than treating a grid of plots as completed housing provision. This is a conceptual scheme, not a statement of current local allocation law.

### BLK-6136-4.2.04-04: Rural development projects
Rural development can improve livelihoods, services and opportunities in rural areas. It can expand genuine choices and reduce some pressures to migrate, but does not automatically reverse urbanisation or replace urban service provision. Repairing a farm-market connection may improve the viability of rural work where products and buyers exist. People can still prefer or need to move for other reasons. Judge the programme by rural well-being and opportunity, not by treating movement itself as a failure. Topic 5.1 develops the productive strategies in more depth.

### BLK-6136-4.2.04-05: New towns
New towns are planned settlements intended to provide an organised location for housing, work and services. They can distribute activity and provide planned infrastructure, but require substantial coordination, finance and real links between homes, jobs and services. In a fictional proposal, serviced housing is available far from employment without affordable transport. Relocation may shift rather than solve the access problem. A town with credible work opportunities and maintained services has a stronger basis. Compare timing and costs with improving existing settlements; a planned name does not establish a complete community.

### BLK-6136-4.2.04-06: Compare an urgent and a longer response
An invented existing settlement has an urgent water-access gap. A funded extension can connect it to nearby usable supply; a new town would take several years and its job connections are unresolved. The extension better fits the immediate access criterion, while long-term housing planning remains relevant. In a different case, a well-connected serviced site may solve a land shortage more directly. A recommendation names the implementer, the practical action and why it fits the evidence. Local authorities, service providers and residents can have different responsibilities; do not imply residents alone must finance a network.

### BLK-6136-4.2.04-07: Connect causes and responses
Match the time horizon and access problem to a feasible response. Rural Development in Topic 5.1 complements urban provision rather than replacing it.

## UNIT CU-6136-4.2.05: Two different classifications

### BLK-6136-4.2.05-01: Two different classifications
Employment status concerns whether and how much someone works; formal or informal economic activity concerns institutional and regulatory arrangements. An informal economic activity is not an informal settlement, which describes a residential area; neither label alone determines a person’s employment status. A person can work in a registered business while living in an informal settlement. Another can carry out informal economic work elsewhere. Test whether the evidence describes work, registration or housing. The classifications can be combined rather than substituted for each other.

### BLK-6136-4.2.05-02: Unemployment and underemployment
Unemployment is the situation of people without work who are available for and seeking work; exact statistical definitions depend on the source. Underemployment means having work that uses less than a person’s available working time or productive capacity; it differs from having no work. An invented worker seeking work but with no job is distinguished from a worker with six paid hours who is available for more. State what is measured; not everyone without a job is counted as unemployed, for example if unavailable for work under the stated definition. A low-hours example illustrates time-related underemployment without claiming that every part-time worker is underemployed.

### BLK-6136-4.2.05-03: Formal and informal sector
Formal economic activity operates within the relevant recognised registration and regulatory arrangements; informal activity operates outside some of them. The exact statistical boundary depends on the source; informal does not by itself mean unemployed, illegal or located in an informal settlement. An invented unregistered repair activity still produces a service and can provide work and income. Its institutional classification says nothing by itself about the quality of the worker’s home. A registered shop may also offer few hours, so formal status does not establish adequate employment. Use the definition and relevant evidence together.

### BLK-6136-4.2.05-04: An original work comparison
In a fictional survey of 100 people available for work, 30 have no work, 40 have fewer paid hours than they are available for, and 30 have their desired hours. The unemployed total is 30. The employed total is 70 (40 underemployed + 30 with their desired hours). People with unmet work needs total 70 (30 unemployed + 40 underemployed). These two totals overlap because the 40 underemployed people are included in the employed total; do not add the totals together. Calling all 70 with unmet work needs unemployed would erase the distinction. A separate registration table could classify their economic activities; their housing location would not answer it. Topic 3.1 explains labour as a productive input; here classify work status and activity correctly.

### BLK-6136-4.2.05-05: Connect causes and responses
Keep work, institutional registration and residential location separate. An informal settlement can contain people in different work situations.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.2-002-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.2.01-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}

ANSWER: The urban population increases by 200,000 but its share falls from 40% to 30%. Thus the larger number does not show urbanisation during the interval; the share falls by 10 percentage points.

ANSWER: 360,000 ÷ 900,000 = 0.4; multiplying by 100 gives 40% of the population.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.2-025-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.2.01-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain the error in saying urbanisation must have occurred because an invented country’s urban population rose from 400,000 to 600,000 while its total rose from one million to two million.
ANSWER: The urban number rises but its share falls from 40% to 30%. Urbanisation requires a rising proportion, so test the urban count against the total population in each period rather than the count alone.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: urban population growth is substituted for urbanisation.", "Distinction: number versus rising share, supported by the denominators.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.2-004-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.2.02-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how rural–urban migration can contribute to urbanisation.
ANSWER: If net movement adds to the urban population relative to the rural population, the urban share can rise. Other population changes and definitions also matter, so migration is a contributor rather than the definition.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: connect net movement to the relative share, rather than equating any migration with urbanisation.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.2-005-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.2.02-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe the relationship in this original fictional comparison: A’s urban share rises from 30% to 45% with annual net rural–urban inflow 5,000; B’s rises from 70% to 72% with inflow 500. Comparable periods and definitions are used.
ANSWER: A combines a larger inflow with a larger share increase: 15 percentage points compared with two in B. B starts more urban but urbanises more slowly by this share measure.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: compare both inflow and the changes in share.", "Relationship: distinguish starting level and rate of urbanisation; causation is not established by the pair alone.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.2-006-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.2.02-01"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two broad characteristics that can occur in industrialised-country urban patterns.
ANSWER: A high existing urban share can reflect earlier industrial and service concentration. Later movement between urban areas or towards suburbs can change city populations without necessarily increasing the national urban share.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: two relevant qualified characteristics, not a claim that every country follows the same path.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.2-007-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-4.2.02-02"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two possible characteristics of rapid urban growth in a developing economy.
ANSWER: Migration and urban natural increase may combine with a rising urban share. Where housing and services grow more slowly than needs, access pressures can increase.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: two relevant qualified features; poverty is not an automatic outcome for all residents.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-4.2-009-CHAIN
