### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-006-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-00"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: State two interacting influences on rural production.
ANSWER: Seasonal water availability and the organisation of labour.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: two relevant influences; accept tools, access or social organisation.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-007-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the constraint in a fictional village where planting depends on seasonal rain but workers are absent when the rain arrives.
ANSWER: The useful planting window and available labour do not coincide. Delayed planting can limit crop establishment and expected output despite rain arriving.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: timing of labour relative to rainfall.", "Consequence: restricted crop establishment/output.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-008-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-01"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe the difference between land use and rights over land in rural production.
ANSWER: Land use is the activity, such as grazing or growing crops. Land rights determine who may access the land and make decisions about its use.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-009-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-02"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two examples of division of labour in farming households.
ANSWER: Different workers can specialise in tending livestock and cultivating crops. Others can process or market the harvested output while those tasks continue.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-010-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-03"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two uses of tools in rural production.
ANSWER: A hand hoe can loosen soil and remove weeds around crops. Animal-drawn equipment can help cultivate a larger area with less direct human effort per task.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-011-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-04"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two effects of seasonal rain on farming work.
ANSWER: The arrival of suitable rain can determine when rain-fed crops are planted. The resulting harvest period concentrates harvesting and storage work later in the season.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-012-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-05"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two ways social cooperation can support rural production.
ANSWER: Neighbours can share labour during a short planting or harvesting period. Farmers can exchange knowledge about local soils, crops and conditions.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-013-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-06"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two environmental influences on rural livelihoods.
ANSWER: Rainfall affects the water available for crops and grazing. Soil fertility affects which crops can be grown and the output obtained under suitable management.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-014-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-07"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two ways farming could change before colonial rule.
ANSWER: Communities could adopt tools or techniques learned through exchange. They could introduce or adapt crops to changing local growing conditions.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-015-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-08"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two ways colonial arrangements could alter rural livelihoods.
ANSWER: Loss of land could reduce the area available for food production. Pressure to earn cash through migrant work could change who remained to farm and care for the household.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-016-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.02-09"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe how smallholder farming and cash cropping identify different features of production.
ANSWER: Smallholder farming describes household-scale productive arrangements. Cash cropping describes growing crops mainly to sell; a smallholder can combine cash crops with food grown for household use.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-017-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.03-00"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Identify", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Identify the farming orientation when crops are grown primarily for sale.
ANSWER: Commercial farming.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: market orientation.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-018-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.03-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe this fictional comparison: Farm A repeats one crop and receives all crop revenue from it; B rotates crops and earns revenue from three.
ANSWER: A concentrates crop and income exposure; B combines crop rotation with a more varied revenue base. A price fall in one crop therefore exposes a larger share of A’s recorded crop income.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: use crop practice and revenue concentration.", "Relationship: link concentration to exposure, without claiming rotation eliminates risk.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-019-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.03-01"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two tasks that equipment can assist on a commercial farm.
ANSWER: A pump can supply irrigation water where a usable source exists. Harvesting equipment can gather a crop more quickly when trained operators and repairs are available.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-020-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.03-02"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe the difference between monoculture and large-scale farming.
ANSWER: Monoculture means growing one crop across an area, often repeatedly. Scale concerns the size of the operation; a small farm can practise monoculture too.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-021-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.03-03"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two features of plantation farming.
ANSWER: A plantation is an estate growing a commercial crop for sale. Its organised production can supply a processing activity using concentrated quantities of that crop.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-022-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.03-04"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two features of crop rotation.
ANSWER: Different crops follow a planned sequence on the same land. A suitable sequence can interrupt some pests’ life cycles or help maintain soil condition.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-023-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why a rural scheme should start with the actual production or marketing constraint.
ANSWER: Different obstacles require different responses: seed finance cannot restore a blocked delivery road. Identifying the mechanism directs resources towards the barrier to useful output or sales.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: match obstacle and response.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-024-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which rural scheme to fund first in a fictional village. Growers have buyers. An existing usable road causes delays and transport losses; repair would reduce those losses. A seed loan would increase output and can be repaid after harvest, but adds repayment risk. Funds cover only one first. The aim is more dependable net sales.
ANSWER: Road repair can preserve more of the existing harvest and lower delivery losses without adding farmers’ debt. A seed loan can expand saleable output, but repayment remains due if the harvest or prices disappoint. Prioritise repair where current transport losses are large enough to outweigh the extra output from seed. If losses are minor and input shortage is decisive, the loan could offer the better gain. Compare retained sales after costs rather than output alone.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-056-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why a farmer with a harvested crop and buyers but no usable delivery route has a marketing problem rather than simply a growing problem.
ANSWER: The crop has been produced; access to buyers prevents selling it. Test whether the barrier stops growing output or reaching and selling to the market.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: sales-access obstacle treated as crop-growing failure.", "Distinction: production versus marketing test.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-025-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-01"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a seed loan suits this fictional household. Suitable water, skills and buyers exist. Repayment follows harvest and the expected return exceeds borrowing cost, but a poor season could leave a debt. A smaller self-financed planting has lower expected income and no repayment obligation. Weigh income and security.
ANSWER: The loan removes an input constraint and repayment timing matches expected sales, making the larger planting credible. The household also bears a downside if weather or prices reduce returns. Self-financing limits income potential but protects it from a fixed repayment. The loan is preferable when a prudent smaller trial or reserve makes the downside manageable; otherwise the smaller planting may better protect basic needs. Expected gain alone does not establish affordability.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-026-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-02"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a cooperative store suits fictional growers. Shared storage lowers their cost per bag and members agree contributions. A private store costs more but offers flexible collection times; cooperative decisions currently favour larger members. Both stores are reachable and reliable.
ANSWER: The cooperative offers a real cost saving and shared productive capacity, useful for growers unable to store separately. Unequal decision influence can distribute access unfairly, while the private store’s flexibility may suit small growers’ work schedules despite its higher fee. Prefer the cooperative if transparent access and voting arrangements make the savings usable for all members. If that cannot be secured, the flexible store can be more suitable for disadvantaged members.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-027-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-03"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether resettlement suits fictional farmers. New plots offer more land, reliable water and a funded market route, but moving disrupts support networks. Improving the present site preserves those networks but leaves smaller plots and lower potential output. Both arrangements are financially feasible.
ANSWER: Resettlement has credible productive benefits because water and market links accompany the land. Relocation still carries social and adjustment costs, particularly where families rely on nearby care or shared work. Present-site improvement protects those relationships but limits expansion. Relocation is stronger for households whose productive gains outweigh the disruption and who choose it with support; improving the current site may better fit others. More land is one criterion, not a complete welfare judgement.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-028-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-04"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether to begin a fictional integrated water-and-road scheme or a smaller water project first. Agencies have funded both needs and agreed responsibilities. Integration reaches more farms but takes two years; the smaller project reaches fewer households in six months. The present water gap is serious and resources cover one start.
ANSWER: Integration can join production and market access, avoiding a water improvement whose output cannot reach buyers. Its longer timetable leaves urgent needs unresolved for longer. The smaller project delivers earlier relief but with narrower reach and an outstanding road need. Prefer a staged start that secures urgent water while maintaining the agreed wider sequence if feasible. If choosing one alone, weigh the urgency of the gap against the additional households and connected benefits.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-029-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-05"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a fictional grassroots project should proceed now. Broad consultations, including remote households and women, identified the priority. It uses local skills and small member contributions, but expanding coverage requires outside funds and may delay work. A smaller start is feasible now.
ANSWER: A smaller start can meet an agreed need with available skills and preserve local influence. Wider funding can include more households, but delay and funder conditions may weaken immediacy or community control. Proceed with a transparent first stage if its benefits are useful on their own and excluded households have a credible later route. Seek wider funds where the small stage would entrench unfair access. Local initiation is valuable but does not settle coverage.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-030-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-06"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether to choose locally repairable soil-cover methods or a powered conservation service in this fictional farming area. Both reduce erosion. Soil cover uses available skills and little cash but needs more labour during planting. The funded service saves labour and includes repairs for two years, but later fees are uncertain.
ANSWER: Soil cover offers low cash costs and local maintenance, but competes for scarce planting labour. The powered service has a genuine timeliness benefit and initial repair support, with an uncertain later cost. Prefer soil cover where households can supply the labour without harming planting. A service is stronger where seasonal labour is the main constraint and an affordable continuing arrangement can be secured. Compare long-term soil protection and resource demands together.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-031-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why soil conservation matters to rural livelihoods.
ANSWER: Protecting productive soil preserves a resource needed for future yields, so immediate extraction that causes erosion may reduce later food or income.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: connect resource condition to future production.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-032-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the rural-development mechanism in this fictional case: an exposed cultivated slope loses topsoil after heavy rain and later crops grow poorly.
ANSWER: Loss of protective cover lets runoff remove productive soil, reducing the growing base; restoring suitable cover addresses future production, rather than adding seed alone.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism, up to 2 marks: explain how lost cover and rainfall allow runoff to remove topsoil. Credit the stated factor and a valid causal link, not a disconnected list.", "Consequence, 1 mark: link reduced productive soil to poorer growth or an appropriate protective response.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-033-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-01"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the rural-development mechanism in this fictional case: vegetation is cleared on a slope, then rain carries soil into the lower field.
ANSWER: Clearance removes protection and runoff transports soil, reducing productive topsoil and affecting the lower field.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism, up to 2 marks: connect vegetation clearance to reduced protection and movement of soil in runoff. Credit the stated factor and a valid causal link, not a disconnected list.", "Consequence, 1 mark: identify loss at the original site or deposition affecting the lower field.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-034-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-02"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the rural-development mechanism in this fictional woodland: 180 trees are cut each year while 125 replace losses under unchanged conditions.
ANSWER: Harvesting exceeds replacement by 55 trees a year. The stock therefore declines despite wood being renewable, reducing future fuel availability if that imbalance continues.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism, up to 2 marks: compare harvesting with replacement through regrowth and establish a net annual loss of 55 trees. Credit the stated factor and a valid causal link, not a disconnected list.", "Consequence, 1 mark: link continued stock decline to later fuel availability.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-035-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-03"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the rural-development mechanism in this fictional case: a field’s topsoil washes away and the same seed produces less under otherwise comparable conditions.
ANSWER: Loss of productive soil can reduce crop performance; increasing seed does not restore the damaged growing base.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism, up to 2 marks: identify loss of the growing medium rather than seed quantity as the stated constraint. Credit the stated factor and a valid causal link, not a disconnected list.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain why extra seed alone leaves that constraint unresolved.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-036-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-04"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the rural-development mechanism in this fictional case: agricultural waste is left beside a drainage channel and rainfall carries it to a shared water source.
ANSWER: Runoff transports the waste into the supply environment, creating a pollution pathway affecting downstream users; source management must address that route.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism, up to 2 marks: trace waste from its location through rain runoff into the shared supply. Credit the stated factor and a valid causal link, not a disconnected list.", "Consequence, 1 mark: identify downstream water-use consequences or a matched pollution-prevention route.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-037-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-05"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the rural-development mechanism in this fictional case: habitat clearance removes nesting sites and fewer birds are observed using the area.
ANSWER: Loss of habitat can reduce use by affected species and biodiversity; the observation supports a habitat link without proving every species changed.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism, up to 2 marks: link habitat clearance to fewer suitable nesting opportunities. Credit the stated factor and a valid causal link, not a disconnected list.", "Consequence, 1 mark: bound the observed bird-use change rather than infer changes to every species.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-038-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-06"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain how this fictional conservation improvement can support rural livelihoods: lining a leaking irrigation channel cuts water losses from 35 to 15 units out of 140 supplied; crop needs and farmers’ access arrangements are unchanged.
ANSWER: Usable water rises from 105 to 125 units without increasing the original supply. Reducing loss can support farming while limiting additional extraction, provided the lining is maintained and the extra usable water reaches the farms.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism, up to 2 marks: derive a 20-unit loss reduction and connect it to water reaching crops. Credit the stated factor and a valid causal link, not a disconnected list.", "Consequence, 1 mark: retain maintenance and farmers’ access conditions for the livelihood benefit.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-039-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.06-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why public agencies and local groups may need to coordinate a rural water project.
ANSWER: Agencies can supply network resources while residents contribute knowledge about access and use; clear roles join those capabilities and support maintenance.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: complementary resources linked to implementation.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-040-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.06-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a practical arrangement for an invented village with a funded pump but no maintenance responsibility and residents reporting confusing operating times.
ANSWER: The provider and a representative local committee should agree a maintenance contact, operating timetable and fault-reporting arrangement. This addresses responsibility and information gaps using the existing funded asset.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: funded pump, missing roles and timetable.", "Reason: link clear arrangements to usable service.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-041-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.06-01"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how public provision of a rural road can support farmers’ sales.
ANSWER: A usable road allows harvested produce to reach buyers before it spoils. Public provision can connect several farms where individual farmers cannot finance the shared route.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify shared public provision of a usable market route.", "Consequence, 1 mark: link timely produce delivery to possible sales or reduced spoilage.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-042-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.06-02"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how accountable records can help a formal rural group manage shared resources.
ANSWER: Recorded contributions and decisions let members check how shared funds are used. That can reduce disputes and make continued participation more credible.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify how members can check recorded contributions or spending.", "Consequence, 1 mark: link scrutiny to fewer disputes, trust or continued participation.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-043-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.06-03"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain one limitation of relying on an informal neighbour group to maintain a regional water network.
ANSWER: Neighbours can share local work, but may lack the finance and technical capacity for a large network. The maintenance arrangement needs a provider able to meet those wider requirements.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify a scale-related finance or technical limitation of the neighbour group.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain the need for a maintenance actor capable of the wider task.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-044-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.06-04"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how an individual farmer can contribute to soil conservation.
ANSWER: Maintaining suitable ground cover reduces exposure of the soil to wind and rain. This can protect the productive layer without requiring the farmer to finance a regional project.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify suitable ground cover as an action available to the farmer.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain how reduced soil exposure protects later productive capacity.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-045-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.06-05"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why consultation about local values can improve a rural project.
ANSWER: Consultation can identify practices people value and changes they want. A project can then adapt its arrangements to gain useful participation rather than assume one person speaks for the whole community.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify how consultation obtains evidence of varied local preferences.", "Consequence, 1 mark: link adapting arrangements to relevant participation without assuming one representative speaks for everyone.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-046-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.07-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional tourism plan better fits locally retained livelihoods: A uses local guides and suppliers with agreed community control; B attracts more visitors but imports staff and supplies. Both have viable demand.
ANSWER: A has clearer local income links through employment, purchases and influence. B’s larger visitor count may raise total receipts but supplies weaker evidence of retained local benefits; assess costs and capacity too.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: choose against retained local livelihoods.", "Reason: weigh visitor volume against actual participation and supply links.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-047-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.07-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a participation arrangement where a fictional village wants visitor income but residents are concerned about unwanted access to cultural activities.
ANSWER: Residents should agree which activities are offered, access limits, consent and how local guides or suppliers retain earnings. This links feasible income to community control rather than assuming access to private practices.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: income aim and stated cultural concern.", "Reason: match consent and retained benefits.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-048-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.07-01"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether cultural tourism fits a fictional community with willing paid guides but disagreement over showing private ceremonies. A guided public-history walk is an alternative.
ANSWER: The public-history option is better supported while ceremony consent is unresolved. It can use willing guides and retain local earnings without assuming permission; visitor interest alone does not justify intrusion.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: weigh income and consent.", "Reason: compare the actual alternatives and participation conditions.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-049-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.07-02"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a fictional conservation visitor scheme suits local well-being when habitats are protected but grazing access is restricted and residents receive no work or supply contracts.
ANSWER: Habitat protection is a benefit, but livelihood costs and absent local income links weaken the proposal. Participation and workable benefit/access arrangements are needed; a conservation label alone does not resolve the trade-off.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: conditional suitability for local well-being.", "Reason: weigh protection with access costs and retained-benefit gaps.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-050-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.08-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the income mechanism where a fictional visitor centre buys local produce and hires local guides, while confirmed visitors can reach it.
ANSWER: Visitor demand creates paid work and sales for participating households, retaining some spending locally. The access and local supply/employment links make the gain plausible; visitor numbers alone would not establish it.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: visitor spending through local work and purchases.", "Consequence: retained household income under supplied demand/access.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-051-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.08-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe this original fictional tourism comparison: A retains N$60 of each N$100 visitor spending locally and uses 40 water units per visitor; B retains N$20 and uses 70.
ANSWER: A combines greater local retention with lower water demand: N$40 more retained and 30 fewer water units per visitor. The two measures favour A on those criteria, without a complete net-impact verdict.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: accurate paired comparisons.", "Relationship: link retention and resource demand against shared criteria.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-052-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.08-01"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe one social benefit and one social cost of tourism.
ANSWER: Local guide work can develop communication and service skills. Visitor intrusion into private cultural activities can create conflict where consent is absent.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-053-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.08-02"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Describe two economic links between tourism and rural households.
ANSWER: Tourist businesses can pay local residents for work. They can purchase food or other supplies from local producers, retaining more spending in the area.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-054-FEATURE
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.08-03"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}

ANSWER: Tourism income can help fund habitat protection. Concentrated visitors can increase waste and demand for scarce water.
GUIDANCE: ["Feature: credit two distinct accurate descriptions matching the prompt.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.04-00", "OBJ-6136-5.1.04-01"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) one production obstacle and one marketing obstacle [2]. Explain (b) why a seed loan may fail to improve an invented group’s sales when it already has stored crops and buyers but the market road is blocked [4].
ANSWER: (a) Insufficient growing water; a blocked route to buyers. (b) The group already has stored crops and willing buyers, so a shortage of seed is not the immediate sales constraint. The blocked route prevents delivery, leaving existing output unsold. A seed loan would add repayment obligations and may add crops that still cannot reach buyers. A feasible delivery or access arrangement should therefore be investigated first; its cost and reliability must be checked before assuming sales will rise.
GUIDANCE: ["Point: two correctly distinguished obstacles [2].", "Development: use both existing output/buyers and blocked access, then explain why the loan misses the constraint [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-5.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-5.1.05-00", "OBJ-6136-5.1.05-06"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Describe (a) this fictional comparison for 100 harvested bags: A loses 30 before sale; B loses 10, with the same available buyers [3]. Suggest (b) a priority if the aim is higher useful sales without more land clearance, and a feasible storage repair is available for A [3].
ANSWER: (a) B has 90 usable bags versus A’s 70, so lower storage loss accompanies 20 more useful bags from the same harvest. (b) Repair A’s storage and maintain it: reducing avoidable loss can improve sales without expanding cleared land. Buyers and repair feasibility make this a matched response.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: compare loss and useful output [3].", "Meaning: justify the storage priority against useful sales and unchanged land use [3].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 6.1: Women in traditional and pre-colonial societies

## UNIT CU-6136-6.1.01: Women’s productive and social roles

### BLK-6136-6.1.01-01: Women’s productive and social roles
Women’s labour in rural societies has included food production, farming, processing, household provision and community activity. Roles vary across time, place and households. Unpaid work contributes to well-being even when it is absent from wage records; a role description is not a justification for unequal opportunity.

### BLK-6136-6.1.01-02: Labour
Women contribute physical and mental labour in paid and unpaid activities. Counting wages alone can conceal food preparation, care or productive household work, so assess tasks and time rather than assuming no earnings means no contribution.

Applied example: Women may perform paid work such as selling produce. Unpaid farming or household production also supplies goods and services even when no wage is recorded.

### BLK-6136-6.1.01-03: Food production
Women can contribute through planting, tending, harvesting, processing and distributing food. Access to land, tools, time and decision-making affects this contribution. Work performed does not establish control over its product or income.

Applied example: Planting and tending crops help produce the harvest. Processing and preparing harvested food make it usable for households or sale.

### BLK-6136-6.1.01-04: Family welfare
Care, food preparation, water collection and household organisation support family welfare. Unequal caring burdens can restrict time for learning or paid work. Sharing responsibilities or improving services can expand choices without devaluing care.

Applied example: Preparing food supports members’ daily nutritional needs. Caring for children or ill relatives supports their well-being and enables other necessary activities.

### BLK-6136-6.1.01-05: Read the practical connection
A household food diary in a fictional settlement records vegetables grown for consumption, meals prepared and hours of care alongside cash earnings. Selling no produce does not make the vegetables valueless: they meet needs that would otherwise require purchases. Care also supports other members’ participation. Assess the contribution without assuming that the person doing the work necessarily controls the household’s income.

## UNIT CU-6136-6.1.02: Culture and position

### BLK-6136-6.1.02-01: Culture and position
Cultural values and practices influence expectations about work, property and participation. They can support cooperation or restrict opportunities depending on their form and use; culture is diverse and changing, not a fixed explanation of all women’s experiences.

### BLK-6136-6.1.02-02: Values
Values concern what people regard as desirable or acceptable. A value placed on mutual support may encourage shared work, while an expectation that leadership belongs to one gender can restrict voice. Explain the mechanism rather than stereotyping a community.

### BLK-6136-6.1.02-03: Customs
Customs and practices shape how social decisions are carried out. A meeting timetable or speaking arrangement can affect who participates. Consultation can reveal whether a practice supports or restricts access and what affected people want changed.

### BLK-6136-6.1.02-04: Taboos and myths
A taboo restricts an activity or discussion; a myth may be used to explain or justify a belief. If a belief excludes women from a productive role regardless of skill, it narrows opportunity. Describing that effect does not endorse the belief or claim it is shared by all members.

### BLK-6136-6.1.02-05: Read the practical connection
In a fictional savings group, important decisions are made while some members are collecting children from school. Written membership rights do not give those members an effective voice at that time. Consultation over meeting times and a way to submit views can improve participation. Such arrangements should respond to the actual members’ duties rather than assume that all women share the same responsibilities.

## UNIT CU-6136-6.1.03: Assess colonial changes

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-01: Assess colonial changes
Colonial arrangements could reshape education, land access, wages, migration and commercial production, with differing effects on women. Evaluate access, workload and control separately. New wage activity can coexist with exclusion from land or training; one opportunity is not proof of overall equality.

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-02: Education access
Exclusion from schooling or training can restrict access to skills and recognised opportunities. Where selection favours men despite comparable ability, women’s productive choices can be narrowed; a later formal admission rule does not automatically remove fees, distance or time barriers.

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-03: Land entitlement
Access to and control over productive land influence what a person can grow and decide. Where women work land but lack secure decision rights, their labour contribution need not give control over investment or returns. Distinguish labour performed from entitlement.

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-04: Wages and labour
Paid employment can provide income while discriminatory selection, pay or conditions limit its gains. Compare access, earnings and workload together. Entering wage work does not remove unpaid responsibilities unless their arrangement changes.

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-05: Migratory labour
Labour migration separates work locations and can change household income and task allocation. Remittances may help, but absent workers can leave others with more farming and care. The balance depends on actual transfers, timing and available support.

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-06: Commercial farming
Commercial expansion can alter land access, labour demand and what households produce for sale. Women may gain earnings while facing reduced food-growing access or control of proceeds. Assess whose opportunities and resources change rather than assuming a common benefit.

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-07: Read the practical connection
In a fictional historical comparison, a household gains wage income after moving closer to an employer, but now buys food formerly grown on its own plot. The wage gain must be compared with replacement food costs and changed work and care time. Employment can expand opportunities while another change reduces control over resources; neither wage access nor the loss alone establishes the combined welfare result.

### BLK-6136-6.1.03-R01: Historical mechanisms in Namibia
Colonial land dispossession reduced access to productive resources in Namibia. Contract labour and migrant work could separate wage earners from rural households, increasing the farming and caring tasks left to women. Discriminatory access to education and formal jobs restricted some women’s alternatives, while cash-crop and wage relationships changed how households met needs. Do not describe women as passive or all households as identical: women also produced food, traded, organised and adapted to these pressures. Judge control of land and income as well as labour performed; a heavier contribution need not bring stronger decision rights.

PROMPT: Explain whether a fictional historical change establishes an overall improvement for women: wage work becomes available, but women lose food-growing access and retain the same unpaid workload. Judge livelihood choice and welfare.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.1-012-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.1.03-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe this fictional historical comparison: before a change, women hold 60% of household food plots and 10% of paid jobs; afterwards, 30% of plots and 25% of paid jobs.
ANSWER: Paid-job participation rises 15 percentage points while plot control falls 30 points. The two access measures move in opposing directions, so more paid work does not establish wider access to all productive resources.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: accurate paired changes.", "Relationship: contrast paid opportunity and plot control.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.1-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.1.01-00", "OBJ-6136-6.1.03-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) two useful measures of women’s contribution beyond wages [2]. Explain (b) why a fictional change bringing paid jobs but removing food-growing access cannot be judged from job numbers alone [4].
ANSWER: (a) Food production and time spent providing care. (b) New paid work can increase money income, but counting jobs does not show the amount or reliability of those earnings. Losing growing land removes a source of household food and may reduce women’s control over productive resources. Purchasing replacement food can absorb some of the new income, while paid work may add to an unchanged care workload. Compare net resources, food access, time and decision-making control before judging whether overall welfare and choice improved.
GUIDANCE: ["Point: two relevant non-wage measures [2].", "Development: connect the opposing supplied changes to a bounded welfare judgement [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.1.02-00", "OBJ-6136-6.1.03-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Describe (a) this fictional comparison: A offers training at 40% participation and meetings at 20%; B offers 70% and 60% [3]. Suggest (b) a priority for A if both timetable clashes and lack of care cover are reported and the aim is fair access [3].
ANSWER: (a) B combines higher training and meeting participation, by 30 and 40 percentage points. (b) Arrange workable times and care support with affected participants, then monitor access; those changes address stated barriers rather than interpreting low participation as unwillingness.

# TOPIC 6.2: The role of women in modern society

## UNIT CU-6136-6.2.01: Gender equality

### BLK-6136-6.2.01-01: Gender equality
Gender equality means equal rights, opportunities and treatment regardless of gender; formal equality does not by itself establish equal lived outcomes. Equal admission rules can coexist with barriers such as cost, safety, caring responsibilities or discrimination. Judge actual access as well as the written rule.

### BLK-6136-6.2.01-02: A practical access test
A fictional skills centre allows all qualified applicants to enrol but provides instructions only in a language some applicants cannot use. Equal eligibility is a formal opportunity; understandable information is part of practical access. Suitable communication and feedback may widen participation. Check whether the relevant groups actually enrol and complete the programme rather than assuming that a rule change produces equal outcomes.

## UNIT CU-6136-6.2.02: Changing opportunities

### BLK-6136-6.2.02-01: Changing opportunities
Women’s participation in jobs, public decisions and projects can expand through access reforms, skills and support arrangements. A new opportunity needs usable access. Participation percentages should identify the eligible group and distinguish attendance, completion and decision-making influence.

### BLK-6136-6.2.02-02: Access to jobs
Equal job access concerns fair opportunities to apply, gain relevant skills and be considered without gender discrimination. Selection rules, transport, caring duties and workplace treatment can affect actual access. A higher total number of jobs does not show who can obtain them.

Applied example: Fair selection procedures can remove gender discrimination in recruitment. Workable care or transport arrangements can make an available job usable for an applicant.

### BLK-6136-6.2.02-03: Policies and implementation
Policies can set equality aims and change procedures, but need responsibilities, resources and monitoring. A rule’s adoption is an output; reduced barriers or wider influence are outcomes. Compare implementation evidence rather than assuming a policy announcement resolves inequality.

Applied example: Assign responsibility and resources to carry out the policy. Monitor access and outcomes to identify barriers that equal written rules have not removed.

### BLK-6136-6.2.02-04: Targeted schemes
Women-oriented schemes can address a documented gap in finance, skills, services or participation. Targeting can improve access where the barrier is real, but eligibility, timing and decision rules can still exclude some intended participants.

Applied example: Relevant training can address a documented skills gap. Accessible finance can help participants obtain productive equipment where a viable opportunity exists.

### BLK-6136-6.2.02-05: Female-headed households
A female-headed household has a woman identified as its head under the definition used. It is not automatically poor or identical to other such households. Income, resources, care responsibilities and support need evidence rather than assumptions from the label.

Applied example: Evidence on income and productive resources shows material capacity. Evidence on caring responsibilities and available support shows demands on time and access.

### BLK-6136-6.2.02-06: A practical access test
After a fictional programme introduces accessible information and revises its timetable, attendance rises from 35% to 60%, an increase of 25 percentage points. The pattern is consistent with improved access but does not isolate which change caused it. Separate evidence on who attends, who completes and what barriers remain is needed to assess the programme’s effects.

## UNIT CU-6136-6.2.03: Political participation

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-01: Political participation
Women’s political roles include organisation, advocacy, participation and contributions to public decisions. Namibian women contributed to liberation and independence; subsequent integration into development requires substantive voice as well as recognition. Assess influence and access, not presence alone.

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-02: Liberation and independence
Women participated in Namibia’s liberation and independence struggle and in building political and social organisations. Recognition of past participation can challenge claims that public activity belongs to men, but historical contribution does not itself establish present equal influence.

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-03: National development
Integrating women into development includes access to resources, planning, skills and decisions across programmes. Consulting women after priorities and budgets are fixed offers less influence than participation while those decisions remain open.

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-04: Formal and factual equality
Formal equality concerns equal rules or recognised rights; factual inequality concerns unequal lived access or outcomes. Identify the practical barrier and evidence. Unequal outcomes alone do not isolate one cause, but equal written rules alone do not disprove a barrier.

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-05: Promoting rights
Promoting women’s rights can involve information, advocacy, accessible services and accountability for fair treatment. A right is more usable when people know it and can reach an effective response to exclusion. Do not invent a current legal procedure from a general principle.

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-06: A practical access test
An invented council invites women’s associations to submit written priorities before budget decisions. Written submissions reduce some travel demands, but people without literacy support may still be excluded. Facilitated local discussions can collect those views while keeping the final decision process open. Count influence on actual priorities as well as the number of invitations; participation needs a usable route to decisions.

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-07: A dated national context
A 1997 UN account of Namibia’s reporting described equal political participation and measures aimed at representation. Treat it as historical evidence, not a current legal guide: [UN meeting account](https://press.un.org/en/1997/19970708.wom976.html). The syllabus’s distinction between formal equality and lived inequality still requires access and influence evidence.

### BLK-6136-6.2.03-R01: Women in the liberation struggle
Women contributed to Namibia’s struggle for independence through political mobilisation, protest, maintaining households and communities, communication, medical support and participation in organised resistance. These are different roles, not a claim that every woman performed each task. Household and food work could sustain people engaged in public activity even without a wage. After independence, acknowledging those contributions should connect to real access to education, paid work, resources and public decisions. Representation alone cannot establish equal influence if priorities are already fixed before women are consulted.

## UNIT CU-6136-6.2.04: Participation in projects

### BLK-6136-6.2.04-01: Participation in projects
Rural and urban projects can connect women’s organisations, self-help groups and agencies to skills, finance or service needs. Assess retained benefits, meaningful decisions and the inclusion of less-represented participants. A project called a women’s scheme does not establish impact.

### BLK-6136-6.2.04-02: A practical access test
A fictional enterprise course can offer an accessible local venue for fewer people or a large central session with no travel assistance. The central session reaches more places on paper, while the local venue may be usable by residents who cannot afford the journey. Compare completion, quality and access for the intended group; a lower attendance total does not by itself establish a weaker inclusive result.

## UNIT CU-6136-6.2.05: Violence and abandonment

### BLK-6136-6.2.05-01: Violence and abandonment
Gender-based violence concerns harmful acts linked to gender and unequal power; baby dumping is a distinct child-protection issue. Discuss interacting social risks and barriers to support without treating poverty or household type as proof of violence or abandonment. Responsibility for violence rests with the perpetrator, not the survivor.

### BLK-6136-6.2.05-02: GBV-related factors
Unequal power, norms tolerating abuse, coercion and barriers to effective support can contribute to gender-based violence. Risk factors are not excuses or a prediction about an individual. An educational discussion should not request learners’ personal experiences or expose someone’s identity.

Applied example: A person claiming control over another’s choices may use threats or force, especially where norms excuse that behaviour. Unequal power can enable abuse, but responsibility remains with the person committing it.

### BLK-6136-6.2.05-03: Baby dumping factors
Crisis, fear, stigma, coercion, isolation and difficulty reaching trusted support can interact around unsafe baby dumping. No single factor explains a particular case, and young or poor parents should not be labelled as likely offenders. Focus on protection and accessible qualified support, without providing unsafe instructions.

### BLK-6136-6.2.05-04: A practical access test
A fictional information service receives few enquiries because callers expect their names to become public. Clear confidentiality arrangements, trained staff and a safe way to make first contact can reduce this barrier. Low use does not establish low need. Information about violence or child safety should focus on non-blaming support and competent services rather than require learners to disclose personal experiences.

### BLK-6136-6.2.05-05: Protection is a coordinated task
Namibia’s 2012–2016 GBV plan, documented by [UNDP](https://www.undp.org/namibia/publications/namibia-national-plan-gender-based-violence-gbv-2012-2016), combined prevention, service implementation and survivor support, including a distinct infant-abandonment concern. This is a dated example. [UNICEF Namibia](https://www.unicef.org/namibia/child-protection) describes child protection through supportive family, community and institutional environments. Checked 20 October 2026; no current hotline or legal procedure is asserted.

### BLK-6136-6.2.05-R01: Recognise the issue and its different effects
Baby dumping means abandoning an infant, commonly a newborn, without appropriate care, exposing the child to danger and potentially death. It is distinct from seeking qualified support to keep a child safe. Contributing circumstances can include an unintended pregnancy, fear of losing education, lack of financial or partner support, coercion or violence, poor access to confidential information, and emotional crisis. They explain possible support needs, not an excuse or a prediction about a particular parent. Effects can include injury or death, lasting emotional harm to a surviving child, distress for family members, disruption of registration and care, and extra demands on protection services. Avoid using national death-rate changes as a substitute for explaining effects on the people involved.

### BLK-6136-6.2.05-R02: Types and contributing factors of gender-based violence
Gender-based violence includes physical and sexual violence; emotional abuse and economic control can also undermine safety and autonomy. Unequal power, entitlement and norms that excuse abuse can enable it. Substance misuse, exposure to violent behaviour and financial dependence can worsen risks or obstruct help, but neither poverty nor alcohol makes violence inevitable. Blaming clothing or the survivor shifts responsibility away from the person committing violence. Physical injury, distress, missed learning or work and the costs of obtaining protection can affect development. Name the type or mechanism required by a question rather than listing vague social problems.

## UNIT CU-6136-6.2.06: Prevention and support

### BLK-6136-6.2.06-01: Prevention and support
Responses can combine prevention, accountable protection and accessible health and social support. Match the actual barrier, such as unsafe disclosure or transport, to a qualified implementer. A campaign alone may not make an existing service reachable.

### BLK-6136-6.2.06-02: Responses to GBV
Education about respectful relationships, safe reporting routes and survivor-centred professional support can form complementary responses. Confidentiality, safety and non-blaming treatment matter to usable access. A learner should seek help from a trusted responsible adult or qualified service rather than investigate a disclosure.

Applied example: Education can challenge norms that excuse abuse and promote respectful relationships. Accessible professional protection and support can help survivors obtain safety and assistance.

### BLK-6136-6.2.06-03: Responses to baby dumping
Accessible, non-stigmatising pregnancy, parenting, health and social support can address crises and barriers to help. Qualified services must handle immediate safety and child-protection needs. The lesson teaches prevention and referral principles, not current legal advice or instructions for relinquishing a child.

Applied example: Confidential pregnancy and parenting support can help a person seek help before a crisis escalates. Assistance with continued education or financial hardship can reduce specific pressures while protecting the child.

### BLK-6136-6.2.06-04: A practical access test
In a fictional support programme, a nearby trained adviser offers confidential appointments and explains referral options. This may help people seek assistance earlier, but consent, privacy and continued service capacity still matter. Prevention and support need complementary actions; telling people to seek help is insufficient if the route is unsafe, unavailable or unaffordable.

### BLK-6136-6.2.06-R01: Distinct prevention and support responses
Responses to GBV can include challenging norms that excuse abuse, improving equal access to income and education, teaching respectful relationships, providing safe reporting routes, and making professional protection and survivor support reachable. Public institutions, trained health and social workers, schools and community organisations have different responsibilities. Preventing baby dumping can include confidential pregnancy and parenting support, assistance to remain in education, reliable information on voluntary family planning, and help with financial or emotional crises. A response should remove a stated barrier and protect the infant; shame or exposure can deter help. These are educational support principles, not instructions for a learner to investigate violence or manage an emergency alone.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.2-010-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.2.03-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional participation arrangement better supports women’s influence. A broad open meeting can amend the budget but takes travel and care time. Elected local delegates can negotiate the same budget with funded access, but may not reflect every group. Both occur before decisions are final.
ANSWER: The open meeting offers direct voice and a chance to challenge priorities, but time and travel can exclude some participants. Delegation makes participation more practical for those facing barriers, while concentrating influence in representatives. Prefer delegates where they consult diverse groups, report back and can be held accountable; otherwise a supported open meeting may be more representative. Presence or an invitation alone is weaker evidence than a usable opportunity to change decisions.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.2-018-WHY

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.2-020-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.2.05-01"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how unequal power can contribute to gender-based violence.
ANSWER: A person claiming control over another’s choices may use threats or force, especially where norms excuse that behaviour. Unequal power can enable abuse, but responsibility remains with the person committing it.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: explain a control, threat or coercion pathway associated with unequal power.", "Consequence, 1 mark: retain responsibility with the perpetrator rather than blaming the person harmed.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.2-021-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.2.05-02"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how fear of losing education can contribute to a crisis around baby dumping.
ANSWER: A pregnant learner who expects exclusion or cannot reach support may feel trapped and isolated. Continued-education and confidential qualified support can address that pressure; it does not make harmful abandonment inevitable or excuse it.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: link feared educational exclusion or unavailable support to isolation and crisis.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain an appropriate education/support response without treating harmful abandonment as inevitable or excusable.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]
