### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.2-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.2.01-00", "OBJ-6136-6.2.03-03"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) the distinction between formal equality and factual inequality [2]. Explain (b) why equal admission cannot establish equal training access when a fictional programme’s otherwise eligible participants lack transport and cannot attend at its scheduled time [4].
ANSWER: (a) Equal rules differ from unequal lived access or outcomes. (b) Equal admission removes a formal rule excluding otherwise eligible participants. It does not provide an affordable journey to the training site, so people without transport may still be absent. A timetable that participants cannot attend creates a separate barrier even if a journey becomes affordable. Actual access therefore needs suitable travel and scheduling arrangements, with attendance and completion checked across groups; equal written rules alone do not establish equal opportunities in practice.
GUIDANCE: ["Point: rule-versus-lived-access distinction [2].", "Development: connect both actual barriers to limited participation [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-6.2-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-6.2.02-00", "OBJ-6136-6.2.04-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Calculate (a) women’s participation if 45 of 75 eligible women attend an invented project [2]. Explain (b) whether that result alone shows effective participation when only 6 attendees can influence spending and the rest receive no decision information [4].
ANSWER: (a) 45 ÷ 75 = 0.6; multiplying by 100 gives 60%. (b) Attendance covers a majority of those eligible, but the six-person decision group and missing information limit evidence of meaningful influence. Investigate decision access rather than equating 60% attendance with equal control.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: correct denominator and 60% [2].", "Meaning: combine attendance with information and decision-role evidence for a bounded conclusion [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 7.1: Population

## UNIT CU-6136-7.1.01: Density and distribution

### BLK-6136-7.1.01-01: Density and distribution
Population density measures people per unit area; distribution describes where people live and how they are spread. A mean density can conceal concentrations. Density equals population divided by area, using comparable dates and boundaries.

### BLK-6136-7.1.01-02: Population density
Population density is the number of people per unit area, commonly people per square kilometre. A fictional 12,000 residents over 300 km² gives 12,000 ÷ 300 = 40 people per km²; it does not mean each square kilometre contains 40 people.

### BLK-6136-7.1.01-03: Population distribution
Population distribution is the spatial pattern showing where people live and how they are spread across an area. Describe clusters, sparse areas and relationships to locations using map evidence. A number per square kilometre does not describe the full spatial pattern.

### BLK-6136-7.1.01-04: Apply the measure
A fictional district with 18,000 residents across 600 square kilometres has a mean density of 30 people per square kilometre. That calculation says nothing about whether the residents cluster around one town or live in scattered villages. A settlement map supplies distribution evidence, while journey information helps judge actual access to services.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.1.02: Explain settlement patterns

### BLK-6136-7.1.02-01: Explain settlement patterns
Climate, relief, economic opportunities, services and political conditions can influence settlement. Southern African settlement is uneven; water and suitable terrain can support concentration, while jobs and infrastructure may attract people even where physical conditions are difficult. One factor seldom explains the whole pattern.

### BLK-6136-7.1.02-02: Climate and relief
Water availability, temperature and terrain affect the feasibility of settlement and livelihoods. Gentler accessible land with usable water may support farming and building more readily than a dry steep area; infrastructure can modify some constraints.

### BLK-6136-7.1.02-03: Economic factors
Jobs, markets and transport can draw people towards productive opportunities. A viable workplace near a usable route can encourage settlement; a mineral deposit without access or work opportunities need not attract a large population.

### BLK-6136-7.1.02-04: Social factors
Schools, health facilities, family networks and other services can influence where people live. A service concentration may improve access, but housing and travel costs affect whether households can use it. Availability is different from equal access.

### BLK-6136-7.1.02-05: Political factors
Safety, displacement, boundaries and public settlement decisions can affect population distribution. Conflict can force movement regardless of a place’s productive potential; safer conditions and usable rights can support return. Do not assume movements are freely chosen.

### BLK-6136-7.1.02-06: Apply the measure
On an invented settlement map, six of nine settlements are near a railway and its service centres; three lie farther away. This is a concentration associated with transport and service access. It does not prove why each settlement formed. Historical activity, available water and land conditions may also help explain the pattern and should be investigated separately.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.1.03: Growth and rates

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-01: Growth and rates
Population changes through births, deaths and net migration; comparable rates allow populations of different sizes to be assessed. Natural increase excludes migration. Higher population level differs from faster increase: compare differences or rates over equal intervals, not the largest final value alone.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-02: Growth rate
Population growth rate measures change in population over a period relative to its starting population, expressed as a percentage for that period. Use total change including migration where appropriate. A growth rate can fall while population still rises if the rate remains positive.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-03: Crude birth rate
Crude birth rate is live births during a year per thousand people in the population. Use births divided by the relevant population, multiplied by 1,000. It differs from births per woman and from a total number of births.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-04: Death rate
The crude death rate measures deaths during a year per thousand people in the population. A crude rate is influenced by age structure as well as health conditions, so a higher rate does not by itself prove worse services.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-05: Infant mortality
Infant mortality rate is deaths before the first birthday per thousand live births, for a stated period. Its denominator is live births, not total population; compare definitions and periods before interpreting a difference.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-06: Demographic transition model
The demographic transition model simplifies changes in birth and death rates and consequent natural population growth during development. The broad four-stage account moves from high birth/death rates, through falling deaths then falling births, towards low rates. It is a model, not an identical timetable for every country; migration is not its central mechanism.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-07: Apply the measure
Two invented populations change over the same five-year interval: C rises from 200,000 to 210,000, while D rises from 50,000 to 60,000. Both gain 10,000 people, but the percentage increases are 5% and 20%. Comparing size, absolute increase and percentage growth answers different questions; state which measure the judgement uses.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-R01: Why birth and growth rates differ
Birth rates can remain high where children contribute to household livelihoods, families expect support in older age, early marriage lengthens the childbearing period, or high infant mortality leads parents to expect fewer surviving children. Limited access to education and voluntary family-planning information can restrict choices. Birth rates may fall with later marriage, better child survival, women’s education and wider work opportunities, accessible voluntary services, and the costs of raising children. These are interacting explanations, not stereotypes about a country or predictions about individuals. Death rates may fall as water, nutrition, sanitation and care improve. When deaths fall before births, natural increase rises; when births later fall towards deaths, growth slows. Net migration can still change total population growth, so natural increase is not the whole growth rate.

### BLK-6136-7.1.03-R02: Calculate natural increase and keep the units
Crude birth and death rates use events per 1,000 people per year. Natural increase is the birth rate minus the death rate, using the same population basis and period. In a fictional population, 30 births per 1,000 minus 10 deaths per 1,000 gives 20 per 1,000 natural increase. Divide 20 by 10 to express that as 2.0% per year. The symbol ‰ means per thousand; % means per hundred. Natural increase omits immigration and emigration. A growing population with positive natural increase can grow more slowly if that positive rate falls.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.1.04: Population policy

### BLK-6136-7.1.04-01: Population policy
Policies may seek slower or faster growth through services, incentives or restrictions, but need evaluation against well-being and rights. A numerical target alone does not establish a beneficial outcome. Age structure, choices, implementation and long-term effects matter; historical examples must not be presented as current law.

### BLK-6136-7.1.04-02: Reducing growth: a historical example
China’s historical one-child policy illustrates restrictions intended to reduce births; UNFPA recorded the move towards a two-child policy in 2015. Restrictions can affect births but create rights and family-welfare concerns. Evaluate voluntary information and services alongside any growth aim rather than endorsing coercion or assuming policy alone explains fertility.

### BLK-6136-7.1.04-03: Increasing growth: a historical example
Singapore moved in 1987 from its earlier two-child approach towards encouraging larger families, as recorded by its National Library. Support with childcare, housing or parenting costs can address some barriers, but preferences, work and wider conditions mean an incentive need not produce the intended birth increase.

### BLK-6136-7.1.04-04: Apply the measure
A fictional population programme combines confidential voluntary services with measures that keep learners in education. The service can address an immediate information or access need, while education may widen longer-term choices. Their effects occur through different pathways and need evidence over suitable periods. Respect for rights and genuine access are conditions of the programme, not optional costs to be traded away.

### BLK-6136-7.1.04-05: Keep policy examples dated
[UNFPA’s 2015 statement](https://asiapacific.unfpa.org/en/news/statement-chinas-population-policy-changes) records China moving away from its earlier restriction. [Singapore’s National Library](https://www.nlb.gov.sg/main/article-detail?cmsuuid=1d106f7e-aca1-4c0e-ac7a-d35d0772707d) dates its pronatalist change to 1987. These illustrate differing policy aims; neither describes current rules. Checked 20 October 2026.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.1.05: Use population data

### BLK-6136-7.1.05-01: Use population data
Population data can guide the location and capacity of services, employment planning and infrastructure. For a Namibian planning application, compare district age groups and access to schools or clinics rather than distributing capacity solely by national totals. Use the date and local denominators.

### BLK-6136-7.1.05-02: Economic uses
Age and location data can inform workforce training, transport demand and markets. Counts of working-age residents do not directly measure available skills or job seekers; combine population structure with relevant labour information.

Applied example: Age and working-age numbers help plan future training and labour needs. The size and location of households help estimate demand for goods and services.

### BLK-6136-7.1.05-03: Social uses
Children’s and older residents’ counts can inform school, health and care planning. Needs depend on location and actual access as well as age. A distant facility may offer less usable capacity than its place count suggests.

Applied example: Child population estimates help plan school places. The number and location of older residents help plan accessible health and support services.

### BLK-6136-7.1.05-04: Apply the measure
A fictional district expects 360 children to need primary places and currently has 240 usable places, a gap of 120. A neighbouring district expects 180 children and has 200 places. The first has the larger capacity gap, but staff, classroom condition and journey distances still matter. A total population count alone would not reveal this age-specific need.

### BLK-6136-7.1.05-R01: Match data to a planning decision
Age and working-age numbers help estimate future training and labour needs. Household totals and locations help judge demand for goods and services. Child numbers support planning for school places; the location and number of older people support planning for accessible care. Use a relevant area and period and check denominators: a count of people can grow even when their percentage share falls.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.1.06: Population pyramids

### BLK-6136-7.1.06-01: Population pyramids
A population pyramid shows age and sex composition, with age groups vertically and horizontal bars for each sex on a consistent count or percentage scale. A broad base indicates a relatively large young group; a narrower base and substantial older groups suggest a different age structure. Shape alone does not prove causes or future certainty.

### BLK-6136-7.1.06-02: Older age structures
Many developed economies have relatively narrower young bases and larger older shares than youthful populations. These tendencies can inform care and workforce questions but do not describe every country or person. Compare the same age bands and units.

Applied example: A larger older-age share can increase demand for pensions and accessible care. A smaller working-age share can face greater support responsibilities relative to its size.

### BLK-6136-7.1.06-03: Youthful age structures
A youthful pyramid has a relatively broad young base compared with older age groups. A large school-age share can imply future education demand, while future births also depend on survival, migration and fertility choices; do not infer inevitable rapid growth from shape alone.

Applied example: Many children increase present demand for schools and child services. As those cohorts reach working age, more employment and further training opportunities may be needed.

### BLK-6136-7.1.06-04: Apply the measure
This fictional worked example uses percentages of each population’s total, not percentages within each sex. Population C has 36% aged 0–14, 56% aged 15–64 and 8% aged 65 or over. Population D has 24%, 58% and 18% in the same bands. C has the larger young share; D has the larger older share. These differences can inform school and older-age service planning, without implying that everyone in either age group has identical needs.

### BLK-6136-7.1.06-05: Read and construct the shape
The complete age-sex table for this worked example is: C, 0–14 male 18% female 18%, 15–64 male 28% female 28%, 65+ male 4% female 4%; D, 0–14 male 12% female 12%, 15–64 male 29% female 29%, 65+ male 9% female 9%. Each population totals 100%. Draw age groups vertically, youngest at the bottom, with males left and females right on equal horizontal percentage scales. C’s bar lengths on each side are 18%, 28% and 4%; D’s are 12%, 29% and 9%, in the same age order. Add a title, age labels, units and sex key. These broad age groups show grouped shares rather than exact year-by-year patterns. Use finer equal-width age bands for detailed age comparisons. No unseen plotted image is claimed verified.

### BLK-6136-7.1.06-R01: Explain a pyramid before planning services
A broad base indicates a large young share, commonly associated with relatively high recent births; it does not prove one cause. A youthful population can increase demand for schools, child services and later jobs. A higher older-age share can increase demand for accessible health care, pensions and support services, while a smaller working-age share may face greater support responsibilities. Numbers matter as well as percentages: the same age share in a larger population means more people. Check cohort size, migration and changing births or survival before using a pyramid to explain a trend.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.1.07: HIV, AIDS and population

### BLK-6136-7.1.07-01: HIV, AIDS and population
HIV is a virus affecting the immune system; AIDS is an advanced stage of untreated HIV infection. Effective treatment and accessible support change health outcomes. Illness and premature deaths can affect household care, age patterns and livelihoods, but do not assume HIV means immediate death or inability to work. Health and population judgements need evidence and non-stigmatising language.

### BLK-6136-7.1.07-02: Apply the measure
In a fictional service review, continuity of HIV care improves while reported illness-related absence declines. This association can inform investigation of treatment access, but changes in recording or workplace conditions may also matter. Population and employment effects require consistent denominators and dates. People living with HIV should not be treated as a uniform group or excluded on assumptions about their capability.

### BLK-6136-7.1.07-03: Health evidence
[WHO’s HIV fact sheet](https://www.who.int/en/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/hiv-aids), checked 20 October 2026, distinguishes HIV from AIDS and explains the role of effective treatment. Ordinary social contact does not transmit HIV. This is conceptual health education, not an individual diagnosis or treatment instruction.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-005-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.02-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-012-INTERP

PROMPT: Explain why choosing the final population of 310 thousand does not identify the largest increase when another place rises from 100 to 120 thousand and this place from 300 to 310 thousand.
ANSWER: The largest level is 310, but the increases are 20 and 10 thousand respectively. Test change across the interval rather than selecting the highest final value.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: highest level substitutes for largest increase.", "Distinction: level versus difference.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-013-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.03-01"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Define growth rate.
ANSWER: Population growth rate measures change in population over a period relative to its starting population, expressed as a percentage for that period.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: Population growth rate measures change in population over a period relative to its starting population, expressed as a percentage for that period.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-014-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.03-02"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Define crude birth rate.
ANSWER: Crude birth rate is live births during a year per thousand people in the population.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: Crude birth rate is live births during a year per thousand people in the population.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-015-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.03-03"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Define death rate.
ANSWER: The crude death rate measures deaths during a year per thousand people in the population.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: The crude death rate measures deaths during a year per thousand people in the population.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-016-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.03-04"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}

ANSWER: Infant mortality rate is deaths before the first birthday per thousand live births, for a stated period.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-017-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.03-05"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-018-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.04-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional voluntary population policy better balances slower growth and well-being. A funds wider information and confidential services. B combines a smaller service expansion with girls’ continued education. Both respect family choice and have the same budget, but their effects may take different times.
ANSWER: A directly reduces an information and service-access barrier, potentially helping people act on their existing choices sooner. B addresses education and longer-term opportunities as well as access, but serves fewer people immediately. Prefer A where unmet service demand is the main current constraint; B may bring broader lasting benefits where school exclusion restricts choices. Assess access, well-being and later outcomes together, without assuming either policy determines private fertility decisions.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-019-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.04-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a planning response where a fictional district has many young children and too few primary places, while funding can expand local provision.
ANSWER: Use the age and location data to expand accessible primary places and staff them. This addresses present education demand rather than assuming a population target supplies services.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: child numbers, missing places and available funds.", "Reason: match capacity to actual need.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-020-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.04-01"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain why a historical restrictive birth policy cannot be evaluated from fewer births alone when the evidence also records pressure on family choice.
ANSWER: Fewer births may fit its numerical aim, but constrained choice is a material welfare and rights cost. Compare voluntary alternatives and longer-term age effects; do not attribute the entire fertility trend to one policy.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: bounded evaluation against well-being.", "Reason: weigh rate aim with choice and alternatives.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-021-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.04-02"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional pronatalist support better fits parents’ reported needs. A provides a larger childcare subsidy, reducing cost more. B provides a smaller subsidy and workable time support. Both are affordable for the state; parents report both childcare costs and inflexible work hours.
ANSWER: A makes a larger contribution to childcare affordability and may best help families whose main obstacle is cost. B addresses two different barriers, so it better fits families for whom work hours still block usable care. Prefer B when the timetable barrier is substantial; A can be stronger when cost dominates and hours can already be managed. Compare actual participation and family well-being, and do not promise either choice will raise births.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

PROMPT: Describe the population-pyramid pattern represented by this original age-sex table: A, 0–14 male 20% female 20%, 15–64 male 27% female 27%, 65+ male 3% female 3%; B, 0–14 male 10% female 10%, 15–64 male 30% female 30%, 65+ male 10% female 10%.
ANSWER: A has the broader young base, 40% versus 20%, while B has the larger older share, 20% versus 6%. The age structures suggest different education and care demands, without proving causes.

ANSWER: 180 + 220 = 400; 400 ÷ 1,000 = 0.4; multiplying by 100 gives 40%.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-030-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.07-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional programme better supports households affected by HIV-related illness. A funds transport and continuity of professional care for more households. B reaches fewer but also provides care-time and livelihood support. Both protect confidentiality and workplace inclusion. The budget covers one programme initially.
ANSWER: A offers wider health-service access and can reduce interruptions to care, helping more households initially. B tackles the linked loss of income and unpaid caring time more fully, but has narrower reach. Prefer B for households facing combined care and livelihood pressures, while A may be stronger where travel is the dominant barrier across many households. Use evidence on needs and reach to justify the choice; HIV status alone does not establish incapacity.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-031-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.07-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe this fictional service series: access to ongoing HIV care rises from 50% to 80% of an eligible group while recorded missed-work days fall from 200 to 120 under the stated definitions.
ANSWER: Wider care access accompanies 80 fewer missed days and a 30-percentage-point coverage gain. The association supports investigating access and participation, but does not establish treatment caused the whole work change.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: accurate paired changes.", "Relationship: association, with causal limits.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.01-00", "OBJ-6136-7.1.05-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) the meanings of density and distribution [2]. Explain (b) why two fictional districts with the same mean density need different clinic locations when one has a single settlement cluster and the other dispersed villages [4].
ANSWER: (a) People per unit area versus spatial arrangement. (b) Mean density divides each district’s population by its area, so equal means can conceal different settlement patterns. A clinic near a single cluster may be within reach of many residents in the first district. In the dispersed district, the same central-site approach can leave small villages with long journeys even though average density is identical. Locate services using settlement positions and travel evidence; distributed visits or outreach may complement fixed clinics where resources and safe access allow.
GUIDANCE: ["Point: paired meanings [2].", "Development: connect equal average and contrasting locations to usable service provision [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.1.01-00", "OBJ-6136-7.1.05-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Calculate (a) density for fictional A with 9,000 people in 300 km² [2]. Explain (b) whether that figure alone justifies one central school when 70% of children live in dispersed distant villages and no transport is available [4].
ANSWER: (a) 9,000 ÷ 300 = 30 people per km². (b) No: the mean does not show the children’s dispersed locations, and absent transport restricts central access. Investigate local or transport options rather than allocate solely by density.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: method and people per km² [2].", "Meaning: use distribution and transport to justify the bounded access decision [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 7.2: Health

## UNIT CU-6136-7.2.01: Conditions for health

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-01: Conditions for health
Clean water, hygiene, an adequate diet, prevention and treatment of disease, and social conditions can influence health and life expectancy. Life expectancy is an average under the mortality conditions used, not an individual promise. Poverty can restrict food, housing and service access; stress and illness can interact with those constraints.

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-02: Water and hygiene
Safe water and appropriate hygiene reduce exposure to some contamination pathways. A supply needs reliable access and protection after collection; a new water point does not help equally if distance, breakdown or unsafe handling prevents use.

Applied example: It can reduce exposure to contaminated supplies and reduce time lost obtaining water. With fewer interruptions to health and available time, people may attend school or work more consistently.

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-03: Adequate diet
A nutritionally adequate diet provides suitable energy and nutrients for health. Availability, affordability and varied intake matter. More food by weight does not necessarily establish adequate nutrition, and a household label does not diagnose nutritional status.

Applied example: People need suitable nutrients as well as enough energy. A household may obtain a large amount of one food while lacking the variety needed for an adequate diet.

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-04: Disease prevention and care
Prevention and effective accessible care can reduce illness and its consequences. Facilities, trained staff, supplies and timely referral must work together. A building without operating support does not establish useful health provision.

Applied example: A building provides space, but qualified workers deliver and refer care. Without operating staff, the extra physical facility need not provide usable treatment.

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-05: Poverty and stress
Material hardship can limit adequate food, housing and service access, while insecurity and stress can affect well-being. Illness can also reduce earnings and add care costs. Explain interacting pathways without assuming poverty establishes an individual’s health or assigning blame.

Applied example: Limited income can restrict food and access to care. Illness can then interrupt earnings and add care costs, making those material constraints harder to overcome.

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-06: Follow the mechanism
In an invented settlement, a reliable protected water supply is paired with maintained toilets and handwashing facilities. These measures can reduce exposure to some water-borne infections through different routes. They do not prevent every disease: malaria, for example, has a mosquito transmission route. Match the prevention measure to the route and check whether the facilities remain usable.

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-R01: Disease, prevention and productive participation
Illness can interrupt attendance at school and work, add caring time and absorb household income. Malaria is caused by a parasite transmitted through bites of infected mosquitoes; it is not caused by drinking dirty water. Safe water, sanitation and hygiene instead reduce exposure to infections associated with faecal contamination, including cholera. Mosquito control and reducing bites address a different transmission route. Match prevention to the route rather than presenting one measure as protection against all diseases. Immunisation uses vaccines to develop protection against particular infections; it is not treatment for every existing illness. Programmes need trained personnel, dependable supplies, suitable storage and reachable services. This is a development explanation, not personal diagnosis or a vaccination schedule.

### BLK-6136-7.2.01-R02: Worked percentage change
Percentage change compares a change with the starting value. Subtract the old value from the new value, divide that difference by the old value, then multiply by 100. If attendance rises from 80 to 100 users, the increase is 20 users; 20 divided by 80 is 0.25, giving 25% growth. If it falls from 100 to 80, the change is −20 users; dividing by the original 100 gives −0.2, or a 20% fall. The different denominators explain the different percentages. Percentage points instead subtract two percentages, such as coverage rising from 40% to 55%, a gain of 15 points. Starting from zero makes this percentage-change division undefined; report the absolute change.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.2.02: Evaluate health programmes

### BLK-6136-7.2.02-01: Evaluate health programmes
Primary health care links accessible care, prevention and community needs; hospitals, trained doctors and supporting workers provide complementary capabilities. Trained auxiliaries need suitable roles, supervision and referral support; immunisation, community programmes and safe-water provision address different needs. Compare accessible coverage, quality, staffing, maintenance and cost rather than simply counting buildings.

### BLK-6136-7.2.02-02: Follow the mechanism
A fictional health budget can expand a staffed mobile service or equip a fixed centre for more specialised care. Outreach can reduce journeys for routine needs; a fixed centre may handle cases needing equipment that cannot travel. Neither replaces an effective referral route. Priorities depend on the pattern of need, coverage and operating capacity, with benefits and limitations weighed for both choices.

### BLK-6136-7.2.02-03: Programme evidence and limits
[WHO’s primary-health-care overview](https://www.who.int/health-topics/primary-health-care) describes community-centred services and broader health conditions. Its [2024 Namibia account](https://www.afro.who.int/news/namibia-reaches-key-milestone-eliminating-mother-child-transmission-hiv-and-hepatitis-b) reports integration of maternal-child and primary health services and progress on reducing vertical transmission. These show linked programmes, not proof that every household is served. Checked 20 October 2026; exact clinical procedures and schedules are outside this lesson.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.2.03: HIV and development

### BLK-6136-7.2.03-01: HIV and development
HIV-related illness can affect work, household care and public resources in Namibia and other SADC economies, while effective care improves opportunities to remain well and participate. Accessible testing, treatment and support form part of a public-health response. Avoid treating HIV status as incapacity or using a fictional illness series as actual national prevalence evidence.

### BLK-6136-7.2.03-02: Economic pathways
Illness or premature death can reduce available labour, increase caring work and divert household resources. Treatment access and support can reduce these burdens; stigma and exclusion can add avoidable economic costs independently of health status.

Applied example: Illness can reduce paid working hours and income. Caring responsibilities can reduce another household member’s time for work or education.

### BLK-6136-7.2.03-03: System responses
HIV responses can combine prevention information, confidential testing, ongoing treatment, maternal-child services and community support. Continuity, trained services and non-stigmatising access matter. Individual treatment decisions belong with qualified health professionals, not a classroom model answer.

Applied example: Confidential testing and continued professional care support timely access to services. Accurate education and community support can reduce misinformation and barriers to participation.

### BLK-6136-7.2.03-04: Follow the mechanism
A fictional household loses paid hours during a period of illness and spends savings on care and transport. A second adult’s caring responsibilities can reduce earnings further. Accessible professional services and income support may reduce some pressures, but the particular household’s circumstances matter. Do not infer an individual’s health or ability to work from an HIV diagnosis alone.

### BLK-6136-7.2.03-05: Use accurate HIV language
[WHO’s HIV fact sheet](https://www.who.int/en/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/hiv-aids), checked 20 October 2026, explains that treatment can control HIV and that ordinary contact such as sharing food does not transmit it. Do not use a diagnosis as a reason to exclude someone from schooling or work. This lesson does not prescribe individual treatment.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-001-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-002-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the access mechanism in an invented village with a safe water point that repeatedly breaks and households then collecting from an unprotected source.
ANSWER: Breakdowns remove reliable safe access and push collection towards a less protected source. Maintenance addresses the reliability link, rather than counting the installed point as continuous coverage.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: combine breakdown and alternative-source facts.", "Consequence: reduced usable safe access; no invented disease rate.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-00"], "subtype": "CALC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 2}

ANSWER: 100 - 80 = 20; 20 ÷ 80 = 0.25; multiplying by 100 gives a 25% increase.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-014-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why a rise from 80 to 100 users is not a 20% increase.
ANSWER: Twenty is the absolute difference. Divide it by the original 80 and multiply by 100 to obtain 25%; test whether the original-value denominator has been used.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: absolute difference treated as percentage change.", "Distinction: count increase versus relative increase.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-004-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-01"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how reliable safe-water access can support productive participation.
ANSWER: It can reduce exposure to contaminated supplies and reduce time lost obtaining water. With fewer interruptions to health and available time, people may attend school or work more consistently.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify reduced contamination exposure or less time obtaining water.", "Consequence, 1 mark: link that improvement to potential school/work participation rather than guaranteed health.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-005-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-02"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why food availability alone does not establish an adequate diet.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-006-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-03"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why a health programme needs trained staff as well as a clinic building.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-007-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-04"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how illness and poverty can reinforce each other.
ANSWER: Limited income can restrict food and access to care. Illness can then interrupt earnings and add care costs, making those material constraints harder to overcome.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: link low income to restricted food or care access.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain the reverse pathway from illness to lost earnings or added care costs.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-008-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.02-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional health programme better fits remote communities. A funds outreach for routine care and referrals for 80% of residents. B funds a reachable district hospital and transport serving 55%, with stronger specialist capacity. Both have staff and supplies; only one can be expanded first.
ANSWER: Outreach reaches more residents and can support early and preventive care, but relies on referral for specialist needs. The hospital offers more complex care and transport, but covers fewer people in the current plan. Prefer outreach where unmet routine needs dominate; prioritise hospital capacity where the evidence shows serious referral bottlenecks. The strongest long-term arrangement connects the two. A building count or one coverage figure cannot establish overall quality and need.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-010-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.03-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the economic pathway in an invented household where illness interrupts paid work and another member reduces hours to provide care, while savings are limited.
ANSWER: Lost paid hours reduce income while care limits a second earner’s time. Limited savings make the combined loss harder to absorb; continued health and social support could address the burden without excluding affected people.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-011-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.03-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional HIV-support programme better fits continued participation. A reaches more people with confidential care and transport. B reaches fewer people but also funds household care-time support. Both preserve employment access for people able to work, and only one fits the initial budget.
ANSWER: A removes the travel barrier for a wider group and supports continuity of care. B addresses care burdens as well, potentially protecting both the person’s and another household member’s participation, but helps fewer households. Prefer B where care-time losses are the most serious unmet need, or A where transport excludes many people from effective services. Judge reach and the depth of support against actual needs rather than choosing exclusion as a false alternative.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-00", "OBJ-6136-7.2.02-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) two supports needed to turn a clinic building into usable care [2]. Explain (b) a fictional programme where a new clinic is built but no staff or medicine supply is funded and travel remains unaffordable [4].
ANSWER: (a) Trained staff and dependable supplies. (b) The new building supplies space but cannot examine or treat patients without trained staff. Without a funded medicine supply, it also cannot provide treatments that depend on those medicines. Unaffordable travel can prevent residents reaching the clinic even after staffing and supplies improve. The programme therefore needs an operating budget and practical access arrangements alongside construction; assess care actually delivered and who can use it rather than counting buildings alone.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.2-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.2.01-00", "OBJ-6136-7.2.02-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Calculate (a) usable service coverage if 180 of 300 eligible residents can reach staffed care in a fictional district [2]. Explain (b) whether a second unstaffed building alone solves the gap when the unserved residents also lack transport [4].
ANSWER: (a) 180 ÷ 300 = 0.6; multiplying by 100 gives 60%. (b) Forty percent remain uncovered, but an unstaffed building adds no operating care and does not remove transport barriers. Prioritise feasible staffing and access arrangements rather than building counts alone.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: correct denominator and 60% [2].", "Meaning: combine uncovered share, absent staff and transport to justify the practical priority [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 7.3: Education

## UNIT CU-6136-7.3.01: Two classifications

### BLK-6136-7.3.01-01: Two classifications
Traditional and modern describe broad educational knowledge and institutions, whereas formal, informal and non-formal describe how learning is organised. They are different axes. Traditional knowledge can be taught in a formal course, and contemporary digital skills can be learned informally.

### BLK-6136-7.3.01-02: Traditional education
Traditional education is learning rooted in a community’s inherited knowledge, practices and values; this classification differs from formal versus non-formal provision. Learning through demonstration and participation can transmit relevant knowledge. Practices vary and adapt; traditional does not mean unchanged or without reasoning.

### BLK-6136-7.3.01-03: Modern education
Modern education is education shaped by contemporary institutions and knowledge, including formal systems; distinguish this broad contrast with traditional education from the separate formal/informal/non-formal classification. It can extend access to scientific and technical knowledge but needs locally usable resources and inclusive provision.

### BLK-6136-7.3.01-04: Apply the distinction
A recognised school course can teach inherited farming knowledge, so traditional content does not automatically make the organisation non-formal. Conversely, a community-run literacy course can be planned and systematic while operating outside the main formal system. Distinguish what is taught from how learning is organised, and apply each classification to the stated facts.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.3.02: Compare strengths and limits

### BLK-6136-7.3.02-01: Compare strengths and limits
Community-rooted learning can offer relevance and participation, while institutional education can offer broader curriculum and recognised qualifications. Either may exclude people or miss useful knowledge. Compare suitability for the learner’s goal, access and quality rather than assuming one wholly replaces the other.

### BLK-6136-7.3.02-02: Apply the distinction
In a fictional comparison, a community apprenticeship teaches local craft through participation, illustrating traditional education; it offers no formal qualification. A college course illustrates modern institutional education with technical theory and an assessed qualification, alongside affordable fees and reduced earning time. Community learning can suit local practical aims. College better fits a learner seeking recognised technical skills if the qualification is accepted and its costs are manageable. These are features of the stated examples, not fixed properties of all traditional or modern education.

### BLK-6136-7.3.02-R01: Distinct advantages and limitations
Traditional learning can pass on local knowledge, practise tasks through participation, strengthen intergenerational relationships and use accessible local resources. Its limits may include narrow exposure to unfamiliar technical knowledge, unequal access to some roles and a lack of widely recognised qualifications. Modern education can provide scientific knowledge, specialist facilities, recognised qualifications and contact with wider occupations. Fees, distance and language can restrict access; teaching may be poorly matched to local needs or overemphasise recall. Neither description fixes the quality of every programme. Compare relevance, access, practical learning, recognition and the ability to adapt, using separate points with an explanation of each contribution or limitation.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.3.03: Organisation of learning

### BLK-6136-7.3.03-01: Organisation of learning
Formal education operates within the recognised structured education system; non-formal education is organised outside its main system, while informal learning occurs through everyday experience. The distinction concerns organisation, not quality or whether teaching uses old or new knowledge. An adult course may be non-formal but carefully planned.

### BLK-6136-7.3.03-02: Formal education
Formal education is education organised within the recognised, structured education system, normally with planned stages and qualifications. A school course illustrates it. Whether its teaching is effective requires separate evidence about access, learning and support.

### BLK-6136-7.3.03-03: Informal education
Informal education is learning through everyday experience and interaction outside a planned education programme. Learning a household repair through daily interaction may illustrate it. Informal does not mean worthless or necessarily inaccurate.

### BLK-6136-7.3.03-04: Non-formal education
Non-formal education is organised learning outside the main formal education system, such as a planned adult skills programme. It can fit adult work schedules, but needs suitable teaching and resources. It differs from unplanned everyday learning.

### BLK-6136-7.3.03-05: Apply the distinction
A fictional adult literacy group meets at a community hall on two agreed mornings each week, uses a planned course and checks progress. It is organised learning outside the main school system, illustrating non-formal education. A conversation that happens to teach someone a new word is informal learning instead. A planned setting and purpose distinguish the two.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.3.04: Measure provision

### BLK-6136-7.3.04-01: Measure provision
Literacy, school enrolment and tertiary participation describe different aspects of educational provision. Use age group, denominator and period consistently. Enrolment is not attendance, completion or learning quality, and a larger count can reflect a larger eligible population.

### BLK-6136-7.3.04-02: Literacy
A literacy measure identifies reading and writing ability under the source’s stated definition and population. Compare the same age and measurement rules. A national average can hide group or regional gaps and does not measure every skill.

### BLK-6136-7.3.04-03: School enrolment
Primary and secondary enrolment counts show participation registered at those levels. Compare counts with eligible populations or capacity and keep levels separate. More registered learners do not establish regular attendance or adequate teaching.

### BLK-6136-7.3.04-04: Tertiary enrolment
Tertiary enrolment measures participation in post-secondary institutions under the source’s definition. A rising total can indicate expansion while its rate remains unchanged if the eligible population grows similarly. Access, course relevance and completion need separate evidence.

### BLK-6136-7.3.04-05: Apply the distinction
In an invented comparison, one district enrols 180 of 300 eligible children and another enrols 160 of 200. Their enrolment rates are 60% and 80%; the first has more enrolled children but a lower share of its eligible group. Enrolment also differs from regular attendance, completion and learning quality, which require separate measures.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.3.05: ICT and access

### BLK-6136-7.3.05-01: ICT and access
ICT can support learning materials, communication and flexible access, but requires usable devices, power, connectivity, skills and suitable content. It may widen opportunities or deepen a digital gap. Learning gains need evidence; distributing devices alone does not establish teaching or access.

### BLK-6136-7.3.05-02: Apply the distinction
An invented learning centre supplies offline audio lessons with charged players and a tutor trained to support their use. The equipment can widen access to materials, but lessons still need to suit the curriculum and learners’ language needs. A device count is an input measure; participation and learning provide better evidence of whether the arrangement is useful.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.3.06: Alternative routes

### BLK-6136-7.3.06-01: Alternative routes
Non-formal courses, vocational education and lifelong learning can provide different routes to skills and continued participation. Their development contribution depends on relevance, access and opportunities to use learning. A certificate alone cannot create demand for a skill.

### BLK-6136-7.3.06-02: Vocational education
Vocational education develops practical knowledge and skills relevant to particular occupations or work activities. It can fit a documented skill gap when tools, training quality and work opportunities align; a narrow course with no usable equipment or demand offers weaker evidence of benefit.

### BLK-6136-7.3.06-03: Lifelong learning
Lifelong learning continues across life through different formal, non-formal and informal opportunities. It can help people adapt to change but needs time, access and support. Adult responsibilities should inform the arrangement rather than be treated as lack of motivation.

### BLK-6136-7.3.06-04: Apply the distinction
A fictional tailoring course has trained instructors, appropriate machines and local employers willing to assess applicants. Practical work can develop useful skills, while classroom explanations help learners understand methods. Employment still depends on opportunities and the quality and recognition of the training. A vocational label alone does not guarantee either competence or a job.

## UNIT CU-6136-7.3.07: Education and HIV

### BLK-6136-7.3.07-01: Education and HIV
Accurate education can counter HIV misinformation, reduce stigma and help people understand prevention and qualified support routes. It should protect privacy and continued participation. Sharing classrooms, food or ordinary contact does not transmit HIV; no learner should be asked to disclose status in a class exercise.

### BLK-6136-7.3.07-02: Apply the distinction
An invented school introduces accurate HIV education and a confidential route for reporting discrimination. Explaining that ordinary classroom contact does not transmit HIV can address a false belief, while staff action is needed when exclusion continues. Information and supportive behaviour reinforce each other; learners should not be required to identify anyone’s health status.

### BLK-6136-7.3.07-03: Health information source
The [WHO HIV fact sheet](https://www.who.int/en/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/hiv-aids), checked 20 October 2026, supports the ordinary-contact distinction used here. Educational inclusion is not an individual clinical assessment, and classmates should not be asked to investigate or disclose health status.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-006-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.02-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Describe and evaluate", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Describe these fictional education routes and give a reasoned judgement for a learner seeking recognised technical skills. Here, a community apprenticeship illustrates traditional education through local craft learning and daily participation; it provides no formal qualification. A reachable college course illustrates modern institutional education through technical theory and an assessed qualification, but charges affordable fees and reduces earning time. Both are feasible.
ANSWER: Traditional community learning offers locally relevant practice without course fees, but in this case gives no formal qualification and may cover a narrower range of technical knowledge. Modern college education provides structured theory and an assessed qualification, but involves fees and less time for earning. College better fits the stated aim of recognised technical skills if its qualification is accepted and costs remain manageable. Community learning may better fit a different aim centred on local craft practice. Judge the stated routes rather than assume that all traditional or modern education has identical quality.
GUIDANCE: ["Description: identify the traditional/community and modern/institutional routes and their stated features.", "Judgement: weigh practical relevance and recognition against fees and time for earning, using the learner’s stated aim.", "Reason: develop a conditional conclusion; do not invent an allowance or recognised apprenticeship assessment.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-018-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.05-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional ICT programme better fits accessible learning. A equips more schools with shared devices, basic teacher training and power. B equips fewer schools with more teaching support, accessible materials and reliable connectivity. Both operate, and the same budget covers one.
ANSWER: A spreads a usable minimum of equipment and training more widely, but shared access may limit learning time. B offers richer teaching support and access arrangements, potentially helping learners with more varied needs, but covers fewer schools. Prefer B where unsupported learning needs are the main barrier, or A where basic access across many schools is the urgent priority. Monitor actual use and learning rather than device totals, and plan how the other group will later be reached.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-019-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.06-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how an alternative education route can support sustainable development.
ANSWER: Relevant accessible learning can build skills for productive work and resource decisions. People need opportunities and means to apply those skills; attendance or a certificate alone does not establish the benefit.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason: learning through usable capability to a development outcome.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-020-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.06-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional adult course better fits usable skills and work. A offers a broad recognised qualification, supervised practice and higher fees. B offers a shorter affordable repair course with confirmed local demand, but narrower progression. Both have equipment and teachers; learners have limited time.
ANSWER: A supports wider recognised progression and practical learning, but its cost and length can restrict completion. B meets a documented local skill need at lower time and cash cost, while leaving fewer alternatives if that demand changes. B is stronger for immediate repair opportunities and tight resources; A may suit learners able to invest in broader future options. Compare completion and actual use of skills, rather than assuming a certificate or a short course creates employment.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-021-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.06-02"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain whether a vocational repair course is suitable when fictional local workshops report vacancies but the course has no tools. A funded equipment partnership is feasible.
ANSWER: The skill aim matches demand, but tool access is needed for practical competence. The partnership can make the course viable if training and maintenance are provided; vacancies alone cannot replace practice.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-022-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.06-03"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which lifelong-learning arrangement better fits fictional shift workers. A offers regular daytime classes with immediate teacher feedback. B offers flexible supported distance sessions with equal standards, but needs learners to organise time and travel to monthly practical meetings. Both are usable for some workers.
ANSWER: A provides structured contact and quick feedback, useful for workers whose shifts allow attendance. B can fit changing schedules and preserve access for others, but self-organisation and monthly travel remain demands. Prefer B for workers regularly missing daytime sessions if the support and meeting arrangements are workable; A can serve those who benefit from fixed teaching. Compare participation and learning quality across shift patterns rather than declaring one timetable suitable for everyone.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-023-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.07-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain how accurate HIV education can reduce exclusion from learning.
ANSWER: Correcting misinformation about ordinary social contact can remove a false basis for excluding classmates. Privacy and safe qualified support routes support participation without asking learners to disclose status.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-024-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.07-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the access mechanism in a fictional school where learners believe sharing desks transmits HIV and affected classmates are being avoided.
ANSWER: The belief is incorrect, and avoidance adds an unnecessary participation barrier. Accurate information, respectful behaviour and private qualified support can address misinformation without exposing anyone’s status.
