### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.03-00", "OBJ-6136-7.3.06-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) the distinction between formal and non-formal education [2]. Explain (b) why a fictional evening skills programme with tools can be more usable to working adults than daytime-only provision, even if both have equal admission rules [4].
ANSWER: (a) Within the recognised structured system versus organised learning outside its main system. (b) Working adults may be unable to attend daytime lessons because attendance conflicts with their paid work. Evening provision removes that stated time conflict, though its actual travel and safety arrangements still need checking. Available tools allow learners to practise the skills rather than receive theory alone. Equal admission rules do not supply either usable time or practical equipment, so judge effective access and learning opportunities from these delivery conditions as well as formal eligibility.
GUIDANCE: ["Point: organisational distinction [2].", "Development: use both timing and tools to explain access and skill development [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-7.3-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-7.3.04-00", "OBJ-6136-7.3.04-02"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Calculate (a) enrolment coverage for fictional A with 360 enrolled among 600 eligible [2]. Explain (b) whether A offers broader access than B with 300 among 400, and whether rates alone prove teaching quality [4].
ANSWER: (a) 360 ÷ 600 = 0.6; multiplying by 100 gives 60%. (b) B has 75% coverage despite fewer learners, so A’s larger count does not show broader registration. Neither rate establishes attendance, learning or teaching quality, which need separate evidence.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: correct denominator and 60% [2].", "Meaning: compare B’s 75% and bound the quality inference [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 8.1: Politics and Development

## UNIT CU-6136-8.1.01: State and government

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-01: State and government
A state is an enduring political organisation with territory, population and governing institutions; a government exercises public power through those institutions. Rules, representation and accountability influence whose needs affect public decisions. A change of government does not necessarily create a different state.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-02: Constitution
A constitution is the fundamental rules establishing how a state is organised, how public power is exercised and the rights recognised. It differs from an ordinary policy and requires implementation and accountability to influence lived access.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-03: Authority
Authority is recognised legitimacy to make decisions or exercise power within a specified role. An authorised officer can have limited resources; formal authority differs from the practical capacity to influence outcomes.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-04: Power
Power is the capacity to influence actions or outcomes; it can exist without recognised legitimate authority. A person may exert influence through resources or pressure without holding the authorised role.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-05: Representation
Representation is acting or speaking on behalf of others in decision-making. Meaningful representation needs communication and accountability to those represented, rather than presence alone.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-06: Delegation
Delegation assigns a task or decision responsibility to another person or body within defined limits. Clear limits, resources and reporting matter; assigning work without means to perform it may fail.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-07: Election
An election is a process for choosing representatives or office holders through voting. Fair choice also depends on accessible participation, information and credible procedures.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-08: Voting
Voting is expressing a choice in an election or collective decision under its rules. A vote records a choice, while representation concerns subsequent decisions on others’ behalf.

### BLK-6136-8.1.01-09: Apply the distinction
An elected fictional committee publishes its budget, records reasons for major decisions and allows residents to question spending. Elections give members a mandate, while reporting and scrutiny help make their exercise of power accountable between elections. Representation does not remove the need to explain decisions or provide a route for concerns.

## UNIT CU-6136-8.1.02: Types can overlap

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-01: Types can overlap
Government types describe different features such as head of state, accountability and concentration of power. A republic can be democratic or authoritarian; a constitutional monarchy may coexist with parliamentary democracy. Do not treat the labels as mutually exclusive.

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-02: Monarchy
A monarchy has a monarch as head of state, commonly through hereditary succession. The monarch’s powers vary; the title alone does not establish absolute control.

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-03: Parliamentary democracy
Parliamentary democracy bases democratic government on an elected parliament to which the executive is accountable. Voting, scrutiny and rights need effective practice; the name alone does not establish equal influence.

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-04: Republic
A republic has a non-hereditary public office as head of state rather than a monarch. This does not by itself establish free elections or limits on executive power.

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-05: One-party state
A one-party state permits political rule through a single authorised party without genuine competitive party alternation. Restricted competition limits voters’ alternatives; compare actual accountability and participation.

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-06: Dictatorship
A dictatorship concentrates political power with weak or absent effective democratic checks and competition. It differs from authority exercised within accountable limits; announced efficiency does not establish development benefit.

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-07: Totalitarianism
Totalitarianism seeks pervasive control over political and wider social life, often through a dominant ideology and coercive institutions. It describes an extent of control beyond the mere presence of a strong executive; avoid inferring it from one policy.

### BLK-6136-8.1.02-08: Apply the distinction
A fictional state can have a hereditary head of state while elected representatives make ordinary laws under a constitution. The succession arrangement describes monarchy; it does not by itself tell us how much democratic control citizens exercise. Classification needs separate evidence about selection of rulers, limits on power and public participation.

## UNIT CU-6136-8.1.03: Ownership and ideology

### BLK-6136-8.1.03-01: Ownership and ideology
Capitalism, socialism, mixed economies and communism concern productive ownership and coordination in differing ways; fascism also concerns authoritarian political ideology. Economic and political classifications are separate. Private enterprise can coexist with public services and regulation.

### BLK-6136-8.1.03-02: Capitalism
Capitalism emphasises private ownership of productive resources and market allocation. Regulation and public activity can still exist; the label does not imply no state role.

### BLK-6136-8.1.03-03: Socialism
Socialism emphasises social ownership or control and collective provision or distribution aims, in varied forms. It is not a single institutional arrangement or a synonym for any public service.

### BLK-6136-8.1.03-04: Mixed economy
A mixed economy combines private and public economic activity and different roles for markets and public decisions. The balance varies; a state-owned provider alongside private producers distinguishes it from an exclusively private arrangement.

### BLK-6136-8.1.03-05: Communism
Communism as an economic ideal involves common ownership of productive resources and a classless society. Historically labelled communist states have generally used extensive state ownership and planning; the ideal and actual institutions should be distinguished.

### BLK-6136-8.1.03-06: Fascism
Fascism is an authoritarian ultranationalist ideology emphasising concentrated leadership, suppression of opposition and subordinating individuals to the state or nation. It is not merely another word for government intervention or a mixed economy.

### BLK-6136-8.1.03-07: Apply the distinction
In an invented economy, privately owned farms sell produce while a publicly owned enterprise operates electricity networks. Ownership is mixed across activities. This differs from a claim that all production is privately owned, and says nothing by itself about service quality or the distribution of benefits. Use the actual ownership arrangements before judging outcomes.

## UNIT CU-6136-8.1.04: Public conditions for development

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-01: Public conditions for development
Government and regional or local institutions can support peace, opportunities, infrastructure, education and social services. For a Namibian application, connect the level’s resources and responsibilities to actual access; an announced allocation is not delivered provision.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-02: Peace and security
Safety can allow production, travel, learning and public participation to continue. Security pursued without accountable protection can itself restrict rights; judge who can use the resulting opportunities.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-03: Opportunities and finance
Publicly supported credit or training can address missing productive resources. Repayment, eligibility and viable demand matter; a loan cannot by itself repair a market or skills gap.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-04: Infrastructure
Infrastructure provides supporting networks such as water, roads, electricity and communications. Maintenance and usable connections turn installed assets into services; a disconnected network segment may not remove the barrier.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-05: Education and social services
Education and social provision can build capabilities and protect well-being. Staff, supplies, access and inclusion determine use; building counts alone cannot establish outcomes.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-06: Decentralisation
Decentralisation transfers specified functions or decision responsibilities from central to subnational levels. Local knowledge can improve relevance if resources, capacity and accountability accompany functions; transfer without funding can leave a service gap.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-07: Apply the distinction
A fictional local authority receives maintenance responsibilities together with a funded budget, trained operators and public reporting requirements. Local knowledge can help identify recurring faults, while resources make a response possible. If the budget is repeatedly delayed, the service can still fail despite the transfer. Distinguish responsibility, capacity and accountability when assessing decentralisation.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-08: Namibian institutional context
The official [decentralisation portal](https://decentralisation.gov.na/) and [subnational-government directory](https://murd.gov.na/en/sub-national-governments), checked 20 October 2026, identify Namibia’s regional and local institutional context. Use the principles above without inventing a specific council’s current powers, budget or service record.

### BLK-6136-8.1.04-R01: Evaluate decentralisation with several criteria
Decentralisation can bring decisions closer to residents, use local knowledge of needs, allow quicker responses and make local representatives easier to question. These gains depend on real authority, operating funds and capable staff. Poorer areas may lack the tax base or skills of richer areas, creating unequal services. Local capture or weak oversight can also restrict whose interests are heard. Central coordination remains useful for shared networks and fair resource distribution. A local decision is not automatically more inclusive: check participation, accountability, resources and the service outcome.

## UNIT CU-6136-8.1.05: Implementation barriers

### BLK-6136-8.1.05-01: Implementation barriers
Planning, skills, ethics, finance and continued training affect how resources become development outcomes. These are mechanisms to investigate, not proof that all people in a lower-income country lack effort or integrity.

### BLK-6136-8.1.05-02: Planning
Poor planning can mismatch activities, resources and actual needs. An unused facility may result when staffing or access was omitted; checking the need and operating resources can prevent a construction-only response.

### BLK-6136-8.1.05-03: Skills
Missing relevant skills can prevent productive equipment or services operating effectively. Recruitment and accessible training can address a documented gap, while more equipment alone may remain idle.

### BLK-6136-8.1.05-04: Ethics and transparency
Corruption diverts entrusted decisions or resources for improper benefit; transparency makes decisions and resource use open to scrutiny. Clear records and accountability can help detect diversion, but publishing a budget alone does not ensure effective oversight.

### BLK-6136-8.1.05-05: Finance
Insufficient finance can stop feasible inputs or maintenance being obtained. Identify the missing resource and sustainable funding route; borrowing without viable returns can add repayment pressure.

### BLK-6136-8.1.05-06: In-service training
In-service training updates skills during employment. Relevant practice and support can help staff use changed procedures; a one-off certificate without application need not improve performance.

### BLK-6136-8.1.05-07: Apply the distinction
An invented training facility has equipment and power, but purchases repeatedly bypass transparent procedures. Funds may buy less useful provision if decisions favour private interests instead of the stated need. Published procurement rules, competent oversight and usable complaints routes can reduce this risk; a rule’s existence alone does not establish that it is enforced.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-8.1-019-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-8.1.03-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-8.1-039-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-8.1.03-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why private shops alongside a state-owned water provider indicate a mixed arrangement rather than exclusively private production.
ANSWER: Private and public enterprises coexist. Test ownership across the productive activities, rather than assuming markets or private shops exclude a public role.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: private activity taken as proof of an exclusively private economy.", "Distinction: private emphasis versus explicit coexistence.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-8.1-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-8.1.01-00", "OBJ-6136-8.1.04-05"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) the meanings of representation and delegation [2]. Explain (b) why an invented transfer of water-service tasks without staff or funds may not improve local access [4].
ANSWER: (a) Acting for others; assigning specified responsibilities within limits. (b) Transferring tasks gives the local body responsibility for water services and may bring decisions closer to users. It does not itself create trained operators, so maintenance and reliable operation can remain impossible. Missing funds separately prevent the purchase of supplies and payment for necessary work. Transfer should therefore be accompanied by defined resources, skills and accountability; measure reliable access for residents rather than assuming that a change of responsibility improves services.
GUIDANCE: ["Point: correct meanings [2].", "Development: connect transferred tasks and missing means to the service outcome [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-8.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-8.1.04-00", "OBJ-6136-8.1.05-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Describe (a) this fictional comparison: A spends N$100,000 on buildings and has 30% functioning service coverage; B spends N$80,000 with staffing and maintenance and has 70% [3]. Suggest (b) a priority for A where faults and unstaffed facilities are reported, aiming at usable access [3].
ANSWER: (a) Higher capital-building expenditure accompanies lower functioning coverage in A; B has coverage 40 points higher despite lower expenditure. (b) Address staffing and maintenance with clear responsibility and funding, then measure access. The pair does not isolate causes, but A’s stated faults identify practical priorities.

# TOPIC 9.1: Trade

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.01: Trade

### BLK-6136-9.1.01-01: Trade
Trade is exchange of goods and services, commonly through buying and selling. International trade crosses national borders. Exports are sold abroad; imports are bought from abroad, from the perspective of the economy being described.

### BLK-6136-9.1.01-02: Use the evidence
A fictional pottery group sells surplus goods to another district and buys packaging it cannot produce efficiently itself. Exchange can widen its market and supply useful inputs, provided transport costs and product quality permit viable trade. The benefit comes from the goods and opportunities obtained, not simply from money changing hands.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.02: Reasons for exchange

### BLK-6136-9.1.02-01: Reasons for exchange
Trade can connect differing resources, skills, products and markets, allowing access beyond domestic production. Benefits depend on viable costs, access and terms; reliance on a narrow product or supplier can expose livelihoods to disruption.

### BLK-6136-9.1.02-02: Use the evidence
Two fictional producers specialise in different products suited to their skills and resources, then exchange part of their output. Specialisation may improve productivity and widen the range available to each. Dependence on a single customer or an unreliable supplier can limit those gains. Assess the terms and reliability of exchange alongside the productive opportunity.

### BLK-6136-9.1.02-R01: Several reasons for international trade
Natural resources and climate differ, so countries can obtain products unavailable locally. Different skills and equipment make some goods cheaper or better suited to production elsewhere. Specialising can allow larger-scale production and lower average costs when demand is sufficient. Producers can reach more buyers beyond a small domestic market. Consumers and firms can obtain a wider variety of goods, services and productive inputs. These are different benefits, but transport costs, unreliable suppliers or dependence on one export can reduce them. Explain the particular benefit and its condition rather than treating every import as a loss.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.03: Two external measures

### BLK-6136-9.1.03-01: Two external measures
Balance of trade is export value minus import value for goods over a period; balance of payments records wider economic transactions between residents and non-residents. State explicitly if a supplied trade measure includes services. An investment inflow is not itself a goods export and cannot automatically be counted as an improved goods trade balance.

### BLK-6136-9.1.03-02: Balance of trade
Balance of trade is the value of goods exported minus goods imported over a stated period. Exports above imports give a surplus; below give a deficit. Use comparable currency, period and scope rather than combining counts and values.

### BLK-6136-9.1.03-03: Balance of payments
Balance of payments is a record of economic transactions between a country’s residents and non-residents over a stated period. It includes more than goods trade, such as services, income, transfers and financial flows. Detailed sub-account accounting is outside this lesson.

### BLK-6136-9.1.03-04: Use the evidence
A fictional country records annual goods exports of N$75 million and goods imports of N$68 million. Exports minus imports gives a goods trade surplus of N$7 million. This balance excludes services and financial transactions, so it is not a complete measure of external payments or development. A separate investment inflow must not be added as if it were a goods export.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.04: Prices and external pressure

### BLK-6136-9.1.04-01: Prices and external pressure
Export revenue depends on price and quantity, while import needs create external payment requirements. A fall in export prices with unchanged quantities can reduce receipts and make essential imports harder to finance. Foreign investment provides finance through a different transaction, not an immediate export-value change.

### BLK-6136-9.1.04-02: Trading and fluctuation
Countries trade goods and services, directly or through organised markets and firms. A producer relying on one export faces concentrated price or demand exposure; diversification and viable processing can reduce some risks but need resources and buyers.

Applied example: A price fall can reduce sales revenue even when the quantity sold is unchanged. Uncertain future prices can make investment and repayment planning harder.

### BLK-6136-9.1.04-03: Payment difficulties
External payment pressure can arise when available foreign receipts and finance are insufficient for payment commitments. Prioritising essential needs or seeking support can help, but borrowing creates obligations and restricting imports may remove necessary productive inputs.

Applied example: Essential imported equipment or inputs can become harder to obtain. Meeting external repayment obligations can compete with other uses of scarce finance.

### BLK-6136-9.1.04-04: A fairer economic order
Debates about a new economic order concern fairer participation, bargaining, access to technology and the distribution of gains in global exchange. Better terms can support development, but agreements need implementation and capacity; a declaration alone does not change productive opportunities.

Applied example: Improve developing producers’ opportunities to obtain a reasonable return from trade. Reduce barriers that leave weaker economies with little influence over trading or financing terms.

### BLK-6136-9.1.04-05: Use the evidence
A fictional exporter sells 500 units at N$20 each, then the same quantity at N$16. Receipts fall from N$10,000 to N$8,000. If its foreign payment commitments do not change, fewer export receipts are available to meet them. This illustrates price exposure; a real judgement would also examine other receipts, finance and the possibility of adjustment.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.05: Broad trade patterns

### BLK-6136-9.1.05-01: Broad trade patterns
Developing, newly industrialised and developed economies participate in varied commodity, manufactured and service trade. Broad patterns can include primary-product dependence or expanding manufactures, but countries differ and also trade within these groups. A category is not a current trade-data table.

### BLK-6136-9.1.05-02: Developing economies
Some developing economies rely heavily on a few primary exports and imported equipment or goods. That concentration can expose receipts to price changes; it does not describe every developing country or prevent manufactured and service exports.

Applied example: World price falls can sharply reduce earnings from its narrow export base. It may still need to buy machinery and other manufactured goods from abroad.

### BLK-6136-9.1.05-03: NIC patterns
Industrial expansion can increase manufactured exports and imports of equipment or components. Participation in production chains may create jobs, while imported-input dependence and low local retention can limit gains. NIC is a broad syllabus category, not a permanent current list.

Applied example: Manufactured products can form a larger share of exports. Imported equipment and intermediate inputs may support the expanding production.

### BLK-6136-9.1.05-04: Developed economies
Many developed economies trade manufactures, services, technology and raw materials across extensive networks. They also differ in resources and specialisation. Higher income does not mean an economy exports every advanced product or avoids dependence.

Applied example: Exports can include high-value manufactured products or services. Imports can include raw materials and products made through international production networks.

### BLK-6136-9.1.05-05: Use the evidence
In an invented export table, Country C receives 65% of export earnings from primary goods and 35% from manufactures. Country D receives 40% and 60%. D has the larger manufactured share; C may be more exposed to its primary-export markets. Neither table establishes income distribution, every product traded or the permanent development category of a country.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.06: Globalisation

### BLK-6136-9.1.06-01: Globalisation
Globalisation is increasing international connectedness and interdependence through flows such as trade, investment, information and people. It differs from global warming, a rise in global average temperature. Communication can support globalisation but does not by itself establish fair access.

### BLK-6136-9.1.06-02: Use the evidence
An invented design business shares plans with a foreign workshop and coordinates orders across several countries. These links illustrate globalisation through connected production, communication and exchange. They are conceptually different from a change in global average temperature. A cross-border link does not itself establish the scale or distribution of local development benefits.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.07: Opportunities and constraints

### BLK-6136-9.1.07-01: Opportunities and constraints
Global connections can expand markets, finance and knowledge while exposing producers to competition, shocks and unequal bargaining. Judge participation, local retention, capacity and environmental effects. An increased flow is not by itself proof of a net local benefit.

### BLK-6136-9.1.07-02: Opportunities
Access to larger markets, suitable investment and knowledge can support productive activity. Benefits require the ability to meet quality and delivery needs and opportunities for local workers or suppliers to participate.

Applied example: Access to foreign buyers can expand a producer’s market. International links can supply technology or knowledge that improves productive capability.

### BLK-6136-9.1.07-03: Constraints
Infrastructure gaps, missing skills, finance barriers or dependence on dominant buyers can limit global participation. External competition can challenge local firms while widening consumer options; compare who gains and loses under the stated conditions.

Applied example: Stronger foreign competition can displace producers unable to match price or quality. Reliance on external buyers can expose local work to distant demand shocks.

### BLK-6136-9.1.07-04: Use the evidence
A fictional processing agreement offers local technicians supervised training and includes purchases from nearby suppliers. Those links can retain skills and spending beyond immediate output. They require workable training, suitable supplier quality and fair work conditions. Assess actual delivery, costs and retained benefits; ownership or a foreign connection alone does not establish the result.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.1.08: Participate through capabilities

### BLK-6136-9.1.08-01: Participate through capabilities
Infrastructure, human-resource development, market exposure and suitable investment can support international participation. Choose the action that addresses the actual constraint; promotion cannot repair a blocked road or supply a missing quality standard.

### BLK-6136-9.1.08-02: Infrastructure
Transport, communication and reliable productive services connect firms to markets. Usable maintained networks reduce some delivery or coordination barriers, but must fit the goods and destination.

### BLK-6136-9.1.08-03: Human resources
Relevant education and training develop capabilities to produce, manage quality and coordinate exchange. Training needs practice and work opportunities; it complements rather than replaces equipment or access.

### BLK-6136-9.1.08-04: International exposure
Market research, exchange and appropriate promotion can help producers understand buyers and requirements. Exposure becomes useful when firms can act on the information; attention alone does not establish sales.

### BLK-6136-9.1.08-05: Investment
Suitable foreign investment can provide productive finance, equipment or networks. Local participation, viable terms and maintained capacity affect benefits. Attracting funds is not equivalent to exporting goods.

### BLK-6136-9.1.08-06: Use the evidence
An invented exporter has customers and transport but its containers fail the buyers’ agreed labelling requirements. Feasible quality-control training addresses the immediate access constraint more directly than extra advertising. The producer must still meet cost and delivery requirements. International exposure is useful when it leads to capabilities that fit the actual market opportunity.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.1-005-DIST
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.1.03-00"], "subtype": "DIST", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Differentiate", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Differentiate the goods trade balance from the balance of payments.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.1-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.1.03-00", "OBJ-6136-9.1.04-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) the distinction between an export receipt and an investment inflow [2]. Explain (b) why fictional unchanged export quantity with lower unit price can worsen import-financing pressure when essential import costs stay fixed [4].
ANSWER: (a) Payment for foreign sales versus finance invested through a different transaction. (b) Export receipts equal quantity sold multiplied by the unit price, so a lower price reduces receipts even when export quantity is unchanged. Essential imports still require the same payment under the stated fixed costs. Export receipts therefore cover less of those import payments, increasing the need for other foreign receipts, finance or adjustment. Investment could help finance a payment gap, but it is a different transaction and does not itself improve the goods trade balance or remove future financing risks.
GUIDANCE: ["Point: transaction distinction [2].", "Development: connect price, unchanged quantity and fixed payment needs [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.1.03-00", "OBJ-6136-9.1.04-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: Calculate (a) fictional annual goods balance with N$140 million exports and N$170 million imports [2]. Explain (b) whether N$40 million foreign investment makes it a goods surplus when those trade values stay unchanged [4].
ANSWER: (a) 140 - 170 = -30 million N$, a N$30 million goods deficit. (b) No: investment is a separate financial flow, so the deficit remains. It may help finance activity or payment needs, but a later trade effect needs evidence of changed exports or imports.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: correct method, deficit and N$ million [2].", "Meaning: distinguish the unchanged goods measure from new financing and bound later effects [4].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 9.2: Foreign investment and aid

## UNIT CU-6136-9.2.01: Investment and aid

### BLK-6136-9.2.01-01: Investment and aid
Foreign investment places resources in an economy from abroad with an investment interest or return; aid provides assistance for development or humanitarian needs. Aid can include grants, expertise, goods or concessional finance. Purpose and terms differ; foreign money is not automatically a gift.

### BLK-6136-9.2.01-02: Foreign investment
Foreign investment is investment by an investor based outside the recipient economy. Direct investment involves lasting interest and influence in an enterprise; other holdings may be financial investments without the same productive control.

### BLK-6136-9.2.01-03: Aid
Aid is assistance intended to support development or humanitarian needs, through resources such as grants, expertise, goods or suitable finance. A grant need not be repaid, while a loan carries repayment terms. Assess delivery, purpose and conditions separately.

### BLK-6136-9.2.01-04: Assess the arrangement
A fictional school has trained staff but needs technical design and funding for a safe sanitation system. Suitable assistance should match both gaps and include a credible operating and maintenance arrangement. A grant avoids repayment, but its size alone does not establish useful delivery. Recipient priorities and the terms of support affect whether the result can last.

### BLK-6136-9.2.01-R01: Benefits and limitations of outside resources
Aid can finance essential services when local public resources are limited, supply technical knowledge and provide relief after a shock. A grant avoids repayment, but donor conditions or poorly chosen projects can reduce local control or usefulness. Foreign investment can provide equipment, jobs, training and market connections. Profits may leave the economy, local suppliers may be bypassed, and weak safeguards can leave pollution or poor work conditions. Loans can spread payment for useful capacity over time but commit later resources to debt service. Judge feasibility, local influence, maintained benefits and opportunity cost for each arrangement; a larger inflow is not proof of greater welfare.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.2.02: Forms and conditions

### BLK-6136-9.2.02-01: Forms and conditions
Investment, grants, technical assistance, loans and credits have different ownership, repayment and delivery arrangements. A loan is a borrowing arrangement; debt is the amount outstanding. Emergency or development purpose differs from bilateral delivery between countries or multilateral delivery through institutions.

### BLK-6136-9.2.02-02: Direct investment and companies
Foreign direct investment can establish productive facilities and connect technology or markets through firms, including multinationals. Local skills, suppliers and retained benefits matter; finance and jobs can coexist with profits leaving or environmental costs.

### BLK-6136-9.2.02-03: Loans and credits
Foreign loans provide resources with repayment obligations, often with interest and agreed timing. They may finance useful capacity but create debt-service needs. Borrowing for an unusable asset adds obligations without the expected earning or service benefit.

### BLK-6136-9.2.02-04: Assess the arrangement
An invented council compares a small grant for a limited service with an affordable loan that reaches more residents but commits later revenue. The grant protects future funds; the loan widens current reach. Neither financing label settles the judgement. Compare need, maintained operation, repayment capacity and the services postponed under each feasible option.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.2.03: Institutions have distinct roles

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-01: Institutions have distinct roles
The syllabus groups several international development bodies, but their mandates and institutional forms differ. The World Bank and IMF are international financial institutions, UNDP a development programme and UNICEF a children’s fund; FAO and UNESCO are specialised agencies. Match the actual need and role rather than calling them interchangeable donors.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-02: World Bank
The World Bank supports development through finance, knowledge and technical assistance, including long-term projects and institutional capacity. A proposed infrastructure or service project needs feasible design and implementation; institutional support does not prove the project’s outcomes.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-03: IMF
The IMF supports monetary cooperation and economic stability through monitoring, advice, capacity development and lending for balance-of-payments difficulties. Its stabilisation role differs from simply building schools or giving unconditional grants; lending conditions and effects require case evidence.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-04: UNDP
UNDP works on development including poverty reduction, governance, resilience and environmental sustainability through partnerships and technical support. It can help strengthen capabilities and coordination; a broad mandate does not mean it performs every service itself.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-05: UNICEF
UNICEF focuses on children’s rights and well-being, including health, nutrition, education, water and protection. Child-focused assistance can link several needs, while implementation and reach still require evidence.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-06: FAO
FAO works on food security, agriculture and related knowledge and cooperation to address hunger. Agricultural advice and evidence can support suitable production, but need local access, capabilities and conditions to become useful.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-07: UNESCO
UNESCO promotes international cooperation in education, science, culture and information. Its education and heritage work differs from general lending or food-distribution functions; select the role relevant to the development question.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-08: Assess the arrangement
A fictional programme seeking child-focused nutrition support needs a different institutional role from one seeking finance for an external-payment shortfall. Match the agency’s mandate and type of assistance to the actual problem before evaluating terms and delivery. A broad international role does not mean an organisation performs every service or supplies unconditional funds.

### BLK-6136-9.2.03-09: Official mandate references
Roles checked 20 October 2026 against [World Bank/IMF](https://www.worldbank.org/en/about/history/the-world-bank-group-and-the-imf), [UNDP](https://www.undp.org/about-us), [UNICEF](https://www.unicef.org/what-we-do), [FAO](https://www.fao.org/about/about-fao/en/) and [UNESCO](https://www.unesco.org/en/about-us). These support the broad distinctions, not promises of a particular grant or programme outcome.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.2-004-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.2.01-02"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.2-005-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.2.02-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which fictional water offer better fits reliable community access. A is a grant for a smaller maintained network reaching 60 households. B is an affordable loan for a maintained network reaching 100; the council can repay from revenue but would postpone another service project. Both have qualified operators.
ANSWER: The grant improves access without repayment and protects funds for the other service, but leaves more households unserved. The loan reaches a wider group with a credible operating and repayment route, while committing public income and delaying another benefit. Prefer the loan where the additional water need outweighs that opportunity cost and repayments remain manageable. Otherwise a grant-led first stage is safer. Neither grant status nor the larger funding amount settles the development judgement.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.2-006-CHAIN
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.2.02-00"], "subtype": "CHAIN", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Explain the economic mechanism when a fictional foreign loan finances equipment that cannot operate because power and training are absent, while repayments begin immediately.
ANSWER: The asset supplies no expected productive benefit while debt service consumes resources. Missing complementary inputs prevent the hoped-for return, so borrowed finance alone does not establish a development gain.
GUIDANCE: ["Mechanism: unusable asset plus immediate obligations.", "Consequence: resource pressure without expected productive capacity.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

PROMPT: Explain the difference between the loan and debt in this fictional case: a group borrows N$1,000 and later repays N$300 of principal, with no interest or fees.
ANSWER: The loan is the borrowing arrangement that advanced N$1,000. The remaining debt is N$700 after the principal repayment. The original amount advanced and the outstanding obligation therefore differ.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.2-018-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.2.02-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain whether fictional emergency food support delivered through an organisation funded by several countries must be either emergency relief or multilateral aid.
ANSWER: It can be both. Emergency relief describes its immediate purpose; multilateral describes the channel through a shared international organisation. The categories answer different questions, so one does not exclude the other.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: treat purpose and delivery channel as competing categories.", "Distinction: apply both labels to the same supplied programme with reasons.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.2.03-02"], "subtype": "FEATURE", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["A"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 2}

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.2-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.2.01-00", "OBJ-6136-9.2.02-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) the difference between grant and loan repayment terms [2]. Explain (b) why a fictional large loan for an unused machine with repayments due can be weaker than smaller assistance addressing a working producer’s missing input [4].
ANSWER: (a) A grant is not normally repayable; a loan carries agreed repayment obligations. (b) A machine that remains unused cannot supply the output or earnings expected to justify its cost. Loan repayments still fall due, committing resources without that productive return. Smaller assistance addressing the working producer’s missing input can enable existing labour and equipment to produce, if demand and operating conditions support it. Compare usable output, retained benefits and repayment obligations; a larger headline loan amount does not by itself establish a better development result.

Suggest (b) the first issue to investigate in A if its aim is usable access and unfinished distribution is reported [3].
ANSWER: (a) B achieves coverage 40 points higher with less funding, so finance volume and reach do not rank alike. (b) Investigate distribution and maintenance responsibilities, not merely seek more funds; the reported delivery gap directly affects reach. The pair alone does not prove the reason for the difference.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: compare amount and reach [3].", "Meaning: justify a delivery investigation against the supplied fault and aim [3].", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

# TOPIC 9.3: Regional and Global cooperation

## UNIT CU-6136-9.3.01: Regional cooperation

### BLK-6136-9.3.01-01: Regional cooperation
Regional cooperation joins states around shared development, trade or service goals; SACU and SADC have distinct scope and institutions. Agreements can widen opportunities but implementation, infrastructure, different capacities and the distribution of gains affect success. An objective is not proof of an achieved result.

### BLK-6136-9.3.01-02: SACU
The Southern African Customs Union links Botswana, Eswatini, Lesotho, Namibia and South Africa in a customs union, including common external tariff arrangements and shared customs revenues. Its common customs area can facilitate trade and coordination; revenue dependence, unequal productive capacity and administrative barriers still require assessment. It is not the same organisation as SADC.

### BLK-6136-9.3.01-03: SADC
The Southern African Development Community promotes regional integration, development, poverty reduction, peace and security. Cooperation can connect infrastructure and markets, while capacity differences, implementation gaps and cross-border coordination can limit benefits. Wider membership does not establish uniform rules or outcomes.

### BLK-6136-9.3.01-04: Agreement and implementation
A fictional regional agreement recognises shared product-testing results, reducing repeated testing for qualifying goods. Producers still need compliant products and workable implementation at borders. The agreement creates an opportunity to reduce costs, while unequal testing capacity can leave some firms unable to benefit. Assess practical participation and costs rather than equate cooperation with identical gains for all members.

### BLK-6136-9.3.01-05: Official institutional references
Roles and membership checked 20 October 2026 using [SACU](https://www.sacu.int/about-sacu), its [trade-facilitation functions](https://www.sacu.int/trade-facilitation-and-revenue-management) and [SADC objectives](https://sadc.int/pages/sadc-objectives). Shared revenue and integration are institutional arrangements; the fictional cases are not official performance assessments.

### BLK-6136-9.3.01-R01: Achievements and continuing challenges
SACU’s established common customs arrangements and shared revenue mechanism are practical forms of cooperation, rather than merely a declaration of friendship. They can ease internal goods trade and provide public revenue. Dependence on customs receipts exposes budgets to changes in that revenue, while the much larger South African economy creates unequal productive capacities within the union. SADC has created regional institutions and a free-trade framework among participating states. These provide routes for cooperation but do not remove every border delay, transport bottleneck or political disagreement. Unequal infrastructure and capacity can make gains uneven. Distinguish an institution or agreement that exists from the size of its economic effect, which needs dated performance evidence.

## UNIT CU-6136-9.3.02: EU cooperation

### BLK-6136-9.3.02-01: EU cooperation
The European Union negotiates trade arrangements and cooperates with external partners, including development-related agreements. Market access can support exporters if they meet relevant requirements and can deliver; compliance costs and unequal capacities can limit who benefits. Do not assume an agreement removes every barrier or applies identically to all goods and countries.

### BLK-6136-9.3.02-02: Market access requires capabilities
An invented small producer receives information about an external-market agreement but lacks a reliable product-testing process. Suitable technical support can help it meet the applicable requirements; market access on paper does not guarantee orders. Compare compliance costs, viable buyers and locally retained benefits before judging the cooperation’s contribution.

### BLK-6136-9.3.02-03: Cooperation source
The [EU Council’s trade-agreement overview](https://www.consilium.europa.eu/en/policies/trade-agreements/), checked 20 October 2026, describes trade partnerships including development-related agreements. Specific tariffs, product rules and eligibility require the relevant current official agreement; this lesson does not invent them.

### BLK-6136-9.3.02-R01: EU cooperation in a Southern African example
The 2016 EU–SADC Economic Partnership Agreement illustrates a regional trade relationship involving Namibia. Market-access arrangements can support exporters and encourage coordination, but firms still need to meet applicable standards and deliver reliably. Compliance can be costly for smaller producers; competition can benefit buyers while placing pressure on some local firms. An agreement’s existence is an achievement of cooperation, not evidence that all products or producers gain equally.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.3-001-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.3.01-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Discuss", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Discuss how to phase two fictional regional-trade improvements. Coordinated documents can reduce delays at existing border facilities sooner and at lower cost. A funded freight-link upgrade serves more producers but takes two years. Both have agreed maintenance or operating responsibilities; resources cover one first.
ANSWER: Document coordination offers earlier savings for existing traders and uses fewer resources. The freight link can widen participation beyond those traders, but its benefits take longer and commit more funds. Prioritise coordination where present administrative delay is the main obstacle; choose the link where excluded producers face much larger transport barriers. Judge actual reach, timing and costs, and retain a route to the second improvement. An agreement alone does not establish those outcomes.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.3-002-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.3.01-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe this fictional corridor comparison: A has low tariffs but deliveries take ten days with repeated bridge closures; B has similar tariffs and maintained links with four-day delivery.
ANSWER: B combines maintained physical access with delivery six days faster despite similar tariffs. The contrast indicates that tariff arrangements alone do not describe practical market access.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: use tariff similarity and delivery times.", "Relationship: connect access conditions to the observed contrast without isolating all causes.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.3-003-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.3.01-01"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Discuss", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Discuss which fictional SACU-related support to phase first. Joint document assistance would reduce frequent delivery delays for existing producers. Quality training would help smaller producers qualify for buyers but takes longer. Both operate within common customs arrangements and are feasible.
ANSWER: Document support helps existing sellers turn common-market access into more dependable deliveries quickly. Quality training expands the set of producers able to benefit, but brings less immediate relief. Prefer document assistance where delays threaten current sales; prefer training where exclusion through quality is the larger development gap. Assess the distribution as well as the size of gains. Common customs rules provide an opportunity without guaranteeing either reliable trade or equal participation.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.3-004-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.3.01-02"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Discuss", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Discuss a fictional SADC transport project. Joint construction and funded maintenance can connect more markets, but cost-sharing commits resources that each state could use for smaller domestic routes. States agree roles; benefits are expected to be larger near the cross-border corridor.
ANSWER: A joint route can overcome a bottleneck that separate national projects leave in place, widening trade and shared service opportunities. It also uses scarce public funds and may concentrate benefits near the corridor. Support it where expected cross-border gains justify those costs and less-connected communities have a credible access plan. Domestic routes may be preferable where local needs outweigh the marginal regional gain. Funded responsibilities improve feasibility but do not prove an equitable outcome.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.3-005-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.3.02-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Discuss", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Discuss which support best helps fictional exporters use an EU-partner trade opportunity. Both have compliant products. A marketing programme would reach more buyers for existing large firms. A quality-training programme would help smaller suppliers maintain compliance but reach fewer export orders initially. Both have feasible finance.
ANSWER: Marketing may convert existing capacity into sales sooner, using firms already able to comply and deliver. Training can broaden participation and reduce future quality failures, but initial sales may be smaller. Prefer training where lasting local supplier capability and inclusion are priorities; marketing may better serve urgent earnings from established capacity. Check cost, retained local gains and actual orders, rather than assuming agreement access makes every firm competitive.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.3-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.3.01-00", "OBJ-6136-9.3.01-02"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "short_answer", "assessment_objectives": ["A", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 6}
PROMPT: State (a) one distinction between SACU and SADC [2]. Explain (b) why fictional tariff cooperation without a functioning bridge may not deliver the intended regional market gains [4].
ANSWER: (a) SACU is a customs union with common customs arrangements; SADC has a broader regional development and integration scope. (b) Tariff cooperation can reduce the charges on qualifying regional goods, making exchange more attractive. The broken physical connection still prevents those goods reaching buyers, so lower charges alone cannot create the intended deliveries. Without deliveries, producers cannot realise the expected market access or sales gains from the agreement. A feasible maintained transport connection and workable border implementation must complement the tariff arrangement; judge actual participation and delivered trade rather than agreement signing alone.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-9.3-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-9.3.01-00", "OBJ-6136-9.3.02-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 6}

ANSWER: (a) A combines five more delivery days with a late share 25 points higher. (b) Address the documented road reliability with responsible providers and maintenance, then monitor delivery; identical agreement terms do not resolve unequal practical access.
