# TOPIC 10.1: Research skills

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.01: Choose an enquiry

### BLK-6136-10.1.01-01: Choose an enquiry
A research topic identifies an issue that can be investigated with accessible evidence in a specified setting. Choose a safe, feasible focus rather than an entire national problem. Learners may collect in groups, but each presents an individual report; the written examination asks about the enquiry, which is not formally assessed coursework.

### BLK-6136-10.1.01-02: Worked enquiry connection
With permission to observe a safe public site for one hour on three days, a fictional research group narrows its topic to the number of buses arriving in each equal interval. It does not claim to study private reasons for travel because it cannot interview passengers. The question fits what can be safely observed within the available time.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.02: A usable topic

### BLK-6136-10.1.02-01: A usable topic
A good topic combines interest, relevance, ethical practice and a specific area and time. Test whether a manageable enquiry can answer it with available resources. Interest alone does not justify sensitive questions or unsafe fieldwork.

### BLK-6136-10.1.02-02: Interest
Interest gives a researcher a reason to sustain attention to an issue. It should not preselect the answer; record observations that challenge expectations as well as those that agree.

Applied example: Interest can sustain attention through collection and checking. It does not justify ignoring evidence that challenges the researcher’s expectation.

### BLK-6136-10.1.02-03: Relevance
Relevance links an enquiry to a meaningful development issue and the evidence needed. A water-journey question can inform access, while an unrelated opinion survey may not address that aim.

Applied example: It concerns a defined development issue, such as access to a service. Its variables provide evidence that can answer a practical question about that issue.

### BLK-6136-10.1.02-04: Ethics
Ethical enquiry respects consent, privacy, safety and participants’ choice not to take part. Use non-sensitive topics, explain purpose and avoid identifiable records where unnecessary. Do not pressure participants, enter unsafe sites or ask classmates for health or violence disclosures.

Applied example: Explain the purpose and allow people to decline. Record answers without unnecessary identifying details and protect participants’ privacy.

### BLK-6136-10.1.02-05: Specific scope
State the study area, population, variables and collection dates or period. A bounded question such as school-route travel time in one settlement during one week is more manageable than asking why education is unequal everywhere.

Applied example: Specify the place and population to be investigated. State the collection period and the variables to be measured.

### BLK-6136-10.1.02-06: Worked enquiry connection
A fictional learner is interested in employment but cannot safely contact employers or collect private earnings records. A narrower enquiry into the opening times of accessible public training services may be feasible and relevant to development. Interest helps sustain the work; relevance, ethical access and a clear scope determine whether usable evidence can answer the question.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.03: Aim and objectives

### BLK-6136-10.1.03-01: Aim and objectives
An aim states what the enquiry intends to find out; objectives are specific tasks that help achieve it. Start with a question, then choose measurable objectives and collection steps. A recommendation is a later response, not an observation already established before collection.

### BLK-6136-10.1.03-02: Research aim
A research aim is a broad statement of what an enquiry intends to find out, linked to its research question or hypothesis. For a water-access enquiry, the aim might be to investigate return-journey times during the stated week.

### BLK-6136-10.1.03-03: Research objective
A research objective is a specific task or target that helps the enquiry achieve its aim. For that aim, record return minutes including waiting for selected households and compare the resulting categories.

### BLK-6136-10.1.03-04: Worked enquiry connection
An invented enquiry asks whether waiting times differ between two authorised service points. Its aim is to investigate that comparison. Specific objectives are to record waiting minutes over the same agreed period and compare the resulting distributions. The objectives guide collection; a proposal to change staffing would follow the findings rather than stand in for them.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.04: Question and hypothesis

### BLK-6136-10.1.04-01: Question and hypothesis
A research question asks what the enquiry will investigate; a hypothesis predicts a testable relationship or outcome. “Do longer routes accompany lower attendance?” is a question; “Longer routes accompany lower attendance” is a hypothesis. Evidence may challenge either expectation.

### BLK-6136-10.1.04-02: Hypothesis
A hypothesis is a tentative, testable statement about a relationship or outcome that evidence can support or challenge. Specify measurable variables and scope. A claim so vague that no observation could challenge it is unsuitable.

### BLK-6136-10.1.04-03: Research question
A research question is a focused question that the enquiry seeks to answer using evidence. It need not predict the answer. Its wording should guide variables, participants and method.

### BLK-6136-10.1.04-04: Worked enquiry connection
For an invented study, the question is whether sites farther from a collection depot have more uncollected containers during the same week. A testable hypothesis predicts that association. Distance and counts must be defined and collected consistently. An observed association could support the hypothesis without proving distance caused the missed collections.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.05: Data origins

### BLK-6136-10.1.05-01: Data origins
Primary data is gathered first-hand for the current enquiry; secondary data was previously collected or published for another purpose. Classify origin relative to this enquiry, not whether the information is printed, digital, numerical or spoken.

Applied example: The researcher can collect current local observations first-hand. Existing records can supply a comparison if their population, units and dates are suitable.

### BLK-6136-10.1.05-02: Primary data
Primary data is data collected first-hand for the present enquiry. A learner’s own journey observations qualify; accuracy still depends on selection, definitions and recording.

### BLK-6136-10.1.05-03: Secondary data
Secondary data is data already collected or published for another purpose and used in the present enquiry. A published school register or service table can help if its dates, definitions and population fit; publication does not guarantee suitability.

### BLK-6136-10.1.05-04: Worked enquiry connection
A fictional group records its own queue counts for its present enquiry: these are primary data. It also uses a previously published service timetable: that is secondary data for this enquiry. Either can be numerical, printed or digital. Origin determines the classification; suitability and accuracy still require checks on definitions, dates and coverage.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.06: Collection methods

### BLK-6136-10.1.06-01: Collection methods
Questionnaires, interviews, observation and secondary-source research obtain different kinds of evidence. Specify who or what, where, when and how it is recorded. A bar chart presents collected data; it does not collect responses.

### BLK-6136-10.1.06-02: Questionnaires
A questionnaire uses a consistent set of questions with recordable answers. Use neutral wording, one issue per question, units and non-overlapping categories. Pilot it with relevant volunteers, protect privacy and allow refusal.

Applied example: Respondents may have reasons or circumstances outside the offered choices. Results can therefore conceal relevant differences unless categories and optional explanation are carefully piloted.

### BLK-6136-10.1.06-03: Interviews
An interview obtains information by asking participants questions and recording answers consistently. Explain purpose and consent, use a neutral schedule and ask clarifying questions without suggesting an answer. Interviews can explore reasons but require time and careful recording.

Applied example: The interviewer can ask a neutral clarifying question when an answer is unclear. That can give richer explanation than a fixed choice, although leading questions would distort it.

### BLK-6136-10.1.06-04: Observation
Observation records directly visible or measurable features through a planned record. Define categories, units, site and time before collecting. Count from a safe public place; do not handle waste or infer private motives from visible behaviour.

Applied example: Observation records visible items or behaviour. Private motives are not visible, so an explanation of reasons needs suitable additional evidence rather than an inference from the count.

### BLK-6136-10.1.06-05: Secondary-source research
Secondary-source research selects and records relevant existing evidence. Note publisher, date, unit, population, method and limitations. A different district or old period may not answer the current local question.

Applied example: Its period or population may differ from the question being investigated. Using it without checking those differences can produce a comparison that does not describe the current study group.

### BLK-6136-10.1.06-06: Worked enquiry connection
A fictional travel survey asks consenting adults for minutes spent on a specified journey, including waiting, using the same neutral questions for each respondent. A separate observation sheet records visible queue length at agreed times. The questionnaire supplies reported experience, while observation supplies visible counts; the researchers should not silently substitute one measure for the other.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.07: Choose methods

### BLK-6136-10.1.07-01: Choose methods
Evaluate a method by fit to the question, evidence quality, resources and ethics. Direct observation can measure visible counts but not private reasons; interviews can ask reasons but may face recall or response bias. A researcher difficulty obstructs collection; a community problem is what the enquiry investigates.

Applied example: Different methods produce different evidence. Direct counts can measure visible litter, while interviews can explore reasons; choosing first can leave the method unable to answer the actual question.

### BLK-6136-10.1.07-02: Worked enquiry connection
An invented enquiry needs to know when a public service opens and why its hours change. Repeated safe observation records the visible opening times more directly than people’s recollections. A consenting provider interview can explain the arrangements, but its account should not be treated as an observed timetable. Different questions can justify complementary methods.

### BLK-6136-10.1.07-R01: Compare collection methods by their strengths and limits
A questionnaire can collect comparable answers from many respondents at manageable cost, and a self-completed form may reduce interviewer pressure. Closed choices can hide reasons, unclear wording can distort answers, and low literacy or non-response can exclude people. Interviews allow clarification and detailed explanations, but take more time and can be affected by leading questions or pressure to please the interviewer. Observation records visible conditions directly and can use repeatable categories, but it cannot establish private motives; unusual times or weather can distort a short visit. Secondary evidence saves collection time and may supply wider or historical comparisons, but dates, units, definitions or coverage may not fit the enquiry. Compare at least two relevant benefits and limitations when choosing; do not substitute a definition of the method for its evaluation.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.08: Pilot study

### BLK-6136-10.1.08-01: Pilot study
A pilot study is a small preliminary trial of research procedures or instruments used to identify problems and make improvements before the main enquiry. Try with relevant volunteers, record difficulties, revise the instrument and check the revision. Pilot answers do not establish the whole population’s pattern.

### BLK-6136-10.1.08-02: Worked enquiry connection
A fictional pilot finds that respondents interpret “frequent” visits differently. The researcher revises the question to ask for a count during the past seven days, checks that participants understand the period, then tries the revised wording again. The pilot identifies a measurement problem; its few answers do not establish the population’s actual pattern.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.09: Population and sample

### BLK-6136-10.1.09-01: Population and sample
The research population is the whole defined group; a sample is the selected subset studied. The sampling method is the selection procedure, not the group itself. Findings need limits where the frame misses groups or responses are incomplete.

### BLK-6136-10.1.09-02: Research population
The research population is the whole group of units or people that the enquiry is intended to investigate. Define it before sampling: all households on the relevant settlement list differs from everyone in Namibia.

### BLK-6136-10.1.09-03: Research sample
The research sample is the selected subset of the research population from which data are collected. A list of 12 selected households describes a sample; choosing every fifth household describes the method.

### BLK-6136-10.1.09-04: Worked enquiry connection
An invented study defines its population as households listed in two named settlements during a specified month. A sample selects some of those households for observation or voluntary questions. It cannot automatically represent households outside that area or later residents. A clear population definition makes it possible to assess what the sampling frame includes or misses.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.10: Sampling choices

### BLK-6136-10.1.10-01: Sampling choices
Random, stratified and systematic sampling use different explicit selection procedures. Start with a relevant frame, justify size and selection, record non-response and avoid silently replacing a refusal with a friend. No sample guarantees perfect representativeness.

Applied example: A selection procedure can only choose members covered by its list. Missing a group from that frame can bias coverage even when selection from the list is fair.

### BLK-6136-10.1.10-02: Random sampling
Random sampling selects by chance so each member of the defined research population has an equal chance of selection. Number a complete list and use a fair draw or random numbers without duplicates. Selecting whoever is nearest does not meet that rule.

Applied example: Distance from the researcher determines who is selected, so other households do not have an equal chance. A fair draw from a complete numbered list provides the required chance procedure.

### BLK-6136-10.1.10-03: Stratified sampling
Stratified sampling divides a population into relevant groups and selects from each using an explicit method. For proportional selection, allocate sample places by each group’s share, then randomly select within groups. It preserves planned group coverage but cannot remove every frame or response bias.

Applied example: It deliberately allocates places to both groups in proportion to their population shares. A smaller group is therefore included by design, although non-response can still distort the final evidence.

### BLK-6136-10.1.10-04: Systematic sampling
Systematic sampling selects at a fixed interval from an ordered list after an appropriate starting point. For 48 households and 12 places, interval is four; choose the start fairly among positions one to four, then take every fourth entry. A repeating pattern in the list may bias it.

Applied example: A repeating list pattern can coincide with the selection interval. The sample may then overrepresent one type of unit even though a fixed rule was followed.

### BLK-6136-10.1.10-05: Worked enquiry connection
For an original stratified example, a list contains 60 households in one area and 30 in another. A proportional sample of 18 allocates 12 places and six places respectively, followed by random selection within each group. The design preserves planned group proportions. Incomplete lists and refusals still need recording; proportional allocation does not remove every source of bias.

### BLK-6136-10.1.10-06: Sampling calculations and frames
For this separate worked example, a list of 48 households and a sample of 12 gives interval 48 ÷ 12 = 4. A fair starting position of 2 produces 2, 6, 10, 14, 18, 22, 26, 30, 34, 38, 42 and 46. For two fictional villages of 30 and 15 households, a proportional sample of 9 allocates 6 and 3 places: 30/45 × 9 and 15/45 × 9. Randomly select within each village. For a list of 24 schools, a fair draw of four distinct numbers is random selection; the sample is the selected schools, while the draw is the method. Record refusals and limitations of the lists.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.11: Organise, present and interpret

### BLK-6136-10.1.11-01: Organise, present and interpret
Organise records consistently, reconcile totals, then choose a suitable graph or sketch map to answer the enquiry. A table states observations; interpretation links relevant evidence into a pattern. Percentage equals category count divided by the relevant total, multiplied by 100; identify missing data and avoid asserting causation from association alone.

### BLK-6136-10.1.11-02: Findings
Findings report and interpret collected evidence, including comparisons and exceptions relevant to the aim. A high value differs from a large increase. Use paired observations and units rather than a list with no relationship.

### BLK-6136-10.1.11-03: Graphs
Bar charts compare categories; pie charts or divided rectangles show parts of a stated whole when categories are compatible. For bars state axes, units, category labels and an even scale. Pie angle is category share multiplied by 360°; a divided rectangle allocates proportional lengths. Totals must reconcile. Graphs here use data and construction specifications, not an unseen verified rendering.

### BLK-6136-10.1.11-04: Sketch maps
A sketch map locates relevant features and patterns using clear symbols and a key, title and orientation. Use observed or supplied relative positions; do not imply accurate scale unless measured. Distinguish a spatial pattern from a graph of category totals.

### BLK-6136-10.1.11-05: Worked enquiry connection
An original observation table records 3, 6 and 5 users in three named waiting-time categories. A bar graph gives the categories on the horizontal axis and users on a labelled vertical scale from zero; separated equal-width bars show heights 3, 6 and 5. A title, time period and source identify what is represented. The middle category is largest, but the graph alone does not explain why.

### BLK-6136-10.1.11-06: Check representation by construction
For a separate worked pie example, 20 journeys comprise 8 walking, 7 cycling and 5 using a bus. Their sector angles are 144°, 126° and 90°: divide each count by 20 and multiply by 360°. The angles total 360°. For production-cost shares of 50%, 30% and 20%, a 15 cm divided rectangle has sections 7.5, 4.5 and 3 cm, with boundaries at 7.5 and 12 cm. Label the whole, categories and period. A separate sketch-map example has a north–south road, a clinic at its north end, a bus stop midway and a reading room east of its south end; use symbols, a key, orientation and title, without implying measured scale. A research-process flow chart links question, planning/ethics, pilot, collection, presentation and conclusion; a pilot decision can loop back to revision. Arrows show procedure, not quantities. These specifications do not verify an unseen plotted figure.

## UNIT CU-6136-10.1.12: Conclusions and recommendations

### BLK-6136-10.1.12-01: Conclusions and recommendations
A conclusion answers the question from findings within their limits; a recommendation proposes a feasible response tied to that answer and identifies an implementer. Do not introduce uncollected evidence. For community action distinguish aims, organisation, funding and implementation obstacles: assigning volunteers does not obtain money, and an obstacle needs a matched response and responsible actor.

### BLK-6136-10.1.12-02: Conclusion
A conclusion is an evidence-based answer to the research question or judgement about the hypothesis, within the enquiry’s limits. A sample pattern can support a bounded answer, not a claim about all places. Findings are the supporting observations, not interchangeable with the conclusion.

### BLK-6136-10.1.12-03: Recommendation
A recommendation is a proposed feasible action linked to the findings and conclusions, identifying who could implement it. Name the practical change, implementer and relevant resource condition. A wish such as “improve development” is too vague to follow.

### BLK-6136-10.1.12-04: Worked enquiry connection
In an invented enquiry, repeated records show that a service opens late because a qualified operator arrives after the scheduled start. A feasible recommendation could revise staffing arrangements through the responsible provider. It addresses the observed obstacle more directly than an unrelated poster. State the evidence, responsible actor and resources, and distinguish a proposed response from one already carried out.

### BLK-6136-10.1.12-05: Organisation, funding and implementation
A fictional storage activity costing N$600 with N$200 available needs N$400 additional resources. Agreeing volunteers and dates organises it; recording contributions funds it. If a required lid is late, the coordinator should arrange a provider-approved suitable alternative or delay use. For a book project costing N$100 with N$40 available, seek N$60 or suitable in-kind materials and use the offered dry room to prevent the stated storage loss. These are project plans, not evidence that either was implemented. A recommendation identifies an implementer and follows findings; a conclusion does not introduce a new result.

### BLK-6136-10.1.12-R01: Keep your own enquiry record
Keep an individual dated record with these fields: topic and study area; question or hypothesis; aim and specific objectives; dates and the defined population; sample size and actual selection procedure; consent and safety arrangements; instruments and pilot problem, revision and recheck; collection procedure; anonymised observations, units and missing values; table or graph with title and source; main findings and an exception; conclusion linked to the question; limitations; recommendation, implementer and required resources; actual researcher obstacle and response. Preserve changes and reasons. If working in a group, state what was shared and what you personally did. A missing stage should be marked not completed, rather than replaced by a fictional event. Use this record to answer Section B in your own words.

### BLK-6136-10.1.12-R02: Separate fictional demonstration from personal evidence
This demonstration is invented, not an enquiry you carried out. Suppose a safe observation found 12 visible items at one site and six at another during the same period, with one route inaccessible. The finding is a two-to-one count contrast in the observed places, not a result for the whole community. A conclusion can say the recorded sites differed, while noting missing coverage. A recommendation might ask the responsible collection provider to inspect the higher-count site. For your own answer, replace the demonstration with your actual observations and limits. Do not claim these counts, visits or recommendations as your own experience.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-010-PROC

{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.03-02"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}

GUIDANCE: ["Feature: a collection method, not a display.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-022-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-00"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe a way to collect fictional household water-journey times when selected adults freely consent.
ANSWER: Use a questionnaire: explain purpose and privacy, ask return minutes including waiting for a stated recent journey, record the numerical response with date and non-identifying code, and allow refusal.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: consent, consistent question and recording.", "Application: water-journey unit and time reference.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-060-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why drawing a bar chart does not collect people’s reasons for choosing a water point.
ANSWER: A chart presents already obtained information. A suitable interview or questionnaire obtains reasons; test obtaining observations versus displaying results.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: presentation substitutes for collection.", "Distinction: collecting versus displaying.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-023-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-01"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe a questionnaire for this fictional task: selected households’ return water journeys.
ANSWER: A questionnaire: Ask a neutral single-issue question in minutes including waiting, use an anonymous code and date, record replies consistently and allow refusal.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Ask a neutral single-issue question in minutes including waiting, use an anonymous code and date, record replies consistently and allow refusal.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-024-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-01"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain one limitation of a questionnaire using only fixed answer choices.
ANSWER: Respondents may have reasons or circumstances outside the offered choices. Results can therefore conceal relevant differences unless categories and optional explanation are carefully piloted.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify relevant responses or reasons excluded by fixed choices.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain the resulting loss of information or a suitable pilot/category improvement.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-025-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-02"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe an interview for this fictional task: why selected consenting adult volunteers use different water points.
ANSWER: An interview: Use a neutral schedule at an agreed safe time, ask which point and reasons without suggesting an answer, record consented responses consistently and omit names.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Use a neutral schedule at an agreed safe time, ask which point and reasons without suggesting an answer, record consented responses consistently and omit names.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-026-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-02"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why an interview may be suitable for understanding a participant’s reasons.
ANSWER: The interviewer can ask a neutral clarifying question when an answer is unclear. That can give richer explanation than a fixed choice, although leading questions would distort it.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify neutral clarification or follow-up as a feature of interviews.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain its contribution to understanding reasons while avoiding leading prompts.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-027-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-03"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe structured observation for this fictional task: litter visible from two safe public paths.
ANSWER: Structured observation: Define litter categories, observation length and time, count visible items on each route using the same record and do not handle waste or enter unsafe areas.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Define litter categories, observation length and time, count visible items on each route using the same record and do not handle waste or enter unsafe areas.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-028-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-03"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why observing litter cannot establish residents’ reasons for dropping it.
ANSWER: Observation records visible items or behaviour. Private motives are not visible, so an explanation of reasons needs suitable additional evidence rather than an inference from the count.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: distinguish directly visible evidence from private motives.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain why counts need additional appropriate evidence to support reasons.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-029-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-04"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe secondary-source research for this fictional task: whether an existing school table fits a local enquiry.
ANSWER: Secondary-source research: Locate the original publisher, record dates, age groups, units and collection definitions, then compare its scope with the present question and note mismatches.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Locate the original publisher, record dates, age groups, units and collection definitions, then compare its scope with the present question and note mismatches.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-030-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-04"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why an old published table may be unsuitable for a new local enquiry.
ANSWER: Its period or population may differ from the question being investigated. Using it without checking those differences can produce a comparison that does not describe the current study group.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify a mismatch in dates, definitions or population.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain how that mismatch weakens relevance to the new local question.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-031-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.07-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why a researcher should choose a collection method after defining the variable.
ANSWER: Different methods produce different evidence. Direct counts can measure visible litter, while interviews can explore reasons; choosing first can leave the method unable to answer the actual question.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify the kind of evidence required by the defined variable.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain how method choice follows that need rather than precedes the question.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-032-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.07-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which method to prioritise for a fictional enquiry into visible litter and service explanations. Safe structured observation measures counts directly; consenting provider interviews explain collection arrangements but take longer. Resources cover one detailed method first, with brief follow-up possible.
ANSWER: Observation gives direct, consistently categorised counts and is stronger when the immediate question concerns where and how much litter is visible. It cannot establish why collection failed. Interviews can explain schedules and constraints but depend on accurate responses and are weaker for exact site counts. Start with observation for the count question, then use brief interviews to investigate the pattern; reverse the priority if the question mainly concerns service arrangements. Match the judgement to the required evidence.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-064-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.07-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["D"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why a researcher’s broken recording device and a community’s long water journey need different responses.
ANSWER: The device obstructs collection and may need a replacement record; the journey is the development issue under study. Test what prevents the enquiry versus what the enquiry investigates.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: community problem substituted for research difficulty.", "Distinction: collection obstacle versus development issue, with matched responses.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-033-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.08-00"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Define pilot study.
ANSWER: A pilot study is a small preliminary trial of research procedures or instruments used to identify problems and make improvements before the main enquiry.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: A pilot study is a small preliminary trial of research procedures or instruments used to identify problems and make improvements before the main enquiry.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-034-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.08-00"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Describe a pilot for an invented water questionnaire whose draft asks whether journeys are “long”.
ANSWER: Try it with relevant volunteers, note requests to define long, replace it with return minutes including waiting, and retry to check understanding before main collection.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: trial, recorded difficulty, revision and recheck.", "Application: ambiguous water-journey wording repaired.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-035-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.08-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["D"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a repair when four of six fictional pilot volunteers interpret “long journey” differently.
ANSWER: Ask return minutes including waiting for a stated journey and retry it with relevant volunteers. A common measure reduces the ambiguity that the pilot revealed.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: divergent interpretations.", "Reason: comparable recording and recheck.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-036-DIST
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.09-00"], "subtype": "DIST", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Differentiate", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Differentiate research population and sample.
ANSWER: The population is the whole defined group; the sample is the selected subset from which evidence is gathered.
GUIDANCE: ["Comparison: whole group versus studied subset.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-037-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.09-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a suitable population definition for an invented enquiry into households using one settlement water point during a stated week.
ANSWER: Define all households using that point in the settlement and week, with an appropriate way to identify them before selection. National residents are too broad for that question.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: one point, settlement and period.", "Reason: frame fits the intended inference.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-059-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.09-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why “the 12 selected households” names a sample rather than a sampling method.
ANSWER: It identifies the studied group. A procedure such as selecting every fifth household names the method; test selected units versus the selection steps.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: group substituted for procedure.", "Distinction: sample versus sampling method.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-038-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.09-01"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Define research population.
ANSWER: The research population is the whole group of units or people that the enquiry is intended to investigate.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: The research population is the whole group of units or people that the enquiry is intended to investigate.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-039-DEF
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.09-02"], "subtype": "DEF", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Remember", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 1}
PROMPT: Define research sample.
ANSWER: The research sample is the selected subset of the research population from which data are collected.
GUIDANCE: ["Meaning: The research sample is the selected subset of the research population from which data are collected.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-040-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-00"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe systematic sampling for this fictional task: 12 households from a complete ordered list of 60.
ANSWER: Systematic sampling: Use interval five; draw a start fairly from one to five, then take each fifth entry. With start three choose 3,8,13,18,23,28,33,38,43,48,53,58 and record refusals.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Use interval five; draw a start fairly from one to five, then take each fifth entry. With start three choose 3,8,13,18,23,28,33,38,43,48,53,58 and record refusals.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-041-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-00"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why a sample needs an appropriate population list.
ANSWER: A selection procedure can only choose members covered by its list. Missing a group from that frame can bias coverage even when selection from the list is fair.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify that selection can operate only on units included in the frame.", "Consequence, 1 mark: link omitted groups to coverage bias despite fair selection within the list.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-042-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-01"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe random sampling for this fictional task: five entries from a complete list of 20.
ANSWER: Random sampling: Number entries one to 20, use a fair draw or random-number procedure for five distinct numbers, map them to entries and record selection and non-response.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Number entries one to 20, use a fair draw or random-number procedure for five distinct numbers, map them to entries and record selection and non-response.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-043-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-01"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why selecting the nearest households is not random sampling.
ANSWER: Distance from the researcher determines who is selected, so other households do not have an equal chance. A fair draw from a complete numbered list provides the required chance procedure.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify proximity rather than chance as the selection rule.", "Consequence, 1 mark: contrast unequal selection chances with a fair draw from a complete list.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-044-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-02"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe stratified sampling for this fictional task: 12 households from groups of 40 and 20.
ANSWER: Stratified sampling: Allocate eight and four places proportionally, then fairly select distinct numbered households within each group. Record frame coverage and refusals rather than substitute convenient respondents.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Allocate eight and four places proportionally, then fairly select distinct numbered households within each group. Record frame coverage and refusals rather than substitute convenient respondents.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-045-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-02"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain one advantage of proportional stratified sampling for two differently sized groups.
ANSWER: It deliberately allocates places to both groups in proportion to their population shares. A smaller group is therefore included by design, although non-response can still distort the final evidence.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify planned representation of both groups in proportion to size.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain inclusion of the smaller group while retaining response/frame limitations.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-046-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-03"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Name and describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Name and describe systematic sampling for this fictional task: ten records from a complete ordered list of 50.
ANSWER: Systematic sampling: Set interval five, choose a fair start among one to five and take every fifth record until ten are selected; inspect the list for periodic patterns.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: explicit reproducible steps.", "Application: Set interval five, choose a fair start among one to five and take every fifth record until ten are selected; inspect the list for periodic patterns.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-047-WHY
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.10-03"], "subtype": "WHY", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain one risk when using systematic sampling from an ordered list.
ANSWER: A repeating list pattern can coincide with the selection interval. The sample may then overrepresent one type of unit even though a fixed rule was followed.
GUIDANCE: ["Reason, 1 mark: identify a repeating list pattern matching the fixed interval.", "Consequence, 1 mark: explain resulting overrepresentation or systematic omission despite a consistent procedure.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-048-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe the relationship in this original fictional table: near-route households, 8 of 10 journeys below 20 minutes; distant-route households, 3 of 10 below 20.
ANSWER: Short journeys are more common in the near group, 80% versus 30%, a 50-percentage-point difference. The association does not isolate distance from waiting or route conditions.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: compare both group denominators.", "Relationship: route category and short-journey prevalence.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-049-CALC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00"], "subtype": "CALC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Calculate", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Calculate the share of 24 fictional journeys taking at least 30 minutes if six meet that condition.
ANSWER: 6 ÷ 24 = 0.25; multiplying by 100 gives 25%.
GUIDANCE: ["Working: category divided by total then converted.", "Unit: percent of recorded journeys.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-050-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Describe how to organise and draw a bar graph for fictional journey categories: below 10 minutes four, 10–29 five, 30 or more three.
ANSWER: Check the total is 12; place non-overlapping categories horizontally and households vertically with scale 0–5 at unit intervals. Draw separated equal-width bars of heights 4,5,3 with title, units and labels; read the largest category and compare relevant counts.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: reconcile, axes/scale, bars and interpretation.", "Application: exact supplied values and non-overlapping categories.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-062-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why “eight of ten near-route journeys are short” is a finding rather than a complete conclusion about all water access.
ANSWER: It reports observed evidence for one group. A conclusion must answer the specified question using relevant findings and limits; the observation alone cannot establish every household’s experience.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: single finding treated as unrestricted answer.", "Distinction: evidence versus bounded conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-051-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.11-01"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe this fictional enquiry result: 12 of 20 respondents report interrupted supply and 8 report reliable supply; 9 of those 12 also report a longer alternative journey.
ANSWER: Interrupted supply is reported by a majority, 60%, and longer alternatives are common within that group, nine of 12. The observations link reliability and journey burden in this sample without showing every interruption has the same effect.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: use both total and conditional counts.", "Relationship: supply category and alternative journey burden.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-052-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.11-02"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Describe how to construct a pie graph from original fictional counts: walk 6, bus 3, other 3 out of 12.
ANSWER: Convert shares to angles: walk 180°, bus 90°, other 90°, totalling 360°. Draw one circle, measure adjacent sectors and add a title, labels/key and the total and period; do not use unequal totals as directly comparable pies.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: shares, angles, sectors and labels.", "Application: correct 180/90/90 model specification.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-053-PROC
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.11-03"], "subtype": "PROC", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Describe a sketch map for a fictional 200 m east-west road with a school at the west end, water point at its midpoint and litter site just north of the east end.
ANSWER: Orient north upwards; draw the road west to east, place school left, water point midway and litter site above the right end. Add distinct symbols, a key and title; state that positions are schematic unless measured to scale.
GUIDANCE: ["Sequence: orientation, relative locations, key/title.", "Application: map positions, not a category chart.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-054-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe what follows from this fictional enquiry: 15 of 20 households report queues over 20 minutes at the same point; five report shorter queues. The aim is to identify whether long queues are common in this sample.
ANSWER: Long queues are common among the sampled households, affecting 75%, but five provide counterexamples. The conclusion fits the sample and period, not a claim that all households always face long queues.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: majority and exception.", "Relationship: answer the aim within scope.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-055-EVAL
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": "EVAL", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 4}
PROMPT: Explain which recommendation to prioritise after fictional records show repeated pump breakdowns. A trained provider can undertake repairs sooner, but a local monitoring-and-reporting arrangement costs less and can identify faults between visits. Either is feasible; funds cannot expand both immediately.
ANSWER: Provider repairs directly restore the faulty equipment and have a qualified implementer, but recurring faults can go unnoticed between visits. Local reporting is cheaper and supports earlier diagnosis, but cannot itself repair the pump. Prioritise repair where the current fault prevents access, while retaining a basic reporting route. If the pump now works and delayed reporting is the main evidence-based problem, monitoring may merit the next funds. Judge restoration and continued reliability separately.
GUIDANCE: ["Judgement: compare genuine benefits and costs against the stated aim.", "Reason: develop each option using supplied evidence, weigh the trade-off, and give a conditional conclusion.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-056-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["D"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a recommendation where fictional results show unsafe litter near a public path and the responsible council has a collection team but lacks a site report.
ANSWER: Submit the non-identifying location record and request a council-led safe collection and maintenance response. Do not ask learners to handle waste; the recommendation links evidence to the competent implementer.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: location evidence and available collection team.", "Reason: matched feasible action.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-063-MISCON
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": "MISCON", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["C"], "blooms_level": "Understand", "command_word": "Explain", "mark_tariff": 2}
PROMPT: Explain why appointing volunteers does not itself fund a fictional N$600 storage project.
ANSWER: It organises people and tasks. Funding obtains money or in-kind resources to meet costs; test responsibility allocation separately from the resource contribution.
GUIDANCE: ["Error: organisation substituted for funding.", "Distinction: tasks versus obtaining resources.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-057-INTERP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.12-01"], "subtype": "INTERP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Describe", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Describe a bounded conclusion from a fictional route survey: longer routes have 50% attendance compared with 80% for shorter routes, but one longer-route school has 90%.
ANSWER: Lower attendance accompanies longer routes overall in the supplied groups, with a notable longer-route exception. This supports an association, not proof of distance causing attendance or a universal rule.
GUIDANCE: ["Evidence: main contrast and exception.", "Relationship: conclusion answers the route-attendance question within causal limits.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-058-APP
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.12-02"], "subtype": "APP", "item_type": "flashcard", "assessment_objectives": ["D"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Suggest", "mark_tariff": 3}
PROMPT: Suggest a response where an invented researcher cannot safely reach one sampled route but equivalent information may be obtained from an approved existing route record.
ANSWER: Consult the supervising teacher about using the suitable record, check its date and definitions, and disclose the changed method and coverage limit. Do not enter the unsafe route or silently invent observations.
GUIDANCE: ["Context: researcher safety/access difficulty and possible secondary evidence.", "Reason: ethical feasible repair with stated limitations.", "Original indicative practice guidance; not an official mark scheme."]

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-P01
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.10-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "practical_task", "assessment_objectives": ["C", "D", "B"], "blooms_level": "Apply", "command_word": "Identify", "mark_tariff": 50}
PROMPT: Fictional Omi water enquiry: population is 60 listed households using the shared point, sample 12. List order is fixed with no known five-entry pattern; random start is 3. Pilot: four of six volunteers ask what “long” means. Main records: return journey including waiting, below 10 minutes 4 households, 10–29 minutes 5, 30+ minutes 3. All 12 consented; no names recorded. Proposed community storage activity costs N$600; N$200 is available, six volunteers and a qualified provider can check siting and use. A lid supplier may be late. Section B: use your own completed enquiry and real records. If it is not yet completed, state that and use these as preparation prompts; do not invent fieldwork. Section B answers are fill-in scaffolds for your own real record; they are not completed fictional fieldwork. A fictional demonstration in the notes is a separate teaching example.
A1(1) Identify the enquiry population. [1]
A1(2) Design five questionnaire elements to measure the stated journey enquiry. [5]
A1(3) Describe systematic selection for the supplied frame. [4]
A1(4) Explain consent and privacy for this survey. [2]
A1(5) Suggest the pilot wording repair and its benefit. [3]
A1(6) Calculate the share taking 30 minutes or more, showing working. [2]
A1(7) Draw a suitable bar chart of the journey categories. [4]
A1(8) Describe the journey pattern using the chart data. [4]
A2(1) State two feasible storage-activity aims. [2]
A2(2) Describe how to organise the activity. [3]
A2(3) Suggest feasible funding for the stated cost. [2]
A2(4) Explain a response to the lid-supply obstacle and name the implementer. [3]
B(1) State your actual topic, area and linked aim. [2]
B(2) Explain your actual pilot finding and resulting change. [2]
B(3) Describe your actual collection procedure. [4]
B(4) Describe your findings with evidence and implications. [4]
B(5) Suggest a recommendation and implementer tied to your actual findings. [3]
ANSWER: A1(1) All 60 listed households using the shared point.
A1(2) Non-identifying household code; collection date; water point used; return minutes including waiting for the stated journey; whether that journey included an interruption/alternative, with clear yes/no and optional explanation. Use neutral wording and consent before questions.
A1(3) Interval five, fair start among 1–5; with start 3 select 3,8,13,18,23,28,33,38,43,48,53,58. Record refusals without convenient substitutions.
A1(4) Explain the aim and voluntary participation, allow refusal, omit names and keep coded records securely; report aggregate findings.
A1(5) Replace long with return minutes including waiting for a stated journey, then retry it; this gives a common measure.
A1(6) 3 ÷ 12 = 0.25; multiplying by 100 gives 25%.
A1(7) Title and period stated; x-axis categories below 10,10–29,30+ minutes; y-axis households from 0–5 in ones. Equal-width separated bars have heights 4,5,3; labels and units shown.
A1(8) The 10–29 group is largest at five, two more than the 30+ group. Eight of 12 take at least ten minutes, so substantial journey time is common in this sample; not every household has the same journey.
A2(1) Provide appropriately checked local storage access and reduce interruption-related collection difficulty; confirm suitability with the provider.
A2(2) Agree permission and provider check, allocate volunteers to coordination, records and logistics, set dates and maintenance responsibility, and communicate access arrangements.
A2(3) Use the available N$200 and seek N$400 in agreed donations or suitable in-kind contributions covering actual costs; keep transparent records.
A2(4) The coordinator should confirm delivery or arrange a provider-approved suitable alternative before use; do not operate incomplete storage merely to meet a date.
B(1) Own-enquiry scaffold: My actual topic was [topic] in [area and dates]. My linked aim was [what I intended to find out]. Refer to my own record; if not completed, state that.
B(2) Own-enquiry scaffold: My pilot revealed [actual problem]. I changed [instrument or procedure] because [reason], and rechecked it by [actual step]. If no pilot was done, acknowledge that limitation.
B(3) Own-enquiry scaffold: I defined [population], selected [sample] using [actual procedure], obtained [permission/consent], then recorded [variables, units and dates] using [instrument]. Note missing responses and any changes.
B(4) Own-enquiry scaffold: My main finding was [pattern], supported by [two comparable observations with units]. This suggests [bounded implication]; [exception or limitation] prevents a wider claim.
B(5) Own-enquiry scaffold: Based on [actual finding], I recommend [specific feasible action]. [Responsible actor] could implement it using [resources], subject to [relevant constraint].
GUIDANCE: ["Original package: Section A 35 marks (A1+A2), Section B 15, total 50. ### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-P02
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.10-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "data_response", "assessment_objectives": ["C", "D", "B"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "Name", "mark_tariff": 50}
PROMPT: Fictional rural-production enquiry: 60 listed grower households, village A 40 and B 20; sample 12, proportionally allocated. A researcher asks each consenting grower the same neutral price questions aloud and records their spoken answers and researchers observe sealed labelled output records with permission. A damaged route blocks one visit; approved appointments at a safe meeting point are possible. Earlier total output 100 bags, later 130; these totals include spoiled bags; shares of the later operation’s total production cost (one stated whole): water 40%, land preparation 35%, storage 25%. Earlier 20 bags spoiled, later 10, with unchanged definitions. These observations do not isolate all causes. Pilot exposed inconsistent bag sizes; the revision specifies the same labelled standard bag. Field record needs date, village code, standard-bag output, price per standard bag and visible storage condition, with no names. Section B: use your own completed enquiry and real records. If it is not yet completed, state that and use these as preparation prompts; do not invent fieldwork. Section B answers are fill-in scaffolds for your own real record; they are not completed fictional fieldwork. A fictional demonstration in the notes is a separate teaching example.
A1(1) Name the collection method used for the growers’ price answers. [1]
A1(2) Name and describe the two collection methods used for price answers and permitted output records. [4]
A1(3) Describe a proportional stratified sample. [4]
A1(4) Explain the sampling fit and a limit. [3]
A1(5) Suggest a safe response to the damaged-route obstacle. [3]
A1(6) Design an observation/record sheet for the stated variables. [5]
A2(1) Calculate the change in production, showing working. [2]
A2(2) Describe the two-period production and loss pattern. [3]
A2(3) Draw a divided rectangle of the production-cost shares. [4]
A2(4) Explain how lower spoilage relates to usable output and why the cost shares cannot prove the cause. [3]
A2(5) Suggest an improvement to the stated recording problem. [3]
B(1) Explain two actual reasons for your research issue selection. [2]
B(2) Describe your actual sampling and its fit. [4]
B(3) Describe findings, evidence, conclusion and limits. [6]
B(4) Suggest a matched response to a difficulty you actually encountered. [3]
ANSWER: A1(1) Interview.
A1(2) Interview consenting selected growers about price per standard bag with a neutral schedule; observe permitted labelled output records using a consistent dated sheet. Do not infer prices from appearance.
A1(3) Allocate eight places to A and four to B, then randomly choose distinct household numbers within each complete group list; record refusals.
A1(4) Both villages enter in population proportions, but incomplete lists or non-response can still distort the sample; it is not a guarantee of population truth.
A1(5) Arrange approved safe meeting appointments for selected consenting growers, disclose the change and record whether it reduces coverage; do not enter the unsafe route.
A1(6) Columns: date, village and anonymous unit code, standard-bag output count, price per standard bag from the interview, visible storage category and missing-data note. Mark source of price separately from observed records.
A2(1) 130 - 100 = 30 additional standard bags.
A2(2) Output rises by 30 while spoiled bags fall by ten. Usable bags rise from 80 to 120, so production and reduced loss both improve the supplied result.
A2(3) Use a 20 cm rectangle of constant width: water 8 cm, land preparation 7 cm, storage 5 cm. Boundaries at 8 and 15 cm; label 40%,35%,25%, title, period and key; total 20 cm/100%.
A2(4) Lower spoilage leaves more of the harvest usable: 100 minus 20 gives 80 earlier, while 130 minus 10 gives 120 later. The single later-period cost shares show spending composition, not a change in spending or proof that storage caused the improvement. Other conditions may differ.
A2(5) Use the standard-bag definition throughout, distinguish observed output from reported price and record missing values; the coordinator should check sheets before comparison.
B(1) Own-enquiry scaffold: I selected [actual issue] because [development relevance] and [feasibility or other real reason]. Explain each using my actual setting and available evidence.
B(2) Own-enquiry scaffold: My population was [defined group]. I selected [size and actual method] using [frame and procedure]. This fitted [aim or resources], but [coverage/non-response limitation] remains.
B(3) Own-enquiry scaffold: I found [pattern] using [observations and units], with [exception]. Therefore [answer to the actual question]. Limit this conclusion to [sample, dates and evidence quality]; distinguish association from proof of cause.
B(4) Own-enquiry scaffold: I actually encountered [collection difficulty], which affected [procedure or coverage]. I [real response], or would propose [feasible response and responsible person] if it remained unresolved. Distinguish the proposed response from an action already taken.
GUIDANCE: ["Original package: Section A 35 marks (A1+A2), Section B 15, total 50. ### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-P03
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.10-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "case_analysis", "assessment_objectives": ["C", "B", "D"], "blooms_level": "Analyse", "command_word": "State", "mark_tariff": 50}
PROMPT: Fictional school-access enquiry: full list of 20 schools in one district, sample five selected by fair distinct-number draw. The aim is to examine route length and attendance in one week. The five sampled schools record 12 learner journeys, each classified once by its main travel mode during the stated week; this small sub-sample need not represent every learner. Mode counts: walk 6, bus 3, other 3. Group attendance: shorter routes 80%, longer routes 50%; School R on a longer route records 90%, an exception. A pilot repaired an unclear one-way/return instruction. Community book project: 30 donated usable books, six volunteers, council permission available, N$100 record/material cost with N$40 already available. Wet storage threatens books; a school offers a dry room. The project must have fair lending access and records. Section B: use your own completed enquiry and real records. If it is not yet completed, state that and use these as preparation prompts; do not invent fieldwork. Section B answers are fill-in scaffolds for your own real record; they are not completed fictional fieldwork. A fictional demonstration in the notes is a separate teaching example.
A1(1) State an aim and measurable objective for the school enquiry. [2]
A1(2) Describe random selection from the supplied school list. [4]
A1(3) Explain a suitable collection method for attendance and why. [3]
A1(4) Design five interview elements for comparable attendance-access information. [5]
A1(5) Calculate the walking share’s pie angle, showing working. [2]
A1(6) Draw the travel-mode pie chart. [4]
A1(7) Describe the route-attendance pattern with evidence. [4]
A1(8) Identify the exception to a simple longer-route/lower-attendance rule. [1]
A2(1) State two achievable book-project aims. [2]
A2(2) Describe the project organisation. [3]
A2(3) Suggest funding or in-kind support for the stated costs. [2]
A2(4) Explain a matched response to wet storage. [3]
B(1) State your actual question/hypothesis and one objective. [2]
B(2) Explain the fit of your actual presentation method. [3]
B(3) Describe and compare actual findings using evidence. [4]
B(4) Outline conclusions answering your question within limits. [3]
B(5) Suggest a feasible recommendation and implementer. [3]
ANSWER: A1(1) Aim: investigate route length and attendance during the week. Objective: record comparable route categories and attendance percentages for selected schools.
A1(2) Number the 20 schools, use a fair draw or random numbers for five distinct entries, record selected codes and non-response; do not choose the nearest five.
A1(3) Use approved non-identifying school attendance records for the same week because they provide the relevant counts; check definitions and permission, and avoid individual names.
A1(4) Consenting school staff: school code; stated week; eligible learner total; attendance count or rate with its definition; route-length category and how measured. Record sources and missing values, without identifiable learner details.
A1(5) 6 ÷ 12 = 0.5; 0.5 × 360 = 180 degrees.
A1(6) One circle with walking 180°, bus 90°, other 90°, totalling 360°. Title with period and 12 journeys, clear labels/key and accurately measured adjacent sectors.
A1(7) Longer-route group attendance is 50% versus 80% for shorter routes, a 30-point gap. School R’s 90% shows an important exception; mode counts alone do not prove distance caused attendance.
A1(8) School R has a longer route and 90% attendance.
A2(1) Improve fair access to the 30 usable donated books and maintain an accountable lending arrangement.
A2(2) Obtain permission, allocate volunteers to checking, dry storage, lending records and communication, agree fair lending rules and designate ongoing responsibility.
A2(3) Use N$40 and seek N$60 or appropriate record materials in kind; donated books and the offered room cover different needs, so do not count them as cash automatically.
A2(4) The school and coordinator should arrange the offered dry room before accepting books, with safe storage responsibility; more book donations alone leave damage unresolved.
B(1) Own-enquiry scaffold: My actual question or hypothesis was [statement]. One objective was [specific measurable task that served it].
B(2) Own-enquiry scaffold: I used [presentation] for [actual variables and units] because [fit to comparison or pattern]. I included [title, labels, source and relevant scale/key] and avoided [a genuine distortion].
B(3) Own-enquiry scaffold: In my records, [group/date] had [value and unit] compared with [other value and unit]. This supports [relationship], with [exception or uncertainty] retained.
B(4) Own-enquiry scaffold: The evidence supports/challenges [actual question or hypothesis] because [finding]. It applies to [actual scope] and is limited by [real limitation], so I cannot conclude [unsupported wider claim].
B(5) Own-enquiry scaffold: I propose [specific feasible action] in response to [finding]. [Responsible actor] could arrange [resources/implementation], with [cost or access condition] checked.

### ITEM ITEM-6136-10.1-P04
{"objective_ids": ["OBJ-6136-10.1.06-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.10-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.11-00", "OBJ-6136-10.1.12-00"], "subtype": null, "item_type": "source_analysis", "assessment_objectives": ["C", "D", "B"], "blooms_level": "Evaluate", "command_word": "Define", "mark_tariff": 50}
PROMPT: Fictional community-waste enquiry: two safe public paths, east and west, surveyed at the same time on two days. Do not touch waste. A 200 m east-west road has school at west end, water point at midpoint and litter site north of east end; north is upward in the sketch specification. Collection periods show 30 then 18 visible litter items. One initially selected site is unsafe and must not be entered. Hypothesis: “overflow occurs only where bins are absent”; a supplied observation records overflow beside an existing full bin. Service stimulus: east path has 12 scattered items and no bin, west has 6 beside a full bin; a collection team and N$300 are available. Proposal A funds one new bin with no emptying plan; B funds a collection schedule and a suitable targeted bin if costs allow. Pilot clarified the category “other” with examples. Local observation is accessible but covers few places and times. Section B: use your own completed enquiry and real records. If it is not yet completed, state that and use these as preparation prompts; do not invent fieldwork. Section B answers are fill-in scaffolds for your own real record; they are not completed fictional fieldwork. A fictional demonstration in the notes is a separate teaching example.
A1(1) Define a research sample. [1]
A1(2) Describe safe recording of visible waste categories on the stated paths. [4]
A1(3) Name and describe two methods for counts and service information. [4]
A1(4) Explain a selection decision and remaining bias. [3]
A1(5) Suggest a response to the unsafe selected site. [3]
A1(6) Draw a sketch map using the supplied locations. [4]
A1(7) Describe the mapped spatial pattern. [3]
A1(8) Calculate the absolute count change, showing working. [2]
A1(9) Identify evidence challenging the no-bin-only explanation. [1]
A2(1) Describe the second service stimulus. [3]
A2(2) Explain which proposal better fits reliable waste management. [3]
A2(3) Suggest a feasible action, obstacle response and implementer. [4]
B(1) Describe advantages and disadvantages of your local enquiry. [4]
B(2) Explain an actual pilot adjustment. [2]
B(3) Describe findings, implications and a supported conclusion. [6]
B(4) Suggest a response to your actual researcher obstacle. [3]
ANSWER: A1(1) The selected subset of the research population from which data is collected.
A1(2) Define item categories and equal observation lengths/times, record anonymous site codes and visible counts from safe public positions, and mark inaccessible sections as missing rather than zero.
A1(3) Structured observation records visible items consistently; a consenting provider interview records collection schedules and capacity with a neutral schedule. Different needs justify different sources.
A1(4) Selecting both east and west paths covers contrasting service situations, but two accessible paths can omit other neighbourhoods and observation times.
A1(5) Stop that visit, consult the supervising teacher about safe alternatives or suitable existing evidence, and record changed coverage. Do not enter or silently replace it with a convenient site.
A1(6) North up; road west-east; school at west end, water point midway, litter site north of east end. Include distinct symbols/key and title, and state schematic positions rather than an unmeasured accurate scale.
A1(7) The litter site is near the east end and north of the road, away from the school at the west end; the water point is between them. The source supports relative locations, not unmeasured pollution effects.
A1(8) 18 - 30 = -12 items, a reduction of 12 visible items.
A1(9) Overflow is observed beside an existing full bin.
A2(1) East has 12 scattered items without a bin; west has six beside a full bin. Absence and unemptied capacity are different possible service issues; counts alone do not isolate causes.
A2(2) B addresses emptying as well as a targeted missing bin within costs. A adds a bin but leaves collection unresolved, which matters because overflow already occurs beside a full one.
A2(3) The responsible collection team should schedule emptying and assess a suitable targeted bin within N$300, using permission and cost checks. If costs exceed funds, phase the action rather than promise unfunded equipment; students report locations without handling waste.
B(1) Own-enquiry scaffold: A strength of my actual local enquiry was [advantage and why it helped]. A second relevant strength was [different advantage]. Limitations were [two distinct constraints and their effects on evidence], not invented events.
B(2) Own-enquiry scaffold: In my actual pilot, [specific wording/recording problem] occurred. I changed [procedure] and checked [result of retry]. State honestly if this stage was not completed.
B(3) Own-enquiry scaffold: I found [pattern] supported by [figures or observations with units]. This suggests [implication]. In answer to my question, [bounded conclusion], but [exception and limitation] prevent a stronger claim.
B(4) Own-enquiry scaffold: My actual research obstacle was [difficulty obtaining evidence]. A matched response is [safe feasible change] arranged by [responsible actor]. Record whether it was done or remains proposed, and any resulting coverage limit.
